# Alankara-kaustubha

## Prathama-kiraṇaḥ

## Atha kāvyādi-sāmānyoddeśaḥ.

A general description of *kāvya* and so on.

### Text 1

*granthārambhe svābhīṣṭa-devatā-nāma-guṇa-kīrtanātmakaṁ maṅgalam aṅgī-kurvan grantha-kāro granthasya nirvighnāṁ parisamāptim āśāste—*

At the beginning of the book, the author, hoping to complete the book without obstacles, invokes auspiciousness by glorifying the names and qualities of his worshipable deity.1

***svānanda-rasa-satṛṣṇaḥ***  
***kṛṣṇa-caitanya-vigraho jayati***  
***ā-pāmaram api kṛpayā***  
***sudhayā snapayāṁ babhūva bhūmau yaḥ***

**Пословный перевод:**

*sva-ānanda-rasa—*to relish the bliss that comes from worshiping him, or the mellow which gives bliss to his devotees; *satṛṣṇaḥ*—who is eager; *kṛṣṇa-caitanya-vigrahaḥ*—the form of Kṛṣṇa-caitanya; *jayati—*is superexcellent; *ā-pāmaram—*up til the lowest kind of people; *api—*even; *kṛpayā sudhayā*—with the nectar of His mercy; *snapayām babhūva—*bathed; *bhūmau—*on the earth; *yaḥ—*who.

**Перевод:**

**All glories to Śrī-kṛṣṇa-caitanya, who is eager to relish the bliss of worshiping Himself.2 He has showered everyone on the earth, including even the lowest of people, with the nectar of His mercy.**

### Text 2

***sa jayati yena prabhavati***  
***dṛśi su-dṛśāṁ vyañjanā vṛttiḥ***  
***atiśayita-pada-padārtho***  
***dhvanir iva muralī-dhvanir murārāteḥ***

**Пословный перевод:**

*jayatir atrākarmakaḥ sarvotkarṣa-vacanas tena namaskāro ’pi vyajyate, “svāpakarṣa-bodhānukūla-vyāpāra-viśeṣo namaskāraḥ” iti nyāyāt. sarvotkarṣaṁ darśayati—padaṁ vaikuṇṭhādi-sthānam, padārtho vastu-bhūto brahmānandas tābhyām apy atiśayī—tatra tatrāpi durlabha ity arthaḥ. tatra bījam āha—yenety-ādi. yena hetu-bhūtena su-dṛśāṁ gopāṅganānāṁ dṛśi netre vyañjanā vigatāñjanā vṛttiḥ prabhavati, ānandāśru-dhautatvāt.*

The verb *jayati* is *akarmaka* here and express superexcellence. Thus it implies *namaskāra,* in accordance with the maxim *svāpakarṣa-bodhānukūla-vyāpāra-viśeṣo namaskāraḥ,* “the particular activity favorable for expressing one’s subordination is called *namaskāra*.” He demonstrates the superexcellence: *Pada* means the abodes of the Lord such as Vaikuṇṭha, and *padārtha* means the substance that is the bliss of Brahman. The sound of Murāri’s flute is superior even to them. The idea is that it is rarely attained even in Vaikuṇṭha and Brahman. He explains the reason for this with the words beginning with *yena. Yena* expresses the cause, *su-dṛśām* refers to the cowherd women, *dṛśi* means “in the eyes,” and *vyañjanā* means “where the ointment is gone.” The ointment on the eyes of the *gopīs* disappears because it is washed away by their tears of joy.

**\[First interpretation\]**

**Пословный перевод:**

*saḥ—*that; *jayati—*is superexcellent; *yena—*because of which; *prabhavati—*occurs; *dṛśi—*on the eyes; *su-dṛśām—*of the beautiful-eyed girls, i.e the *gopīs; vi-añjanā—*where the ointment is gone; *vṛttiḥ—*a condition; *atiśayita—*which is more glorious; *pada-padārthaḥ—*than the place and the substance; *dhvaniḥ—*implied meaning, or primeval, spiritual sound (see the upcoming explanations); *iva—*like; *muralī-dhvaniḥ—*the sound of the flute; *mura-ārāteḥ—*of the enemy of the Mura demon.

**Перевод:**

**All glories to the superexcellent sound of Murāri’s flute, which transcends the Vaikuṇṭha planets and the bliss of Brahman. It is like** ***dhvani,*** **and it causes the ointment on the eyes of the** ***gopīs*** **to become washed away.**

*granthasyābhidheyam apy upamālaṅkāreṇa darśayati—dhvanir iveti. dhvanir uttama-kāvya-tattvaṁ vyaṅgya-bhūtaṁ yat kim api. sa kīdṛśaḥ? atiśayitau pada-padārthau yena, pada-padārthātirikta ity arthaḥ. suṣṭhu paśyantīti su-dṛśa ālaṅkārikās teṣāṁ dṛśi jñāne yena vyañjanā-vṛttiḥ prabhavati prabhur bhavati.*

With the words *dhvanir iva* he also reveals the subject-matter of the book through the use of the poetical ornament called simile. *Dhvani* refers to implied meaning, the very essence of high-grade poetry. What is *dhvani* like? It is beyond words and their meanings, i.e it is distinct from them. The word *su-dṛśām* then refers to those who see well, i.e the knowers of *alaṅkāra-śāstra, dṛśi* means *jñāne*3, and *prabhavati* means “is able.” So *dhvani* is that by means of which the knowers of *alaṅkāra-śāstra* are able to understand *vyañjanā-vṛtti* (“a word’s power of suggestion”).MAd1

**\[Second interpretation\]4**

**Пословный перевод:**

*saḥ—*that; *jayati—*is superexcellent; *yena—*by means of which; *prabhavati—*is able; *dṛśi—*to be known; *su-dṛśām—*by those who know well, i.e the *ālaṅkarikas; vyañjanā-vṛttiḥ—*a word’s power of suggestion; *atiśayita—*are surpassed; *pada-padārthaḥ—*by which words and their meanings; *dhvaniḥ—*implied meaning; *iva—*like; *muralī-dhvaniḥ—*the sound of the flute; *mura-ārāteḥ—*of the enemy of the Mura demon.

**Перевод:**

**All glories to the superexcellent sound of Murāri’s flute, which is just like implied meaning*****.*** **Implied meaning is beyond words and their meanings, and by means of it the knowers of** ***alaṅkāra-śāstra*** **are able to understand a word’s power of suggestion.**

### Text 3

*kiṁ ca, dhvanir nāda-brahma, tad uktam (śaṅkarācāryasya prapañca-sāra-tantre 3.43)—*

***mūlādhārāt prathamam udito yas tu tāraḥ parākhyaḥ***  
***paścāt paśyanty atha hṛdaya-go buddhi-yuṅ madhyamākhyaḥ***  
***vaktre vaikhary atha rurudiṣor asya jantoḥ suṣumṇā-***  
***baddhas tasmād bhavati pavana-prerito varṇa-saṅghaḥ***

Moreover, *dhvani* can also refer to *nāda-brahma* (“primeval, spiritual sound”). This is explained as follows in Śaṅkarācārya’s *Prapañca-sāra-tantra* (3.43): “Sound, when it first arises from the *mūlādhāra-cakra,* is called *parā.* Then, when it reachs the mind, it is called *paśyantī.* After that, when it contacts the intellect, it is called *madhyamā.* Finally, when it appears in the mouth, it is called *vaikharī. Nāda* can be perceived in the *suṣumṇā* nerve \[the central nerve which extends from the base of the spine to the top of the head and in which the six *cakras* are situated\]. After it has reached the *vaikharī* stage it is pushed out of the body by air and manifest as phonemes.”5

*tasyāpi sarvotkarṣa-śālitvaṁ, tata eva vedādi-sarva-siddheḥ. tat-pakṣe, ati atiśayena śayitau suptau pada-padārthau yasmin, paramparayā varṇa-padādīnāṁ sarveṣāṁ dhvanir eva bījam iti bhāvaḥ. vyajyate ’nayā sarvam iti vyañjanā māyā, yuṭ-pratyaya-siddheḥ, tasyā vṛttiḥ prapañco yena prabhavatīti. su-dṛśāṁ jñānināṁ jñāne, śeṣobhaya-pakṣa-svīkāraḥ kāvyopayogitvāt.*

*Nāda-brahma* is also superexcellent because everything including the Vedas is produced from it alone. In the case that *dhvani* refers to *nāda-brahma,* then *atiśayita-pada-padārthaḥ* means “that in which words and meanings rest.” The idea is that *dhvani* alone is indirectly the cause of all phonemes, words, and so on. *Vyañjanā* refers to *māyā,* because *vyañjanā* is made with the *pratyaya yu\[ṭ\]* (*\[ṭ\]ana*) in the sense of *vyajyate ’nayā sarvam,* “that by which everything is made manifest.” *Vṛttiḥ* means the manifestation. *Vyañjanā-vṛttiḥ* therefore means the manifestation of *māyā,* i.e the material world. *Sudṛśāṁ* refers to the *jñānīs* and *dṛśi* means *jñāne* as before. So primeval, spiritual sound is that by means of which the *jñānīs* are able to understand the manifestation of *māyā.*

**\[Third interpretation\]**

*saḥ—*that; *jayati—*is superexcellent; *yena—*by means of which; *prabhavati—*is able; *dṛśi—*to be known; *su-dṛśām—*by those who know well, i.e the *jñānīs; vyañjanā-vṛttiḥ—*the manifestation of *māyā,* i.e the material world; *atiśayita—*rest; *pada-padārthaḥ—*in which words and their meanings; *dhvaniḥ—*primeval, spiritual sound; *iva—*like; *muralī-dhvaniḥ—*the sound of the flute; *mura-ārāteḥ—*of the enemy of the Mura demon.

**All glories to the superexcellent sound of Murāri’s flute, which is just like primeval, spiritual sound*****.*** **Primeval, spiritual sound is the source of words and their meanings, and by means of it the** ***jñānīs*** **are able to understand the manifestation of** ***māyā.***

The last two interpretations are accepted because they are suitable for *kāvya.*6

### Text 4

***prakārāntareṇoktam arthaṁ stauti—***

He praises the subject matter under discussion \[i.e the superexcellence of *dhvani*\] in another way:

***gokula-lalanāmodī***  
***nānā-vidha eva sa khalu bhāvānām***  
***śābalya-praśamodaya-***  
***sandhi-su-gandhiś camatkārī***

*atrāpi traya eva pakṣāḥ. sa iti muralī-dhvaniḥ, kāvya-dhvanir nādaś ca. ādye bhāvānāṁ vyabhicāri-prabhṛtīnām, dvitīye ’pi teṣām eva, tṛtīye bhūtānām. ādye gokula-lalanā gopāṅganāḥ, dvitīye gor vācaḥ kulaṁ varṇādis tasya lalanam īpsā, tṛtīye gaur vāg-devatā, saiva kula-lalanā. śābalyaṁ miśrī-bhāvaḥ, praśamo nāśaḥ, udayaḥ sṛṣṭiḥ, sandhiḥ sandhānam, pakṣa-traye ’pi taulyam.*

In this verse also there are three interpretations. *Saḥ* can refer to the sound of the flute, to poetical implied meaning, or to primeval, spiritual sound. In the first and second interpretations *bhāvānām* refers to the *vyabhicāri-bhāvas* and so on but in the third interpretation it refers to the living beings. In the first interpretation *gokula-lalanā* refers to the cowherd girls. In the second interpretation *gokula* refers to the elements of speech such as phonemes (i.e *gor kulam* = *vācaḥ varṇādiḥ*) and *lalana* means “desire to obtain.” In the third interpretation *go* refers to the goddess of speech, Sarasvatī, who is a *kula-lalanā* (“respectable woman”). *Śābalya* means “mixture ,” *praśama* means “destruction,” *udaya* means “creation,” and *sandhi* means “combination.” The meanings are the same in all three interpretations.

**\[The three interpretations\]**

*gokula-lalanā—*the cowherd girls, or because of which there is a desire to obtain, i.e pronounce, the *varṇas* and so on, or the goddess of speech, Sarasvatī; *āmodī—*which delights; *nānā-vidhaḥ—*of various kinds; *eva—*indeed; *saḥ—*the sound of the flute, or poetical implied meaning, or primeval, spiritual sound; *khalu—*indeed; *bhāvānām—*of the *vyabhicāri* and other kinds of *bhāvas,* or of the living beings; *śābalya—*with the mixture; *praśama—*disappearance, destruction; *udaya—*appearance, creation; *sandhi—*and combination; *su-gandhiḥ—*which is fragrant; *camatkārī—*which astonishes.

**The sound of Murāri’s flute is variegated indeed. It delights the** ***gopīs,*** **and it is astonishing for it is fragrant with the mixture, disappearance, appearance, and combination of the** ***bhāvas*** **headed by the** ***vyabhicāri-bhāvas.*****7**

**Implied meaning is variegated indeed. It makes one want to pronounce the** ***varṇas*** **and so on, it is delighful, and it is astonishing for it is fragrant with mixture, disappearance, appearance, and combination of the** ***bhāvas*** **headed by the** ***vyabhicāri-bhāvas.***

**Primeval, spiritual sound is variegated indeed. It delights Sarasvatī, and it is astonishing for it is fragrant with the mixture, destruction, creation, and combination of the living beings.**

### Text 5

***asmin granthe śabdārthayoḥ prādhānyena tayoś ca varṇa-mūlatvena***  
***varṇānāṁ ca dhvani-mūlatvena dhvaner nāda-brahmaṇa uddeśaḥ kṛtaḥ***

*asmin granthe—*in this book; *śabda-arthayoḥ—*of words and meanings; *prādhānyena—*because of the prominence; *tayoḥ—*of them; *ca—*and; *varṇa-mūlatvena—*because of being based on phonemes; *varṇānām—*of the phonemes; *ca—*and; *dhvani-mūlatvena—*because of being based on *dhvani; dhvaneḥ—*of *dhvani; nāda-brahmaṇaḥ—*which is *nāda-brahman; uddeśaḥ—*the mention; *kṛtaḥ—*has been done.

**The** ***nāda-brahman, dhvani,*** **has been mentioned because words and meanings are prominent in this book, and words and meanings are based on phonemes, and phonemes are based on** ***dhvani.*****8**

*atra dhvaneḥ kāvya-prāṇatvaṁ darśayiṣyan kāvyasya śarīrādi-svarūpam āha—*

Now, in order to show that *dhvani* is the life breath of *kāvya,* he describes what constitutes the body and so on of *kāvya:*

*śarīraṁ śabdārthau dhvanir asava ātmā kila raso*  
*guṇā mādhuryādyā upamiti-mukho ’laṅkṛti-gaṇaḥ*  
*su-saṁsthānaṁ rītiḥ sa kila paramaḥ kāvya-puruṣo*  
*yad asmin doṣaḥ syāc chravaṇa-kaṭutādiḥ sa na paraḥ*

*śarīram—*the body; *śabda-arthau—*words and meanings; *dhvaniḥ—dhvani* (“implied meaning”); *asavaḥ—*the life airs; *ātmā—*the soul; *kila—*indeed; *rasaḥ—rasa; guṇāḥ—*the qualities; *mādhurya-ādyāḥ—*sweetness and so on; *upamiti-mukhaḥ—*headed by simile; *alaṅkṛti-gaṇaḥ—*the ornaments; *su-saṁsthānam—*nice formation; *rītiḥ—*style; *saḥ—*that; *kila—*indeed; *paramaḥ—*endowed with all good features (*Viśvanātha* says *paramaḥ=sal-lakṣaṇa-yuktaḥ*); *kāvya-puruṣaḥ—*the *kāvya* man; *yat—*if; *asmin—*in the personality of poetry; *doṣaḥ—*fault, deformity; *syāt—*there is; *śravaṇa-kaṭutā-ādiḥ*—sounding harsh to the ears (*śruti-kaṭu*) and so on; *saḥ—*that well known; *na—*not; *paraḥ—*any other.

**Words and their meanings are the body of** ***kāvya, dhvani*** **its life-breath,** ***rasa*** **its soul, sweetness and so on its qualities, simile and so on its ornaments, and style its build. In this way the** ***kāvya-puruṣa*** **is endowed with all good features. If there are deformities in the** ***kāvya-puruṣa,*** **then harshness and so on are those deformities, not other minor faults.**

*yadi doṣo bhavet tadā śravaṇa-kaṭutādiḥ prasiddhaḥ sphuṭa-doṣa eva doṣaḥ, na tu paraḥ kṣudrataraḥ, rasānapakarṣatvāt. so ’pi yadi bhavati, ato nirdoṣa evāsau bhavitum arhatīty arthaḥ.*

If there are deformities, then the well known obvious faults such as harshness to the ears are the deformities, not other minor faults, for a minor fault doesn’t detract from the *rasa.* And that is if there are deformities. Therefore the idea is that the *kāvya-puruṣa* should actually be free from fault.9

*uddeśo lakṣaṇaṁ parīkṣā ceti granthasya trayo vyavahārās tatrādau śabdādīnām anenaiva ślokenoddeśaḥ kṛtaḥ. lakṣaṇa-parīkṣe kartavye.*

*Uddeśa* (“simply mentioning something by name”), *lakṣaṇa* (“defining that thing”), and *parīkṣā* (“testing the definition to see if it holds up”) are the three functions of a book. Out of them, first the *uddeśa* of *śabda* and so on10 has been done by this verse. Now the *lakṣaṇa* and the *parīkṣā* will be done:

### Text 6

Now what is that *kāvya* whose body and so on was just described, on account of it’s being a person? In answer to this question he gives a definition of *kāvya:*

***kavi-vāṅ-nirmitiḥ kāvyam****2)*

*kavi—*of a poet; *vāk—*of the words; *nirmitiḥ—*the composition; *kāvyam—*poetry.

**A poet’s wonderful composition of words is called** ***kāvya.***

*kavi-vāg ity ukte kavi-vāṅ-mātrasyaiva kāvyatvāpattiḥ, kavi-nirmitir ity ukte kavi-kṛta-śilpāntarasyāpi, vāṅ-nirmitir ity ukte vyākhyātṛ-viśeṣasaya ca yasya kasyāpi vyākhyā-kauśalasyāpi. asādhāraṇa-camatkāra-kāriṇī racanā hi nirmitiḥ. tena rasāpakarṣaka-doṣa-rahitaṁ yathā-sambhava-guṇālaṅkāraṁ rasātmakaṁ śabdārtha-yugalaṁ kāvyam iti lakṣaṇasya sva-rasaḥ.*

If we just said *kavi-vāk* (“the words of a poet”), then anything a poet says would be *kāvya.* If we just said *kavi-nirmitiḥ* (“the creation of a poet”), then a poet’s other artistic creations \[such as paintings\] would also be *kāvya.* If we just said *vāṅ-nirmitiḥ* (“a composition of words”), then any clever composition of words by a certain author \[other than a poet\] would also be *kāvya.*

The word *nirmitiḥ* refers only to a composition that creates an extraordinary feeling of wonder. Due to this understanding the following definition is self-evident: “A combination of words and meanings that are full of *rasa,* endowed with suitable qualities and ornaments, and free from faults that detract from the *rasa* is called *kāvya.*”11

### Text 7-8

*tena (kāvya-prakāśasya prathamollāse) “tad adoṣau śabdārthau sa-guṇāv analaṅkṛtaḥ punaḥ kvāpi” iti lakṣaṇaṁ “kuraṅga-nayanā” ity-ādāv api paryāptaṁ bhavati, sa-guṇālaṅkāra-nirdoṣa-śabdārthatvāt. “vākyaṁ rasātmakaṁ kāvyam” (sāhitya-darpaṇe 1.3) iti ca lakṣaṇaṁ “gopībhiḥ saha viharati hariḥ” ity-ādau ca paryāptaṁ syāt, rasātmaka-vākyatvāt. vyatirekeṇa doṣaḥ, yad vākyaṁ na bhavati, tat kāvyaṁ na bhavatīty āyāteḥ (yoga-vāśiṣṭha-rāmāyaṇe utpattiḥ 4.20, śaraṇadeva-kṛta durghaṭa-vṛttau 2.28).*

***kūrma-loma-paṭa-cchannaḥ śaśa-śṛṅga-dhanur-dharaḥ***  
***eṣa bandhyā-suto bhāti kha-puṣpa-kṛta-śekharaḥ***

*ity asya vākyatvābhāve ’pi kāvyatva-darśanāt. yas tu “rītir ātmā kāvyasya” (vāmana-kṛta-kāvyālaṅkāre) iti paṭhati, na tad api sādhīyaḥ, rīter bāhya-guṇatvāt, yat tu “lokottara-camatkāra-varṇanā-nipuṇaḥ kavis tasya tādṛg vacaḥ kāvyam” (kāvya-prakāśe 1.2) ity api na sādhu, anyonyāśraya-doṣa-prasakteḥ, tathā hi—lokottara-camatkāra-varṇanā-nipuṇaḥ kaviḥ, kaver lokottara-camatkāra-varṇanā-nipuṇā vāk kāvyam iti parasparāśrayaḥ.*

*tat sādhūktam—“kavi-vāṅ-nirmitiḥ kāvyam” iti, kavir iti pāribhāṣikīyaṁ saṁjñeti parasparāśraya doṣo ’pi nirastaḥ.*

*atha vā, kāvyatvaṁ nāma gotvādi-vaj jātir eva yathā sāsnādy-avayavavatīṣu go-vyaktiṣu praty-ekam ayaṁ gaur ayaṁ gaur ity anugatākārā yenāsādhāraṇa-dharmeṇāvagatiḥ, sa eva jāti-lakṣaṇaḥ ko ’py asādhāraṇa-dharmo gotvam, tathā śabdārtha-saṅghātasya sahṛdaya-hṛdayāsvādyaḥ ko ’pi kāvyatva-lakṣaṇo dharma-viśeṣaḥ kāvyatvaṁ jātiḥ.*

Therefore the definition “*Kāvya* is word and sense free from faults, possessed of qualities, and without ornaments in some places.12” (*Kāvya-prakāśa, ullāsa* 1) suffers from the fault of *ativyāpti* because, according to this definition, even a single meaningful word like *kuraṅga-nayanā* (“a lady with eyes like those of a doe”) would have to be considered *kāvya* since it is possessed of qualities and ornaments and free from fault.

The definition “*Kāvya* is a *vākya*13 whose soul is *rasa.*” (*Sāhitya-darpaṇa* 1.3) also suffers from the fault of *ativyāpti* because, according to this definition, even a *vākya* like *gopībhiḥ saha viharati hariḥ* (“Hari enjoys pastimes with the *gopīs*”) would have to be considered *kāvya* since it is a *vākya* whose soul is *rasa.* This definition is also faulty because by negative logic it implies that what is not a *vākya* is not *kāvya,* whereas in actuality a sentence like “This son of a barren woman appears beautiful, being covered with cloth made of turtle hair, holding a bow made of rabbit’s horns, and wearing a wreath made of flowers from the sky.” is considered *kāvya* even though it is not a *vākya.*14

The person who says “Style is the soul of poetry.” (see Vāmana’s *Kāvyālaṅkāra*) is also wrong, because style is an external quality.15 And the definition “One who is expert in description that invokes an extraordinary feeling of wonder is called a *kavi,* and such words of his are called *kāvya.*” (*Kāvya-prakāśa* 1.2) is also not good because it results in the logical fallacy of mutual dependence. For example, there is circular reasoning when we say that one who is expert in description that invokes extraordinary astonishment is called a *kavi* and that a *kavi’s* words that are expert in description that invokes an extraordinary feeling of wonder are called *kāvya.*

Therefore the definition *kavi-vāṅ-nirmitiḥ kāvyam,* “A poet’s wonderful composition of words is called *kāvya.*” (*Alaṅkāra-kaustubha* 1.6) is a good definition. The word *kavi* here is taken as a technical term and thus the fault of circular reasoning is refuted.

Alternately, *Kāvyatva* is a *jāti* like *gotva* (“cow-ness”) and so on. Just as *gotva* is a particular special characteristic, i.e *jāti,* by which one can recognize the sameness of form that “this is a cow and this is a cow” in every individual cow that possesses a dewlap and so on, so *kāvyatva* is a particular special characteristic, i.e *jāti,* that inheres in words and meanings. It is relished by the connoisseurs in their hearts.

### Text 9

***…nipuṇaṁ kavi-karma tat***

*nipuṇam—*expert; *kavi-karma—*activity of a poet; *tat—*that (*kāvya*).

***Kāvya*** **is the expert activity of a poet.**

*atha kāvyaṁ kavi-karmeti kavi-jijñāsāyāṁ tat-svarūpam āha—*

Now that the curiosity to know who is a *kavi* has arisen because of the statement “*Kāvya* is the activity of a *kavi,*” he will describe the characteristics of a *kavi:* 16

***sa-bījo hi kavir jñeyaḥ sa sarvāgama-kovidaḥ***  
***sa-rasaḥ pratibhā-śālī yadi syād uttamas tadā***

*sa-bījaḥ—*one who has the seed; *hi—*only; *kaviḥ—*a poet; *jñeyaḥ—*is to be considered; *saḥ—*he; *sarva-āgama-kovidaḥ—*expert in all kinds of texts; *sa-rasaḥ—*full of *rasa; pratibhā—*creative intelligence; *śālī—*having; *yadi—*if; *syāt—*is; *uttamaḥ—*first class; *tadā—*then.

**A poet is someone who has the seed \[i.e the innate genius\]. And if he is expert in all the scriptures \[dealing with weapons, botany, etc.\], able to relish** ***rasa,*** **and has creative intelligence, then he is a first class poet.**

*etena dvaye kavayaḥ sambhavanti, arocakinaḥ sa-ṭṛṇābhyavahāriṇaś ceti vāmanaḥ (kāvyālaṅkāra-sūtre 1.2.1). tatra sa-tṛṇābhyavahāriṇaḥ kavaya eva na bhavanti, anādṛtatvāt. arocakina eva kavayaḥ. tena hi “sa-bījaḥ” ity eva kavi-lakṣaṇam, anyāni tu viśeṣaṇāni, sa-bīja-kavir īdṛśaḥ syād ity arthaḥ. kiṁ tat bījaṁ yena sa-bīja iti jñeyaḥ kavir ity āha—*

Thus, according to Vāmana (*Kāvyālaṅkāra-sūtra* 1.2.1), there are two kinds of poets: those who have no taste for vulgar things and those who eat whatever they get, even if it has grass in it.17 In that regard, the last kind \[i.e the indiscriminate poets\] are not really poets, because they are not respected as such. Only the first kind \[i.e the discriminating poets\] are actually considered poets. Therefore the word *sa-bījaḥ* alone forms the definition of a poet. The other words \[i.e *sarvāgama-kovidaḥ, sa-rasaḥ,* and *pratibhā-śālī*\] are just adjectives. The idea is “a poet who has the seed should be like this…” Now he will describe just what is that seed because of which a poet is known as having the seed.

### Text 10-11

***bījaṁ prāktana-saṁskāra-viśeṣaḥ kāvya-roha-bhūḥ***

*bījam—*the seed; *prāktana-saṁskāra-viśeṣaḥ—*a certain previous impression; *kāvya-roha—*from which *kāvya* grows; *bhūḥ—*the place.

**The** ***bīja*** **is a certain previous** ***saṁskāra*** **from which** ***kāvya*** **grows.**

*rohaś ca dvedhā—nirmātṛ-mūlaḥ, svādaka-mūlaś ca, yaṁ vinā nirmātuṁ svādayituṁ ca na śakyate. tenotpatty-āsvādayor evāsya kāraṇatā.*

The growth is of two kinds: growth rooted in the composer and growth rooted in the relisher. Because it is impossible to compose and relish *kāvya* without this previous *saṁskāra,* the *saṁskāra* is taken as the cause of the composition and relishment of *kāvya.*18

*kāsau pratibhety āha—*

Now he answers the question “What is *pratibhā*?”

*prajñā nava-navollekha-śālinī pratibhā matā (Bhāmahālaṅkāre)*

*prajñā—*intelligence; *nava-nava—*in various new ways; *ullekha—*at describing an object; *śālinī—*adept; *pratibhā—pratibhā; matā—*is considered;

**Intelligence that is adept at describing an object in various new ways is called** ***pratibhā.*** **(Bhāmaha’s** ***Alaṅkāra-śāstra*****)**

### Text 12

***athokta-lakṣaṇaṁ kāvyaṁ kiyat-prakārakaṁ bhavatīty ākāṅkṣāyāṁ tad-bhedān āha—***

Now, in answer to the anticipated question “You have defined *kāvya,* but how many kinds of *kāvya* are there?”, he will describe the different kinds of *kāvya.*

***uttamaṁ dhvani-vaiśiṣṭye madhyame tatra madhyamam***  
***avaraṁ tatra niṣpanda iti tri-vidham āditaḥ***

*uttamam—*excellent; *dhvani-vaiśiṣṭye—*when there is excellence of *dhvani* (“implied meaning”); *madhyame—*is mediocre; *tatra—*when that (i.e the *dhvani*); *madhyamam—*is mediocre; *avaram—*inferior; *tatra—*when that (i.e the *dhvani*); *niṣpandaḥ—*is lifeless; *iti—*thus; *tri-vidham—*of three kinds; *āditaḥ—*initially.

***Kāvya*** **is excellent when the** ***dhvani*** **is excellent, mediocre when the** ***dhvani*** **is mediocre, and inferior when the** ***dhvani*** **is lifeless.19 Thus initially there are three kinds of** ***kāvya.***

*vyaṅgyam eva dhvaniḥ. yat tu “idam uttamam atiśāyini vyaṅgye vācyād dhvanir budhaiḥ kathitaḥ” iti kāvyasyaiva dhvanitvam, tat tu dhvani-sambandhād dhvanir iti lakṣaṇā, kiṁ vā dhvanyate ’neneti karaṇa-sādhanena. vastutas tu dhvanyata idam iti karma-sādhanam eva.*

*Dhvani* is simply that which is implied.20 The definition “Excellent *kāvya* in which the implied meaning is more excellent than the expressed meaning is called *dhvani* by the learned.” (*Kāvya-prakāśa*) says that *kāvya* itself is *dhvani.* But this definition only makes sense by *lakṣaṇā vṛtti* (“a word’s indirect, secondary meaning”), the idea being that *kāvya* is called *dhvani* because *kāvya* has a relationship with *dhvani.*21 Alternatively, this definition makes sense if we take the word *dhvani* as expressing the insturment (*karaṇa*), i.e *kāvya* is that by which the meaning is implied. Factually, however, the word *dhvani,* in its literal sense, only expresses the object (*karma*), i.e that which is implied.

### Text 13

***punaś ca—***

And in addition to that:

***dhvaner dhvany-antarodgāre tad eva hy uttamottamam***  
***śabdārthayoś ca vaicitrye dve yātaḥ pūrva-pūrvatām***

*dhvaneḥ—*of the implied meaning; *dhvani-antara—*to another implied meaning; *udgāre—*if there is a giving rise; *tat—*that *kāvya; eva hi—*only; *uttama-uttamam—*superexcellent; *śabda-arthayoḥ—*of the sound and meaning; *ca—*and; *vaicitrye—*if there is the quality of creating a feeling of wonder; *dve—*the two; *yātaḥ—*attain; *pūrva-pūrvatām—*the state of being the previous ones.

***Kāvya*** **is superexcellent when one implied meaning gives rise to another implied meaning or when the sound and meaning create a feeling of wonder.22 Similarly, when the sound and meaning create a feeling of wonder, the other two (*****madhyama*** **and** ***avara*****) attain the state of the previous two (*****uttama*** **and** ***madhyama*****).**

*yadi dhvani-vaiśiṣṭye dhvany-antara-vaiśiṣṭyaṁ syāt, yadi vā śabdārthayor vaicitryaṁ ca bhavati, tadā kāvyam uttamottamam. evaṁ śabdārtha-vaicitrye sati dve madhyamāvare pūrva-pūrvatāṁ yātaḥ, madhyamam uttamaṁ bhavati, avaraṁ madhyamaṁ bhavatīty arthaḥ. śabdārthayor iti kākākṣi-golaka-nyānenobhayatra yojanīyam.*

If in the excellence of one *dhvani* there is excellence of another *dhvani,* or if the sound and meaning create a feeling of wonder, then *kāvya* is superexcellent. Similarly, if the sound and meaning create a feeling of wonder, the two categories, i.e *madhyama* and *avara,* attain the state of the previous two categories, i.e *madhyama* becomes *uttama* and *avara* becomes *madhyama*. The words *śabdārthayor vaicitrye* are syntactically connected to both parts of the verse, in accordance with the logic of the the crow’s eye.23

### Text 14

***krameṇodāharaṇāni—***

Examples, according to the order above, are:

***gaurīm arcayituṁ prasūna-vicaye śvaśrū-nirMAd2diṣṭā hareḥ***  
***krīḍā-kānanam āgatā vayam aho meghāgamaś cābhavat***  
***preṅkholāḥ paritaś ca kaṇṭaka-latāḥ śyāmāś ca sarva-diśo***  
***no vidmaḥ prativeśa-vāsini guroḥ kiṁ bhāvi sambhāvitam***

*gaurīm—*Gaurī (Durgā); *arcayitum—*to worship; *prasūna—*flowers; *vicaye*—to collect; *śvaśrū—*by our mother-in-laws; *nirdiṣṭā—*being ordered; *hareḥ—*of Hari; *krīḍā-kānanam—*to the pleasure-grove; *āgatāḥ*—have come; *vayam—*we; *aho*—oh; *megha—*of the rainclouds; *āgamaḥ*—coming; *ca—*and; *abhavat*—has happened; *preṅkholāḥ—*are swinging to and fro; *paritaḥ—*all around; *ca—*and; *kaṇṭaka-latāḥ—*thorny vines; *śyāmāḥ*—dark; *ca*—and; *sarva-diśaḥ—*all the directions; *no—*don’t; *vidmaḥ*—we know; *prativeśa-vāsini—*O neighbor; *guroḥ—*by my elders; *kim—*what; *bhāvi—*will; *sambhāvitam—*be thought.

**My mother-in-law ordered me to collect flowers for the worship of Gaurī, and thus I have come to the pleasure-grove of Hari. But alas the rainclouds have come, and thus the directions have become dark and the thorny vines are blowing about in the wind. My dear neighbor, I do not know what my elders will think.24**

*atra vācyād bhāvi-śrī-kṛṣna-saṅgama-janya-nakha-kṣata-sthagana-rūpasya vyaṅgyasya vaiśiṣṭyād uttamatvam.*

This is an example of excellent *kāvya* because here the implied meaning in the form of the concealment of the nail marks produced from the upcoming meeting with Kṛṣṇa is more excellent than the expressed meaning.

### Text 15

***madhyamaṁ yathā—***

An example of mediocre *kāvya* is:

***uttamasya puruṣasya vanāntaḥ***  
***satyam āli kusumāya gatāsīḥ***  
***āyayur madhukarās tava paścād***  
***duḥśakaḥ parimalo hi varītum***

*uttamasya puruṣasya—*of the excellent *punnāga* trees, or of the Supreme Person; *vana—*the forest; *antaḥ—*within; *satyam—*truly; *āli—*O friend; *kusumāya—*for collecting flowers; *gatā āsīḥ—*you have gone; *āyayuḥ—*have come; *madhukarāḥ—*the bees; *tava—*you; *paścāt—*behind; *duḥśakaḥ—*is difficult; *parimalaḥ—*the fragrance; *hi—*certainly; *varītum—*to hide.

**My dear friend, truly you have come within the forest of excellent** ***punnāga*** **trees. The bees are following behind you. Certainly it is difficult to hide the scent of the** ***punnāga*** **trees.**

**My dear friend, truly you have come within the forest of the Supreme Person, Kṛṣṇa. The bees are following behind you. Certainly it is difficult to hide Kṛṣṇa’s scent \[which is situated on your body\].**

*atra “punnāge puruṣas tuṅgaḥ kesaraḥ” ity-ādinottamasya puruṣasya prakṛṣṭa-punnāgasya. pakṣe, puruṣottamasya śrī-kṛṣṇasyaiveti vyaṅgyam eva sphuṭam.*

In this verse the word *puruṣa* refers to the Punnāga tree in accordance with the definition *punnāge puruṣas tuṅgaḥ kesaraḥ deva-vallabhaḥ,* “Puruṣa, Tuṅga, Kesara, and Deva-vallabha are all names of the Punnāga tree” (*Amara-koṣa* 2.4.25). Thus the words *uttamasya puruṣasya* mean “of the excellent *punnāga* trees.” In the other case they mean “of the Supreme Person, Kṛṣṇa.” Thus the implied meaning is obvious.25

### Text 16

***avaraṁ yathā—***

An example of inferior *kāvya* is:

***ūrjat-sphūrjair garjanair vāri-vāhāḥ***  
***prodyad-vidyud-dāma-vidyotitāśāḥ***  
***adrāv adrau vidrutā drāghayante***  
***danti-bhrāntyā siṁha-saṅgha-prakopān***

*ūrjat—*powerful; *sphūrjaiḥ—*in which there is thunder; *garjanaiḥ—*with the roars; *vāri-vāhāḥ—*the rainclouds; *prodyat—*which are manifest in an excellent way; *vidyud-dāma—*by the flashes of lightning; *vidyotita—*brightened; *āśāḥ—*by whom the directions; *adrau adrau—*from mountain to mountain; *vidrutāḥ—*running; *drāghayante—*increase; *danti—*for elephants; *bhrāntyā—*because of mistaking; *siṁha-saṅgha—*of the lions; *prakopān—*the anger.

**While running from mountain to mountain and brightening the directions with their flashes of lightning, the rainclouds, with theirs load roars of thunder, increase the anger of the lions, who think that they are elephants.26**

*atra kevalaṁ śabda-vaicitryād dhvaner niṣpanda-bhāvāc cāvaratvam.*

Here the implied meaning is inferior because only the sound \[and not the meaning\] creates a feeling of wonder27 and the implied meaning is lifeless.MAd3

### Text 17

***dhvaner dhvany-antarodgāre uttamottamatvaṁ yathā—***

An example of superexcellent *kāvya* where one implied meaning gives rise to another implied meaning is:

***yātāsi svayam eva ratna-padakasyānveṣaṇārtham vanād***  
***āyātāsi cireṇa komala-tanuḥ kliṣṭāsi hā mat-kṛte***  
***śvāso dīrghataraḥ sa-kaṇṭaka-padaṁ vakṣo mukhaṁ nīrasaṁ***  
***kā te hrīr asamañjasā sakhi gatir dūre rahaḥ su-bhruvām***

*yātā—*went; *asi*—you; *svayam eva*—personally; *ratna-padakasya*—jewelled necklace; *anveṣaṇa—*finding; *artham*—for the sake of; *vanāt—*from the forest; *āyātā—*have come; *asi—*you; *cireṇa—*after a long time; *komala—*is tender; *tanuḥ—*whose body; *kliṣṭa—*have suffered pain; *asi—*you; *hā—*alas; *mat-kṛte—*for my sake; *śvāsaḥ*—breath; *dīrghataraḥ—*drawn out; *sa-kaṇṭaka-padam—*with the marks of thorns; *vakṣaḥ—*chest; *mukham—*mouth; *nīrasam—*dry; *kā—*why; *te—*your; *hrīḥ*—embarassment; *asamañjasā—*improper; *sakhi—*O friend; *gatiḥ*—going; *dūre*—so far away; *rahaḥ*—into the secluded part \[of the forest\]; *su-bhruvām*—for girls.

**You personally went to find my jewelled necklace. Now you have returned from the forest after a long time. I know your body is tender and you have suffered much pain for my sake. Just see, your breath is drawn out, your chest has the marks of thorns on it, and your mouth has dried up. Why are you feeling embarassed? It is not proper for girls to go so far away by themselves into the secluded part of the forest.28**

*ity atra tvaṁ tad-ānayanārthaṁ na gatāsi, api tūpabhogārtham eveti dhvanir ekaḥ. anyo ’pi vaktṛ-prakṛti-prakaraṇa-vaiśiṣṭyāt pratibhāsate. tathā hi prakaraṇaṁ tāvat priya-sakhīm enāṁ śrī-kṛṣṇena saha saṅgamayituṁ tenaiva saha śrī-rādhayā prāg eva yuktiḥ kṛtā, yadāsau mayā prahīyate, tadāsyāḥ saṅgas tvayā karaṇīya iti. paścāt tayā samāgatāyāṁ tasyāṁ tām aparāddhāṁ kartuṁ tasyāḥ parihāsāvahitthāsūyādi-bhāva-śābalyam, sakhyāś ca hrī-sādhvasa-kopādi-bhāva-śābalyam iti bahava eva dhvaneḥ pallavāḥ. tena dhvaner dhvany-antarodgāro ’ta evaṁ-vidha-sthale uttamottamatvaṁ jñeyam.*

Here one implied meaning is “You didn’t go with the purpose of bringing back my necklace. Rather you went with the purpose of enjoying with Kṛṣṇa.”29MAd4

Another implied meaning also arises because of the special context in the form of the nature of the speaker. Rādhā \[the speaker of this verse\] previously made an arrangement with Kṛṣṇa to make her dear friend meet with Kṛṣṇa. The arrangement was “When I send her, then you should unite with her.” Later, when Rādhā met with that friend, she experienced a mixture of *bhāvas* such as joking, concealment, and jealousy in order to make that friend feel guilty, and that friend experienced a mixture of *bhāvas* such as embarassment, fear, and anger. In this way there are many offshoots of the implied meaning. Thus the implied meaning gives rises to another implied meaning, and thus *kāvya* such as this is considered superexcellent.30

### Text 18

***śabdārtha-vaicitryeṇottamottamatvaṁ yathā—***

An example of superexcellent *kāvya* where the sound and meaning create a feeling of wonder is:

***nava-jaladhara-dhāmā koṭi-kāmāvatāraḥ***  
***praṇaya-rasa-yaśoraḥ śrī-yaśodā-kiśoraḥ***  
***aruṇad aruṇa-dMAd5īrghāpāṅga-bhaṅgyā kuraṅgīr***  
***iva nikhila-kṛśāṅgī raṅgiṇi tvaṁ kva yāsi***

*nava-jaladhara—*like that of a fresh raincloud; *dhāmā—*whose complexion; *koṭi—*of millions; *kāma—*of cupids; *avatāraḥ—*the incarnation; *praṇaya-rasa—*in the form of the mellow of conjugal love; *yaśaḥ-raḥ*—who bestows glory; *śrī-yaśodā—*of mother Yaśodā; *kiśoraḥ—*the young boy; *aruṇat—*has caught; *aruṇa—*reddish; *dīrgha—*broad; *apāṅga—*of the sidelong glances; *bhaṅgyā—*with the play; *kuraṅgīḥ—*female deer; *iva—*like; *nikhila—*all; *kṛśa-aṅgīḥ—*the thin-bodied girls; *raṅgiṇi—*O passionate girl; *tvam—*you; *kva—*where; *yāsi—*are going.

**Yaśodā’s young boy, whose complexion is like that of a fresh raincloud, who is the incarnation of millions of cupids31, and who distributes the glorious mellow of conjugal love, has caught all the doe-like slender girls with the net of his reddish sidelong glances. O passionate girl, where are you going?**

*atra śabdārtha-vaicitryeṇa vācyād atiśāyinā dhvaninā ca uttamottamatvam. dhvanis tu—he raṅgiṇi, kutukini, tvam ati-prasiddhā guṇavatī. kva yāsi? tatraiva yāhi, yatra śrī-yaśodā-kiśoro nikhila-kṛśāṅgīr aruṇat rurodha. kayā? aruṇa-dīrghāpāṅga-bhaṅgyā. kuraṅgīr ivety upamālaṅkāreṇāpāṅga-bhaṅgyā vāgurātvena rūpakālaṅkāro dhvanitaḥ. vastutas tu kva yāsīti tatra kiṁ yāsi, mā yāhīti lakṣyo ’rthaḥ, koṭi-kāmāvatāra iti pralobhana-dvārā tatraiva yāhīti vyaṅgyo ’rthaḥ. atrāviśvāsaṁ ca mā kārṣīḥ, yataḥ praṇaya-rasa-yaśoraḥ praṇaya-rasa-yaśaḥ-pradaḥ. nava-jaladhara-dhāmeti—sva-dhāmnaiva sarvatas timiram utpādya niḥśaṅkam alakṣyo bhūtvā viharati, ato loka-bhītir api na kāryeti bahava dhvaneḥ pallavāḥ.*

This is superexcellent *kāvya* because the sound and meaning creates a feeling of wonder32 and the implied meaning is more excellent than the expressed meaning (see 1.12). The implied meaning is as follows: O eager girl (*raṅgini=kutukini*), you are reputable and possess good-qualities. Where are you going? You should go to the place where Yaśodā’s young boy has caught (*aruṇat=rurodha*) all the slender girls. With what has he caught them? With the play of his reddish sidelong glances. The ornament of metaphor (*rūpaka*) is implied because the play of the sidelong glances is a net on account of the ornament of simile (*upamā*), i.e doe-like.

Actually, the indirect meaning (*lakṣya=*by *lakṣaṇā vṛtti*) of the phrase “Where are you going?” is “Why are you going there? Don’t go there.” But the implied meaning that comes (by *vyañjanā-vṛtti*) by means of the enticing phrase “who is the incarnation of millions of cupids” is “You should go there.” “And don’t be doubtful that he is the incarnation of millions of cupids, because He distributes the glorious mellow of conjugal love (*yaśo-raḥ=yaśaḥ-pradaḥ*).” Furthermore, the phrase *nava-jaladhara-dhāmā* implies this: “Making all the directions dark simply by his own complexion, he becomes invisible and enjoys free from fear. Therefore you need not be afraid of what people will say.” In this way there are many offshoots of the implied meaning.

### Text 19

***śabdārtha-vaicitrye madhyamasyottamatvaṁ yathā—***

An example of how mediocre *kāvya* becomes excellent when the sound and meaning create a feeling of wonder is:

***śikṣitāni suhṛdāṁ na gṛhītāny***  
***ukṣitāsi nija-garva-rasena***  
***dīkṣitaḥ kula-vadhū-vadha-yāge***  
***vīkṣitaḥ sakhi sa nanda-kumāraḥ***

*śikṣitāni—*the instructions; *suhṛdām—*of the friends; *na—*not; *gṛhītāni—*accepted; *ukṣitā—*have been sprinkled; *asi—*you; *nija-garva—*of your own pride; *rasena—*with the water; *dīkṣitaḥ—*initiated; *kula-vadhū—*respectable girls; *vadha—*of killing; *yāge—*into the sacrifice; *vīkṣitaḥ—*has been seen; *sakhi—*O friend; *saḥ—*that; *nanda-kumāraḥ—*son of Nanda.

**Sprinkled with the water of your own pride, you didn’t heed the advice of your friends. O friend, now it’s too late. You have seen the son of Nanda, who has taken initiation in the sacrifice of killing respectable girls.33**

*atra dhvaner madhyamatve ’pi śabdārtha-vaicitrya-paripuṣṭyā uttamatvam eva.*

Even though the implied meaning is mediocre, this is considered excellent *kāvya* because it is nourished by sound and meaning that creates a feeling of wonder.34

### Text 20

***śabdārtha-vaicitrye ’varasya madhyamatvaṁ yathā—***

An example of how inferior *kāvya* becomes mediocre when the sound and meaning create a feeling of wonder is:

***kānanaṁ jayati yatra sadā sat***  
***kā na nandati yad etya sukha-śrīḥ***  
***kā na nanda-tanaye praṇayotkā***  
***kānanaṁ dhayati vā na hi tasya***

*kānanam—*forest; *jayati—*is superexcellent; *yatra—*in whom; *sadā—*always; *sat—*transcendental; *kā*—which; *na*—does not; *nandati—*increase; *yat—*which forest; *etya—*after attaining; *sukha-śrīḥ—*opulence of happiness; *kā—*which girl; *na*—not; *nanda-tanaye—*the son of Nanda; *praṇaya—*to love; *utkā—*eager; *kā—*which girl; *ānanam—*face; *dhayati—*drink; *vā—*or; *na hi—*does not; *tasya—*His.

**Which girl is not eager to love the son of Nanda? And which girl does not drink his face? The transcendental forest of Vṛndāvana always exists splendidly within HimMAd6. Which happiness does not increase upon attaining Vṛndāvana?**

*atra dhvaner niṣpandatāyām avaratve ’pi madhyamatvam.*

Even though the implied meaning is lifeless and therefore inferior35, this is considered mediocre *kāvya* because it is nourished by sound and meaning that creates a feeling of wonder.36

### Text 21-22

***yaśaḥ-prabhṛty eva phalaṁ nāsya kevalam iṣyate***  
***nirmāṇa-kāle śrī-kṛṣṇa-guṇa-lāvaṇya-keliṣu***  
***cittasyābhiniveśena sāndrānanda-layas tu yaḥ***  
***sa eva paramo lābhaḥ svādakānāṁ tathaiva saḥ***

*yaśaḥ—*fame; *prabhṛti—*and so on; *eva—*alone; *phalam—*gain; *na—*not; *asya—*of composing *kāvya; kevalam—*only; *iṣyate—*is; *nirmāṇa-kāle—*at the time of composing; *śrī-kṛṣṇa-guṇa-lāvaṇya-keliṣu—*on Kṛṣṇa’s qualities, beauty, and pastimes; *cittasya—*of the mind; *abhiniveśena—*by fixation; *sāndra-ānanda—*in dense bliss; *layaḥ—*absorption; *tu—*but; *yaḥ—*which; *saḥ—*that; *eva—*alone; *paramaḥ—*highest; *lābhaḥ—*gain; *svādakānām—*for the relishers; *tathā eva—*in the same way; *saḥ—*that.

**Fame and so on are not the only gains of composing** ***kāvya.*** **The highest gain both for the composer and for the relishers is the absorption in deep spiritual bliss that comes from fixing the mind on Kṛṣṇa’s qualities, beauty, and pastimes.**

*“kāvyaṁ yaśase ’rtha-kṛte” (kāvya-prakāśa 1.2) ity-ādīny eva kevalaṁ na phalāni, api tūkta-prakāraḥ śrī-kṛṣṇa-guṇānuvādādi-kṛta ānandaś ca.*

The gains described in the verse beginning “*Kāvya* is for the sake of gaining fame, which in turn produces wealth …” (*Kāvya-prakāśa* 1.2) are not the only gains to be had.37 On the contrary, the above-mentioned bliss created from glorifying Kṛṣṇa qualities and so on is the main gain.

*iti śrīmad-alaṅkāra-kaustubhe kāvyādi-sāmānyoddeśo nāma prathamaḥ kiraṇaḥ.*

Thus ends the first ray of *Alaṅkāra-kaustabha,* entitled “A general description of *kāvya* and so on.”

## Dvitīya-kiraṇaḥ

## Atha śabdārtha-vṛtti-traya-nirūpaṇam.

A discussion on sound, meaning, and designative potency.

### Text 1

***atha kāvya-puruṣasya śarīratvena nirdiṣṭayoḥ śabdārthayoḥ śabde nirūpite evārtha-nirūpaṇam iti prathamataḥ śabda eva nirūpyate.***

Sound and meaning were mentioned as the body of the *kāvya-puruṣa* (see 1.5). Out of sound and meaning, sound will be discussed first, since the meaning is ascertained only once the sound is ascertained.

***ākāśasya guṇaḥ śabdo varṇa-dhvany-ātmako dvidhā***

**Sound is the quality of ether. There are two kinds of sound: sound made up of phonemes \[i.e articulate sound\] and sound made up of noises \[i.e inarticulate sound\].MAd7**

*varṇātmako dhvany-ātmakaś ceti dvidhā. yady api varṇā nityās tathāpi tad-abhivyaktiḥ śarīra-stha-vāyunaiva bhavati. ata uktam—(śaṅkarācārya-kṛta-pañca-sāra-tantre 3.43) “tasmāt pavana-prerito varṇa-saṅghaḥ” iti.*

The compound *varṇa-dhvany-ātmakaḥ* means *varṇātmako dhvany-ātmakaś ca* (“sound made up of phonemes and sound made up of noises”). In this way there are two kinds of sound. Even though the phonemes are eternal, they become manifest by means of the air situated in the body. Therefore it is said “After it has reached the *vaikharī* stage it is pushed out of the body by air and manifest as phonemes” (see 1.3).

### Text 2-3

***nityatva-prakāśaś ca yathā—***

And the explanation of the eternality \[of the phonemes\] is as follows:

***sac-cid-ānanda-vibhavāt sakalāt parameśvarāt***  
***āsīc chaktis tato nādas tasmād bindu-samudbhavaḥ***  
***nādo binduś ca bījaṁ ca sa eva tri-vidho mataḥ***  
***bhidyamānāt parād bindor ubhayātmā ravo ’bhavat***  
***sa ravaḥ śruti-sampannaḥ śabda-brahmābhavat param***

**First the spiritual potency became manifest from the embodied Supreme Lord, whose essence is eternity, knowledge, and bliss. Then from the spiritual potency came primeval, spiritual sound. Then from primeval, spiritual sound came the syllable** ***oṁ.*** **In this way there are three kinds of sound: primeval, spiritual sound,** ***oṁkāra,*** **and** ***oṁkāra*** **as the seed of all the phonemes.**

**From the Supreme Lord in His separated form as primeval, spiritual sound came** ***oṁkāra,*** **and from** ***oṁkāra*** **came twofold sound \[i.e sound made up of phonemes and sound made up of noises\]. That sound, which is perceived in the ears, became the transcendental** ***śabda-brahman*** **\[i.e the Vedas\].**

*sakalād iti mūrtāt, nādo ghoṣaḥ, tathā ca (bhāg. 11.12.17) “prāṇena ghoṣeṇa guhāṁ praviṣṭaḥ” iti binduḥ praṇavaḥ, sa ca bījam, sarva-varṇa-prabhavatvāt.*

The word *sakalāt* here means *mūrtāt* (“embodied”), and the word *nāda* is synonymous with the word *ghoṣa* mentioned in *prāṇena ghoṣeṇa guhāṁ praviṣṭaḥ* (*Bhāgavatam* 11.12.17).38 *Bindu* refers to the syllable *oṁ,* and that same *bindu* is called *bīja,* because it is the source of all the phonemes.

### Text 4

***tathā ca (bhāg. 12.6.37)***

***samāhitātmano brahman brahmaṇaḥ parameṣṭhinaḥ***  
***hṛdy ākāśād abhūn nādo vṛtti-rodhād vibhāvyate***

*(bhāg. 12.6.39) “tato ’bhūt tri-vṛd oṁ-kāro yo ’vyakta-prabhavaḥ svarāṭ” ity ārabhya “tato ’kṣara-samāmnāyam asṛjad bhagavān ajaḥ” iti bhāgavate (12.6.43).*

The same thing is also described in *Bhāgavatam* 12.6.37 and in *Bhāgavatam* 12.6.39-43: “O *brāhmaṇa,* first the subtle vibration of transcendental sound \[i.e *nāda*\] appeared from the sky of the heart of the most elevated Lord Brahmā, whose mind was perfectly fixed in spiritual realization. One can perceive this subtle vibration when one stops all external hearing. From that transcendental subtle vibration arose the *oṁkāra* composed of three sounds. The *oṁkāra* has unseen potencies and manifests automatically within a purified heart. It is the representation of the Absolute Truth in all three of His phases—the Supreme Personality, the Supreme Soul and the supreme impersonal truth. This *oṁkāra,* ultimately nonmaterial and imperceptible, is heard by the Supersoul without His possessing material ears or any other material senses. The entire expanse of Vedic sound is elaborated from *oṁkāra,* which appears from the soul, within the sky of the heart. It is the direct designation of the self-originating Absolute Truth, the Supersoul, and is the secret essence and eternal seed of all Vedic hymns. *Oṁkāra* exhibited the three original sounds of the alphabet—*A, U* and *M.* These three, O most eminent descendant of Bhṛgu, sustain all the different threefold aspects of material existence, including the three modes of nature, the names of the *Ṛg, Yajur* and *Sāma* Vedas, the goals known as the Bhūr, Bhuvar and Svar planetary systems, and the three functional platforms called waking consciousness, sleep and deep sleep. From that *oṁkāra* Lord Brahmā created all the sounds of the alphabet.”

*tena nādasya nityatvāt tad-ātmakasyoṁ-kārasya ca nityatvam, svarāḍ iti pūrvokteḥ. tad-ātmakasya varṇa-samūhasya ca tathā. ākāśasya nitya-dravyatve tad-guṇasyāpi nityatvam, guṇāśrayo hi dravyam iti guṇa-guṇinoḥ samavāya-sambandhāt. tena pavana-preraṇāpreraṇa-vaśād evābhivyakty-anabhivyaktī. vastutas tu nityataiva teṣām ity ayam āntara-sphoṭaḥ. uktaṁ ca (bhāg. 12.6.40) “śṛṇoti ya imaṁ sphoṭam” iti dvādaśa-skandhe. ata evaike ācāryā śabdārtham āntaraṁ sphoṭaṁ śabda-brahmety āhuḥ. yathā (puṇyarāja-kṛta-vākyapadīya-prakāśe 1.48) “niraṁśa evābhinno nityo bodha-svabhāvaḥ śabdārtha-maya āntaraḥ sphoṭaḥ” iti.*

Therefore since primeval, spiritual sound is eternal, *oṁkāra,* which is made up of primeval sound, is also eternal, as confirmed by the previous statement *svarāṭ* (*Bhāgavatam* 12.6.39). Similarly, the phonemes, which are based on *oṁkāra,* are also eternal. Since ether is eternal, its quality, sound, is also eternal, because there is a relationship of perpetual co-inherence between the quality and the possessor of the quality, since a quality resides in a substance.

Therefore the manifestation and non-manifestation of the phonemes is dependent upon their being pushed out or not pushed out of the body by air. Factually, however, the phonemes are eternal. Therefore the eternal phoneme is an internal sound (*sphoṭa*)*.*39 The term *sphoṭa* is also seen in the phrase *śṛṇoti ya imaṁ sphoṭam* “\[The Supreme Lord is He\] who hears this eternal, subtle sound \[i.e *oṁkāra*\]” (*Bhāgavatam* 12.6.40). Therefore some *ācāryas* say that a sound’s meaning, perceived in the mind, is called internal *sphoṭa,* and that internal *sphoṭa* is called *śabda-brahma* (the Absolute Truth in the form of sound). For example, Puṇyarāja in *Vākyapadīya-prakāśa* (1.48) says “Internal *sphoṭa* is without parts, indivisible, and eternal. Its inherent nature is knowledge and it is comprised of a sound’s meaning.”

### Text 5

***prayogaś ca (yoga-vāśiṣṭha-rāmāyaṇe, nirvāṇa 2.42)—***

The evidence of internal *sphoṭa* is given in *Yoga-vāśiṣṭha-rāmāyaṇa, nirvāṇa-kāṇḍa* 2.42:

***jātāndha-mūka-badhirasyāntaḥ svīye parāmṛśi***  
***sva-vāk-śabdārthayor bodha āntaraḥ sphoṭa eva saḥ***

**“A person who is blind, deaf, and dumb by birth undertands his speech and its meaning upon reflecting upon them in his mind. This is internal** ***sphoṭa.*****”**

*vaiyākaraṇās tu bahiḥ sphoṭam āhuḥ. tatra pūrva-pūrva-vaṛnoccāraṇābhivyakta-tat-tat-saṁskāra-sahakṛta-carama-varṇa-saṁskāra-niṣṭha-pada-janyaika-padārtha-pratyāyakatā pada-sphoṭaḥ. evaṁ pūrva-pūrva-padoccāraṇābhivyakta-tat-tat-saṁskāra-sahakṛta-carama-pada-saṁskāra-niṣṭha-vākya-janyaika-vākyārtha-pratyāyakatā vākya-sphoṭaḥ. etad-ubhaya-lakṣaṇaṁ śabda-brahma. tathā ca (puṇyarāja-kṛta-vākyapadīya-prakāśe 2.29) “eka eva nityaḥ padābhivyaṅgo ’khaṇḍo vyakti-sphoṭo jāti-sphoṭo bahī-rūpaḥ” ity āhuḥ. vyakti-sphoṭa-puraskāreṇa jāti-sphoṭaḥ. ata āhuḥ “budhaiḥ vaiyākaraṇaiḥ” iti kāvya-prakāśa-kṛtaḥ (1.4). tam anye na sahante.*

The grammarians, however, talk about external *sphoṭa.* In that regard, *pada-sphoṭa* is that which causes one to understand a word’s meaning, a word’s meaning being produced from a word, and a word being based on the impression left by the last phoneme along with the impressions left by the previous phonemes, such impressions being manifested by pronunciation of the phonemes.40 Similarly, *vākya-sphoṭa* is that which causes one to understand a sentence’s meaning, a sentence’s meaning being produced from a sentence, and a sentence being based on the impression left by the last word along with the impressions left by the previous words, such impressions being manifested by pronunciation of the words. These two kinds of *sphoṭa* are *śabda-brahman.* Moreover, Puṇyarāja in *Vākyapadīya-prakāśa* (2.29) says “The external form of *sphoṭa* is one without a second, eternal, and indivisible. It is manifested by words and appears in two forms: first *vyakti-sphoṭa* and then *jāti-sphoṭa* (i.e *vyakti-sphoṭo jāti-sphoṭaḥ* means *vyakti-sphoṭa-puraskāreṇa jāti-sphoṭaḥ*).” Therefore, \[because the grammarians’ opinion is the best in this regard\] Mammaṭa, the author of *Kāvya-prakāśa,* says “by the intelligent grammarians” (*Kāvya-prakāśa* 1.4). \[Thus Mammaṭa accepts *sphoṭa-vāda*\]. Others, however, cannot tolerate *sphoṭa-vāda.*41

*tathā hi pūrva-pūrva-varṇānubhavataś carama-varṇa-śravaṇa-kāle pūrva-pūrva-varṇānubhava-janita-saṁskāra-sahakṛta-carama-varṇa-sambandhena pada-vyutpādana-samaya-grahaṇānugṛhītena śrotreṇa yugapad eva sad-asad-aneka-varṇāvagāhino pada-janya-pratītir janyate, sahakāri-dārḍhyena pratyabhijñāna-vad eva. pratyābhijñāne pratyakṣe ’pi atītādi-pūrvāvasthā sphuraty eva, tena kṛtaṁ sphoṭeneti, tan na. tathā hi pada-vyutpādana-samayo hi sphoṭa-kṛta eva. na ca tatra pramāṇābhāvaḥ, pratyakṣārthāpattyoḥ sambhavāt. yathā “ayaṁ gauḥ” ity ukte na hi ga-kārau-kāra-visargā eva pratīyante, api tu sāsnādimat kim apīti pratyakṣam. ayaṁ gaur ity atra kiṁ ga-kārādayo varṇā vyastā evārtha-pratyāyakāḥ, kiṁ samastāḥ? nādyaḥ, itara-varṇa-vaiyarthyān nāpi dvitīyaḥ, utpanna-pradhvastānāṁ sāmastyābhāvāt. (sarva-darśana-saṅgrahe pāṇini-nidarśane 27-saṅkhyānucchede) “na ca vyāsa-samāsābhyām anyaḥ prakāro ’sti tasmād varṇānāṁ vācakatvānupapattau yad-balād artha-pratītiḥ, sa eva sphoṭo varṇātirikto varṇābhivyaṅgyārtha-pratyāyako nitya eva” ity arthāpattir api. sphoṭānaṅgī-kāre sarve eva saṁskārā viśṛṅkhalāḥ santaḥ pada-janya-padārtha-pratītau na śaknuvanti, nāpi pratyabhijñāne pratyakṣe ’py atītāpi pūrvāvasthā sphurati. na vā sad-asad-aneka-varṇāvagāhinī pada-pratītiḥ śrotreṇa janyate, “so ’yaṁ devadattaḥ” iti pratyabhijñānasya tattvāṁśe saṁskāra-janita-smṛti-viśeṣatvāt śrotreṇa sad-asad-aneka-varṇāvagāhābhāvāc ca. na hi pratyabhijñānam ekaṁ jñānam, tad-aṁśe saṁskāra-janyatayā, idam-aṁśe cakṣur-janyatayā ca smaraṇa-grahaṇātmakatvāt. na ca tad-aṁśe ’pi cakṣuḥ karaṇam, tasya sannihita-grāhitayā tad-abhāvāt. nāpīdam-aṁśe saṁskāraḥ—cakṣur-anvaya-vyatirekānuvidhānāt, smaraṇa-hetu-vyavasāyābhāvāc ca. tena sphoṭa eva aṅgī-kārya iti vaiyākaraṇāḥ, tena pṛthak-sambandhānāṁ saṁskārāṇāṁ krameṇa paraspara-sambandha-kāritvaṁ sphoṭatvam. anyathā rasaḥ saraḥ nadī dīna ity eteṣāṁ pratiloma-pāṭhe ’pi repha-sakārādīnāṁ saṁskāro ’ sti, na tu te ’nuloma-saṁskāravatvena padaṁ vyutpādayanti, anyathā bhedo na syāt.*

\[OMISSION — Various arguments against *sphoṭa-vāda*\]

### Text 6

***sādhv-asādhutayādyo ’pi***

**Furthermore, the first kind of sound \[i.e a word\] is of two kinds: pure and impure.**

*ādyo varṇātmakaḥ śabdo ’pi sādhutayāsādhutayā ca dvedhā bhavati. kiṁ nāma sādhutvaṁ, kiṁ sādhu-janoditatvam, kim uta sūnṛtatvam, utāho veda-vākya-sthatvam, prakṛti-pratyaya-vyutpāditatvaṁ vā? ādye caṇḍālādi-prayuktasya saṁskṛtasyāpy asādhutvāpatteḥ, dvitīye paruṣa-saṁskṛtasya, tṛtīye ’smad-ādi-kṛta-saṁskṛta-śabdānām apy asādhutvāpātāt, caturthe saṁjñā-śabdānām api, tena vyākaraṇa-praṇītatvaṁ sādhutvam iti. tathā sati ḍitthādīnāṁ saṁjñā-śabdānām api ḍittho ’yaṁ ḍavittho ’yam iti vibhakty-utpatteḥ sādhutvam, na tu gāvī-prabhṛtīnāṁ bhrānta-praṇītatvāt. tathāpi tair yad-vyavahāra-niṣpattis tad-vijñānāṁ tat-smārita-go-śabdādi-dvārā, ajñānāṁ tv ajña-paramparā-prāpta-saṁskāra-dvāraiva. prākṛtasya tu sādhu-samatvāt sādhūdbhavatve tad-tad-vyākaraṇa-praṇītatatvāc ca sādhutvam.*

The first kind, i.e sound made up of phonemes (see 2.1), is also of two kinds on account of being pure and impure. What actually constitutes a pure word? Is it a word that is spoken by pure people? Or is it a word that is pleasant? Is it a word that it found in the *Vedas*? Or is it a word that is made up of bases and suffixes? If we accept the first definition, then even a Sanskrit word would be impure if it were spoken by an impure person like a *caṇḍāla.* If we accept the second definition, then a harsh Sanskrit word would be impure. If we accept the third definition, then even Sanskrit words composed by persons such as us would be impure. And if we accept the fourth definition, then even proper names that cannot be derived would be impure. Therefore a pure word is that which is composed in accordance with the rules of grammar. If we accept this definition then even proper names such as *ḍittha* (name of a man) are considered pure since they are formed by applying a noun ending, i.e *ḍittho ’yam* “this is *ḍittha*” and *ḍavittho ’yam* “this is *ḍavittha.*” On the other hand, words such a *gāvī* (cow) are considered impure because they are composed in an incorrect way. Still, learned persons can understand what the word *gāvī* refers to because it reminds them of the correct word *go* (cow). Ignorant persons, however, understand what the word *gāvī* refers to because of the impression they have gained from a chain of ignorant persons. Prakrit words, on the other hand, are considered pure, because they are similar to Sanskrit \[i.e *tat-sama*\] and because they are composed in accordance with the rules of Prakrit grammar, provided they are pronounced properly.

### Text 7

***…sādhavaś ca catur-vidhāḥ***  
***jāti-kriyā-guṇa-dravyaiḥ***

**Furthermore, pure words are of four kinds, depending on whether they refer to a category, action, quality, or individual object.**

*“gauḥ pācakaḥ śuklo ḍitthaḥ” iti kramāj jāty-ādibhiś cātur-vidhyam. ca-kārāj jātir eva padārtha iti ca matam. tathā hi guḍa-taṇḍulādi-pāka-bhedena pācako ’yaṁ pācako ’yam iti pācakatvam asti. evaṁ candra-candana-kundādiṣu ayaṁ śuklo ’yaṁ śukla iti śuklatvam. bāla-vṛddha-yuvādy-udīrita-ḍitthādy-artheṣu ḍittho ’yaṁ ḍittho ’yam iti ḍitthatvam iti.*

Pure words are of four kinds, taking into account category and so on which are expressed sequentially in the words *gauḥ* (cow), *pācakaḥ* (cook), *śuklaḥ* (white), and *ḍitthaḥ* (a man named Ḍittha). Because of the word *ca* in this verse, another opinion shines forth, namely that category alone is what is referred to by a word.42 For example, in this opinion the word *pācakaḥ* actually refers to the common quality of being a cook, i.e because of the special action of cooking sugar, rice, and so on, we say “this is a cook” and “this is a cook.” Similarly, the word *śuklaḥ* refers to whiteness, i.e in reference to the moon, sandal paste, jasmine, and so on we say “this is white” and “this is white.” Likewise the word *ḍitthaḥ* refers to Ḍittha-ness, i.e in reference to individual objects such as Ḍittha as a boy, Ḍittha as an old man , and Ḍittha as a youth, and so on we say “this is Ḍittha” and “this is Ḍittha.”

### Text 8-9

***…mukhyo lākṣaṇikas tathā***  
***vyañjakaś ceti te tredhā***

**Words are further divided into three categories:** ***mukhya*** **\[literal\],** ***lākṣaṇika*** **\[figurative\], and** ***vyañjaka*** **\[suggestive\].**

*te śabdāḥ. mukhyo vācakaḥ, yas tu saṅketam aiśvaraṁ dhatte, sa mukhyaḥ. aiśvaram īśvara-kṛtaṁ saṅketam, asmad-ādi-kṛtaṁ saṅketam api yo dhatte, tasya dravyatve svārthaṁ prati mukhyatā.*

In this verse *te* means *śabdāḥ* and *mukhyaḥ* means *vācakaḥ,* i.e a word that expresses the meaning assigned to it by the Supreme Lord is called a *mukhya* word. *Aiśvaram* means *īśvara-kṛtam.* Even a word that expresses a meaning assigned by persons like us \[i.e a word like *ḍittha*\], is still considered a *mukhya* word \[by extension\], since it expresses its own meaning in the form of a particular individual object.

***…saṅketa īśecchā tatra tattva-kṛt***

**Assignation is the desire of the Supreme Lord that assigns a meaning to a word.**

*tatra ghaṭādau tattva-kṛd ghaṭāditva-bodha-kṛt saṅketaḥ syāt. tathā hi kaścid agṛhīta-saṅketo bālaḥ kadācid uttama-vṛddhena ghaṭam ānayety ukte madhyama-vṛddhe kambu-grīvādimantaṁ vyakti-viśeṣaṁ sthānāt sthānāntaraṁ nayati sati aho ghaṭa-śabdenedṛśī vyaktir iti prathamaṁ vyaktāv eva ghaṭa-śabda-śaktir iti pratipadyate. punas tenaiva paṭam ānayety apy ukte tad-vijātīyaṁ vyakti-viśeṣaṁ tathaiva tasmin nayati sati punaḥ so ’pi ghaṭād bhinnaḥ paṭaḥ, paṭād bhinno ghaṭa iti vyutpādyamānaḥ punar aghaṭa-bhinno ghaṭa apaṭa-bhinnaḥ paṭaś cety apoha-dvāreṇa niścinvan punas tenaiva ghaṭāntaraṁ paṭāntaraṁ cānayety ukte tasmin ghaṭa-paṭayor ākārato visadṛśau ghaṭa-paṭāv ānayati sati punar ayaṁ saṁśete—“aho paṭa-ghaṭa-śabdau na vyakti-viśeṣa-viṣayaka-saṅketau, yata etau pūrva-ghaṭa-paṭato bhinnau, tena ghaṭādiṣu kaścid asādhāraṇo bhaviṣyati dharmaḥ, yad-vaśād ayaṁ ghaṭo ’yam api ghaṭa ity anugatākārāvagāhi-jñānaṁ janyate” iti niścitya jātāv eva saṅketam avadhārayati. tad-bhinna-bhinnādhikaraṇa-mātra-vṛttitvam asādhāraṇatvam, yathā goḥ sāsnādi-mattvam.*

I.e Assignation is that which assigns an undertanding such as *ghaṭatva* (pot-ness) to a word such as *ghaṭa* (pot). For example, a certain boy who doesn’t know any assigned meanings at one point in time sees that when the old man says *ghaṭam ānaya* “bring a pot,” the middle aged man brings from one place to another a certain individual object that has a neck on it. At that time the boys thinks “Oh! The word *ghaṭa* refers to this individual object.” Thus initially he thinks that the word *ghaṭa* refers to a single individual object. Later he sees that when the old man say *paṭam ānaya* “bring a chaddar,” the middle aged man brings a certain individual object that is different than the pot. At that time the boy realizes “The chaddar is different than the pot, and the pot is different than the chaddar.” He then ascertains through negative logic that the pot is different than something that is not a pot and the cloth is different than something that is not a cloth.” Later, he sees that when the old man says *ghaṭāntaraṁ paṭāntaraṁ cānaya* “Bring another pot and another chaddar,” the middle aged man brings a pot and a chaddar that are different in shape than the pot and chaddar he previously saw. He then feels doubt: “Oh! the words *ghaṭa* and *paṭa* do not conventionally refer to particular individual objects, because this pot and chaddar are different than the previous pot and chaddar.” Finally he decides: “Therefore there must be some special characteristic in pots and so on under the influence of which a kind of understanding is produced whereby one recognizes something that has a similar shape, i.e this is a pot and this is also a pot.” Thus he ascertains that actually, by assignation, a word actually refers just to a category. A special characteristic is that which exists only in a similar object and not in a different object. MAd8For example, the special characteristic of a cow is its having a dewlap and so on.

### Text 10

***yoga-rūḍhāś ca rūḍhāś ca yaugikāś ceti te tridhā***

**Words are also divided into another set of three categories:** ***yoga-rūḍha*** **\[words that have both a derived meaning and a conventional meaning\]*****, rūḍha*** **\[words that have a conventional meaning\]*****,*** **and** ***yaugika*** **\[words that have a derived meaning\]*****.***

*te śabdāḥ punas tri-vidhā bhavanti. yoga-rūḍhāḥ paṅkajādayaḥ. paṅkāj janer ḍa-pratyayena paṅka-jani-kartr-abhidhāyakena yogenāpi padmārtha eva pratipādyate, na tu kumudādy-arthaḥ, iti yogārtha-puraskāreṇāpi rūḍhy-artha eveti yoga-rūḍha eva, īśvara-kṛta-saṅketa-mahimnā jhaṭiti padmasyaiva smṛteḥ. yadā tu paṅka-jaṁ vartma-daurgatyam ity-ādi kenāpy ucyate, tadā tatra paṅka-kṛtatvena lakṣaṇā, vastutas tu padme śaktiḥ. rūḍhā maṇḍapādayaḥ. yathā padme paṅka-jani-kartṛtvaṁ vartate, tathā maṇḍape viśeṣe maṇḍa-pāna-kartṛtvaṁ nāsti, tena kevalaṁ gṛha-viśeṣo yogārthaṁ vināpi gamyata iti rūḍha eva. karmaṇi kuśala ity atra kuśala-śabdo na lākṣaṇikas tasya nānārthatvāt, “kuśalaḥ kṣeme puṇye ca śikṣite” iti śikṣito nipuṇaḥ, ato mukhya eva. evaṁ maṇḍapa-śabdo rūḍhatvena gṛha-viśeṣe mukhyaḥ. yadā tu maṇḍapaṁ bhojayatety uktis tadā śabdāntara-sāhacaryān maṇḍa-pāna-kartari lākṣaṇikaḥ. āditeyādi-śabdā yaugikāḥ. aditer apatyāni ḍhak-pratyayena kevalaṁ yogārtha eva. yadā tu apatyārtha-pratyayāntareṇa ṇya-pratyayena niruktis tadā nānārthatve ’py āditya iti deva-paryāye paṭhito ’pi deva-viśeṣe śaktimattvāt pratyaya-māhimnā yoga-rūḍha evārthaḥ.*

*Te tridhā* means “words are of a further three kinds.” Examples of *yoga-rūḍha* words are *paṅkaja* (lotus) and so on. The word *paṅkaja* is formed when the suffix *\[ḍ\]a* (*a\[c\]*) is applied after the verbal root *jan* (to take birth) which comes after the word *paṅka* (mud). This suffix expresses the *kartā* of the action of taking birth from the mud. Thus the conventional meaning “lotus” is also conveyed by derivation (*yoga*). But meanings such as “white lily” are excluded \[even though such things also take birth from the mud\]. In this way the conventional meaning \[i.e “lotus”\] is accompanied by a derived meaning \[i.e that which is born in the mud\]. Thus *paṅkaja* is a *yoga-rūḍha* word because by the power of convention created by the Supreme Lord one remembers only a lotus when one hears the word *paṅkaja.* When, however, somebody says something like “The difficulty in traversing the path is born from the mud,” then by figurative usage the word *paṅka-ja* “born from the mud” is understood to mean *paṅka-kṛta* “caused by the mud.”43 In reality, however, the word *paṅkaja* refers to a lotus.44

Examples of *rūḍha* words are *maṇḍapa* (pavilion) and so on. A particular pavilion is not the *kartā* of the action of drinking (*pa*) liquor (*maṇḍa*) as a lotus is the *kartā* of being born (*ja*) in the mud (*paṅka*). Thus *maṇḍapa* is a *rūḍha* word, since only “pavilion” is understood even without a derived meaning. In the phrase *karmaṇi kuśalaḥ* “expert in work” the word *kuśala* is not *lākṣaṇika,* because the word *kuśala* has many meanings, i.e *kuśalaḥ kṣeme puṇye ca śikṣite* “*Kuśala* means ‘security (*kṣema*),’ ‘auspicious (*puṇya*),’ and learned (*śikṣita*).’” Here learned (*śikṣita*) means expert (*nipuṇa*). Therefore in the phrase *karmaṇi kuśalaḥ* “expert in work” the word *kuśala* is *mukhya.*45 In the same way the word *maṇḍapa* is *mukhya* when, on account of being a *rūḍha* word, it refers to a pavilion. But when someone says *maṇḍapaṁ bhojayata* “feed the drinker of liquor,” then the word *maṇḍapa,* due to being mentioned along with another word \[i.e *bhojayata*\], is *lākṣaṇika* in that it refers to the *kartā* of the action of drinking liquor.

Examples of *yaugika* words are *āditeya* (son of Aditi) and so on. The word *āditeya*, being formed when the suffix *ḍha\[k\]* (*mādhava ḍha*) is applied after the word *aditi* in the sense of “the sons of Aditi,” only has a derived meaning. But when the derivation takes place with the different patronymical suffix *\[ṇ\]ya,* then the word *āditya* (son of Aditi), even though listed as a synonym of the word *deva* (demigod), actually refers to a particular demigod \[i.e Sūrya\], and thus on the strength of this understanding, *āditya* is actually a *yoga-rūḍha* word.

*svārtha-dravya-liṅga-saṅkhyā-karmādy-ātmakaḥ pañcakaḥ prātipadikārthaḥ. prakṛti-śaktiḥ prakṛty-artha-parā. sup-pratyaya-śaktiḥ saṅkhyā-kārakatvoparakta-prakṛty-artha-parā. tiṅ-śaktiḥ saṅkhyā-kartṛ-karma-bhāvoparakta-vartamāna-kāla-parā. upasargā dyotakād dhātv-artha-bhedakāḥ. yathā saṁhāraḥ, abhihāraḥ, vihāraḥ, āhāraḥ, militvā samabhivyāhāraḥ. evaṁ kṛt-taddhita-pratyayāv api pūrva-vat. praty-ekaṁ sūtra-karaṇāt sarvatra yogena mukhya evārthaḥ. (pāṇ. 3.3.1) “uṇādayo bahulam” iti bahula-grahaṇāt prāyaśo na yogārthāḥ. tena gacchatīti (uṇādi 225) “gamer ḍoḥ” iti kṛte ḍo-pratyaye “gauḥ śete” ity atrāpi mukhyārtha-bādhe lakṣaṇā eva syāt, tenoṇādi-pratyaye na yogārthaḥ, api tu rūḍha evārthaḥ. sa ca mukhya eva na lākṣaṇikaḥ.*

*Svārtha, dravya, liṅga*, *saṅkhyā,* and *karmādi—*these five are the meanings of a *prātipadika* (*nāma*).46MAd9 The designative power of the word base is focused on the meaning of the word base. The designative power of the nominal suffix is focused on the number and the meaning of the word base along with its particular *kāraka* relationship. The designative power of the verbal suffix is focused on the number and the present time along with the *kartā, karma,* or *bhāva.* The verbal prefixes, being suggestive in nature, bring out different inherent meaning of the verbal roots. For example, *saṁhāraḥ, abhihāraḥ, vihāraḥ,* and *āhāraḥ* join together to become *samabhivyāhāraḥ. Kṛdantas* and *taddhitas* are like the first kind \[i.e they are focused on the meaning of the word base\]. They are formed by derivation in all cases, since each *kṛt* and *taddhita* suffix is ordained in a *sūtra,* and therefore their derived meaning is their literal meaning. However, due to the word *bahula* in *uṇādayo bahulam* (*Aṣṭādhyāyī* 3.3.1), the *uṇādis* generally aren’t considered as words that have a derived meaning. For example, technically the word *go* is formed when the *uṇādi* suffix *\[ḍ\]o* (*o\[c\]*) is applied after the verbal root *gam* in the sense of “that which goes” by *gamer ḍoḥ* (*Uṇādi-sūtra* 2.67). But if this word were used in a sentence like *gauḥ śete* (“that which is going is sleeping”), then a figurative meaning would have to be used since the literal meaning wouldn’t make sense. Therefore the *uṇādis* don’t have a derived meaning \[like *go*=that which goes\], rather they have a conventional meaning \[like *go=*a cow\], and that conventional meaning is considered *mukhya* not *lākṣaṇika.*

*samāsa-śaktiś ca vividhā. tathā hi bahuvrīher anya-padārthe śaktiḥ, sā ca dvedhā—tad-guṇa-saṁvijñāna-rūpā, tad-itaraś ca. ubhaya-padārtha-pradhānā karmadhārayasya, uttara-padārtha-pradhānā tatpuruṣasya. avyayāṁśa-pradhānāvyayī-bhāvasya, nañ-artha-pradhānā nañ-tatpuruṣasya, pratyeka-pada-pradhānā dvandvasya. tatra sāhitya-prādhānye samāhāre ekatvam, yathā—dhavāśvakarṇam. sāhityāśraya-prādhānye itaretara-yoge dvitva-bahutve dhava-khadirāv ity atra dhava-sāhityavān khadiraḥ, khadira-sāhityavān dhava iti sāhitye ’pi dvayoḥ prādhānyād dvi-vacanam eva. evaṁ dhava-khadira-palaśā ity atra bahu-vacanam eva. sāhitya-mūlake dvitvādau vibhaktir na pratyekaṁ dvitvādikaṁ bodhayati. dhava-khadirau paśyeti kriyā tu pratyekaṁ sambadhyate. eka-śeṣeṣu tu lakṣaṇaiva—pitarāv ity ekasya pitṛ-dvayānvayābhāvāt pitṛ-mātarāv eva lakṣyete, pitṛ-śabdasya janakārtha-mātra-smṛteḥ.*

Moreover, different compounds have different focuses. For example, the focus of *bahuvrīhi* compounds is on the other thing. Such focus is of two kinds: where there is perception of the quality of the other thing and where there is not.47 The focus of *karmadhāraya* compounds is the meanings of both words. The focus of *tatpuruṣa* compounds is the meaning of the last word. The focus of *avyayī-bhāva* compounds is the *avyaya* portion*.* The focus of *nañ-tatpuruṣa* compounds is the meaning of the word *na\[ñ\].* The focus of *dvandva* compounds is each individual word. In that regard, in *samāhāra-dvandvas* the group is the focus and thus the singular is used. For example, *dhavāśvakarṇam* “the *dhava* tree and the *aśvakarṇa* tree.” But in *itaretara-yoga-dvandvas* the individual members of the group are the focus and thus the dual or plural is used. For example, in *dhava-khadirau* “the *dhava* tree and the *khadira* tree” MAd10the dual is used and in *dhava-khadira-palaśāḥ* “the *dhava* tree, the *khadira* tree, and the *palaśa* tree” the plural is used. When the individual members of the group are the focus, the dual and so on doesn’t signify that there are two and so on of each indiviual member. But in *dhava-khadirau paśya* “see the *dhava* tree and the *khadira* tree” the verb *paśya* is connected to each individual member. In *eka-śeṣa* compounds like *pitarau,* however, there is *lakṣaṇā vṛtti.* Since the word *pitṛ* makes one remember only the meaning “father” and since it is impossible for one person to have two fathers, the meaning that comes by *lakṣaṇā vṛtti* is *pitṛ-mātarau* “the father and mother.”

*vācako ’pi śabdaḥ samāsa-sad-bhāve sati lākṣaṇiko bhavati. dhī-vara iti kaivarta-vācakaḥ śabdaḥ, dhiyā vara iti tṛtīyā-tatpuruṣa-samāsena su-buddhiḥ pratipādyate. tatra tu lakṣaṇaiva. evaṁ sarvatra lakṣaṇā boddhavyā, diṅ-mātram udāhṛtam. yaugikās tu śabdāḥ siddhāḥ sādhyāś ca. siddhās tu vāsudevādayaḥ. sādhyā vaktṛ-svecchayā kḷptā ānakadundubhi-nandanādayaḥ. te ca pūrva-pada-parivṛtti-sahāḥ, uttara-pada-parivṛtti-sahāḥ, ubhaya-pada-parivṛtti-sahāś ca. vasudeva-nandana iti pūrva-padasya parivṛttiḥ, ānakadundubhi-suta iti para-padasya, śūra-suta-putra ity ubhaya-padasya. evam unneyam. kvacid ubhaya-padāparivṛttiḥ, pattra-ratho garut-mān go-saṅkhya ity-ādi.*

Even a *mukhya* word becomes *lākṣaṇika* when analyzed as a compound. For example, the word *dhīvaraḥ* literally refers to a fisherman. But the meaning “one whose intelligence is excellent” that comes from analyzing *dhīvaraḥ* as a *tṛtīyā-tatpuruṣa* compound meaning *dhiyā varaḥ* “excellent on account of intelligence” is figurative. *Lakṣaṇā vṛtti* should be understood in all such cases. I have merely illustrated the point with one example.48

The *yaugika* words are of two kinds: existing and made-up. Examples of existing words are *vāsudeva* and so on. Made-up words are words that are created in accordance with the speaker’s desire. Examples of made-up words are *ānakadundubhi-nandana* and so on. *Yaugika* words are of a further three varieties: those that can tolerate switching of the first word, those that can tolerate switching of the second word, and those that can tolerate switching of both words. For example, *vasudeva-nandana* can tolerate switching of the first word, *ānakadundubhi-suta* can tolerate switching of the second word, and *śūra-suta-putra* can tolerate switching of both words.49 Things should be inferred in this way. Sometimes certain *yaugika* words can’t tolerate switching of either of the words. Examples are *patra-ratha, garutmān, go-saṅkhya,* and so on.50

### Text 11-13

*mukhyo lākṣaṇiko vyañjaka iti ye tri-vidhāḥ pūrvoktās te punar api lakṣayiṣyamāṇād vṛtti-trayād dhetor iha prastūyante—*

Actually, the three kinds of words that were described previously, i.e *mukhya, lākṣaṇika,* and *vyañjaka* (see 2.8-9), are being discussed here because of the three modes of meaning that will be defined soon.

***vṛtti-trayāt punas tredhā***

**Words are of three kinds because of the three modes of meaning.**

*te śabdā iti gamyam.*

The words *te śabdāḥ* are understood here.

***…vṛttayas tv abhidhādayaḥ***

**The modes of meaning are** ***abhidhā*** **and so on.**

*vṛtti-trayaṁ tu—abhidhā, lakṣaṇā, vyañjaneti, abhidhaiva śaktiḥ.*

The three modes of meaning are *abhidhā* (literal), *lakṣaṇā* (figurative), and *vyañjanā* (suggestive). *Abhidhā* is also called *śakti* (designative power).

***yasyoccāraṇa-mātreṇa sahajaṁ yat pratīyate***  
***tasya tatra tu yā vṛttiḥ sābhidhā***

*yasya—*of which word; *uccāraṇa-mātreṇa—*by the mere pronunciation; *sahajam—*naturally; *yat—*which object; *pratīyate—*is perceived; *tasya—*of that word; *tatra—*to that object; *tu—*only; *yā—*which; *vṛttiḥ—*reference, i.e mode of meaning; *sā—*that; *abhidhā—*called *abhidhā.*

**The mode of meaning where a word refers only to that object which is perceived naturally by the mere pronunciation of the word is called** ***abhidhā.***

*yathā go-śabdasya sāsnādimati prāṇi-viśeṣe vṛttiḥ. sā na vyaktau api tu jātau. vyaktau cet tarhi go-viśeṣa eva pratipādyate, na tu go-mātram. jātau cet tarhi vyavahārānupapattiḥ, ato jāty-ākṣipta-vyaktāv eva, na tu tatra tatra lakṣaṇā, ākṣepa-labhyatvāt, avinābhāvo hy ākṣepaḥ.*

For example, the word *go* (cow) refers to the particular living entity that possesses a dewlap and so on. Here the reference is not to the individual but to the category. If the reference were to an individual, then the word *go* would refer only to one particular cow and not to a cow in general. If the reference were to a category, then there would be lack of practicality. Therefore the reference is to the individual that is automatically indicated by the category. It is not that such reference is figurative, because it is obtained by *ākṣepa. Ākṣepa* is the absence of existence of one thing without the other thing \[i.e like smoke doesn’t exist without fire, so the category doesn’t exist without the individual\].

### Text 14-15

*…****lakṣaṇā punaḥ***  
***mukhyārtha-bādhe śakyasya sambandhe yānya-dhīr bhavet***

*lakṣaṇā—*called *lakṣaṇā; punaḥ—*furthermore; *mukhya-artha—*of the literal meaning; *bādhe—*when there is blockage; *śakyasya—*of the literal meaning (*vācya*); *sambandhe—*when there is connection; *yā—*which; *anya-dhīḥ—*understanding of another meaning; *bhavet—*is.

***Lakṣaṇā*** **is the mode of meaning which causes the understanding of another meaning connected with the literal meaning when the literal meaning doesn’t make sense.**

*“gaṅgāyāṁ ghoṣaḥ” ity āptoktau kaścit parāmṛśati—gaṅgāyāṁ ghoṣānvayābhāvād ghoṣa-śabdo ’tra dhvany-arthaḥ, na tv ābhīra-pally-arthaḥ. tataḥ prativasatīti śrute gaṅgā-śabdo vā sva-sambandhi-tīram abhidhatte, ghoṣa-śabdo vā sva-sambandhinaṁ pratibimbam abhidhatte. ubhayor eva lākṣaṇikatvaṁ sambhāvyate. tad atra neyaṁ rūḍhi-lakṣaṇā, api tu prayojanavatī lakṣaṇaiva. yad ayam aśrānto vaktā, tat prayojanam eva vicāryam. yadi gaṅgā-śabdas taṭaṁ lakṣayati, tadā tasya śaitya-pāvanatvādikam eva prayojanam. yadi vā ghoṣa-śabdaḥ sva-pratibimbaṁ lakṣayati, tadāpi ghoṣasya tat-tīra-naikatyātiśaya-pratipādanena tad eva tīra-gataṁ śaitya-pāvanāditvam, adhikaṁ ca gaṅgā-jalasya svacchatvam. tenādhika-prayojana-lābhe ghoṣa-śabda eva lākṣaṇika iti parāmarśānantaraṁ punas tam ānayeti śrute gaṅgā-śabda eva lākṣaṇiko na ghoṣa-śabda iti niścinvan, “ghoṣaḥ prativasati, tam ānaya” ity ukte nānvaya-bādhas tena vākye na lakṣaṇā, api tu gaṅgā-śabda eveti vyutpādyate. iyaṁ tu lakṣaṇā jahat-svārthāpi sva-sambandha-mātreṇa svāvinā-bhāva-pratipādayitrī. “kuntāḥ praviśanti” ity-ādāv ajahat-svārthā, kunta-dhāritvena kunta-sahita-praveśāt. “śoṇo dhāvati” ity atra na lakṣaṇā (patañjali-vārttike) “kvacid guṇa-vacanān matupo lug iṣṭaḥ” iti luptenāpi matupā mukhyārtha evābhidhīyate. “chatriṇo gacchanti” iti samūhārthe lakṣaṇā, sarveṣāṁ chatritvenānvayābhāvāt.*

*“ratho gacchati” ity atra jahad-ajahat-svārthā, svākarṣaka-gamanena śakya-sambandhāt sva-kartṛka-gamanābhāvād aṁśato jahat-svārthā gamanāṁśenājahat-svārthā. kecid ākhyātasya prayatna-vācakatvād acetane tad-abhāvāt, vyāpāre lakṣaṇāṁ paṭhanti, kecid ākhyātasyānukūla-vyāpāra-pradhānatayā cetanācetanayor dhātv-arthānukūla-vyāpārasya sattvād ākhyāta-prayogo mukhya eveti.*

*“mañcāḥ krośanti” ity atra sva-sambandha-mātreṇa jahat-svārthaiva, neyaṁ prayojanavatī, na vā rūḍhi-lakṣaṇā.*

For example, When a reliable person says *gaṅgāyāṁ ghoṣaḥ* (“the cowherd village on the river Ganges”), someone thinks “The word *ghoṣa* here must refer to a sound and not to a cowherd village, because of the impossibility of a cowherd village on the river Ganges.” Then, when that person hears the reliable person say *prativasati* (“is situated”) \[i.e *gaṅgāyāṁ ghoṣaḥ prativasati* (“the cowherd village is situated on the river Ganges”)\], he thinks “Either the word *gaṅgā* refers to the shore connected with the river Ganges or the word *ghoṣa* refers to the reflection connected with the cowherd village. Both words could be *lākṣaṇika*. Therefore in this case the figurative usage is not conventional figurative usage but rather purposeful figurative usage. Because the speaker is not tired, therefore a purpose must be understood. If the word *gaṅgā* figuratively refers to the shore of the Ganges then the purpose is to demonstrate the shore’s coolness, purity, and so on. But if the word *ghoṣa* figuratively refers to the reflection of the cowherd village then also the purpose is to describe the same coolness, purity, and so on of the shore, since the cowherd village is described as being very close to the shore, but additionally the purpose is to describe the clearness of the water of the Ganges. Therefore, due to the obtainment of an additional purpose, only the word *ghoṣa* is *lākṣaṇika.*”

However, when, after \[wrongly\] thinking like this, that person hears the sentence *tam ānaya* (“Bring the reflection of the cowherd village”), he decides that only the word *gaṅgā* and not the word *ghoṣa* is *lākṣaṇika.*51 Then, when he hears the sentence *ghoṣaḥ prativasati, tam ānaya* (“The cowherd village is situated. Bring it.”), he rightly concludes that, since the literal meaning is not blocked, there is no figurative usage in this sentence, rather the literal meaning is blocked only when the word *gaṅgā* is used, and thus the word *gaṅgā* is *lākṣaṇika.*”

Even though this figurative usage \[i.e *gaṅgā*=“the shore”\] is *jahat-svārthā* (figurative usage where the word loses its original meaning), on account of the mere connection with the Ganges, it nonetheless conveys the necessary connection between the shore and the Ganges. In examples like *kuntāḥ praviśanti* (“The spears enter.”) the figurative usage is *ajahat-svārthā* (figurative usage where the word doesn’t lose its original meaning), since the spearmen enter along with the spears since they are holding the spears.52 But there is no figurative usage in *śoṇo dhāvati* (“The red runs.”) because the meaning is already literally expressed by the suffix *mat\[up\],* even though *mat\[up\]* is deleted by the rule *kvacid guṇa-vacanān matupo lug iṣṭaḥ* “Sometimes *mat\[up\]* is deleted when it comes after a word expressing a quality.” (*vārttika* on *Aṣṭādhyāyī* 5.2.94)53
