In the example *chatriṇo gacchanti* (“The students are going.”), the word *chatriṇaḥ* figuratively refers to a crowd of people \[primarily made up of students\] because of the impossibility of everyone in the crowd being a student.54 In the example *ratho gacchati* (“The chariot moves.”) the figurative usage is *jahad-ajahat-svārtha.* I.e it is partially *jahat-svārtha* because of the connection with the literal meaning due to the movement of the puller of the chariot on account of the impossibility of the chariot’s moving by itself, and it is partially *ajahat-svārtha* on account of the movement.55 Others say that, since a verb expresses effort and since effort cannot exist in an inanimate object, the verb here figuratively refers to the *vyāpāra* (intermediate activity).56 Yet others say that the use of the verb is literal here, since the verb is based on the favorable *vyāpāras* and both the animate and the inanimate object have a *vyāpāra* favorable for achieving the meaning of the *dhātu* \[i.e movement\].57

In the example *mañcāḥ krośanti* (“The beds scream”) the figurative usage is certainly *jahat-svārtha* on account of the \[babies’\] mere connection with the beds, but it is neither purposeful nor conventional.58

### Text 16

***rūḍhyā prayojanenāpi sā dvidhā***

***Lakṣaṇā*** **is based on either a convention or a purpose. Thus there are two kinds of** ***lakṣaṇā: rūḍhi-lakṣaṇā*** **and** ***prayojanavatī lakṣaṇā.***

*sā lakṣaṇā dvidhā bhavatīty arthaḥ. “viṣvaksena” ity-ādau viṣūcī senā yasyeti bahutara-senāvati mahā-rājādau vyutpannatve ’pi śaktyā bhagavaty api bhagavad-bhakta-viśeṣe eva rūḍhiḥ.*

*Sā dvidhā* means “*Lakṣaṇā* is of two kinds.” *Viṣvaksenaḥ* is an example of conventional figurative usage. Although in terms of its derived meaning, i.e *viṣūcī senā yasya* (“one whose army is everywhere”), it refers to a great king who possesses a vast army and by dictionary meaning (*śakti=abhidhā*) it refers to the Lord, by conventional figurative usage it refers to a particular devotee of the Lord.59

### Text 17-18

***“gaṅgāyāṁ ghoṣaḥ prativasati” ity-ādau prayojanam, tat tu śaitya-pāvanatvādi. ata uktam (kumārila-bhaṭṭa-kṛta-śloka-vārttike)***

***abhidheyāvinā-bhūta-pratītir lakṣaṇocyate***  
***lakṣyamāṇa-guṇair yogād vṛtter iṣṭā tu gauṇatā***

*iti. (kāvya-prakāśe 2.13) “vyaṅgyena rahitā rūḍhau sahitā tu prayojane” iti vacanād vyaṅgya-sahitā prayojanavatī laksaṇaiva prayojikā, na rūḍhi-lakṣaṇā.*

*Gaṅgāyāṁ ghoṣaḥ prativasati* “the cowherd village is situated on the river Ganges” (see 2.14-15) is an example of purposeful figurative usage, the purpose being to demonstrate the shore’s coolness, purity, and so on. Therefore in his commentary entitled *Śloka-vārttika* Kumārila Bhaṭṭa says “*Lakṣaṇā* is the understanding of something that is necessarily connected with the the thing that is being literally expressed. And *gauṇī vṛtti* 60 is so named because of the connection with certain implied qualities (*guṇas*).”

According to the statement *vyaṅgyena rahitā rūḍhau sahitā tu prayojane* “*Lakṣaṇā* is devoid of an implication when there is a convention but is accompanied by an implication when there is a purpose.” (*Kāvya-prakāśa* 2.13), only purposeful figurative usage, which is accompanied by an implication, is meaningful and not conventional figurative usage.61

### Text 19-20

***bhidyate ca sā***

**And there are many kinds of** ***prayojanavatī lakṣaṇā:***

***prayojanavatī lakṣaṇā bhidyate, vividhā bhavatīty arthaḥ.***

*Sā* means *prayojanavatī lakṣaṇā* (“purposeful figurative usage”) and *bhidyate* means *vividhā bhavati* (“is of many kinds”).

***sāropā sāropyamāṇa āropa-viṣayo ’pi ca***  
***yatra vyaktau***

*sa-āropā—*is called *sāropā* (“with a superimposition”); *sā—*that *prayojanavatī lakṣaṇā; āropyamāṇaḥ—*the subject of the superimposition; *āropa-viṣayaḥ—*the object of the superimposition; *api—*also; *ca—*and; *yatra—*where; *vyaktau—*are clear.

***Prayojanavatī lakṣaṇā*** **where both the subject and object of the superimposition are clear is called** ***sāropā lakṣanā.***

*yatra lakṣaṇāyām āropyamāṇa āropa-viśayaś ca sphuṭau, sā sāropā. yathā “amṛtaṁ śrī-kṛṣṇa-guṇa-śravaṇam.” atrāmṛtam āropyamāṇa guṇa-śravaṇam āropa-viṣayo dvāv eva sphuṭau. “gaur bāhīkaḥ” ity anye.*

The *anvaya* of this verse is *yatra lakṣaṇāyām āropyamāṇa āropa-viśayaś ca sphuṭau, sā sāropā.* For example, in *amṛtaṁ śrī-kṛṣṇa-guṇa-śravaṇam* (“Hearing of Kṛṣṇa’s qualities is nectar \[i.e like nectar\]”) the subject of the superimposition, i,e nectar, and the object of the superimposition, i.e hearing of Kṛṣṇa’s qualities, are clear. Others give the example *gaur bāhīkaḥ* (“The *bāhīka* is a bull \[i.e like a bull\]”).62

### Text 21

***…ādināntar-nigīrṇe carame sati***  
***bhavet sādhyavasānā sā***

*ādinā—*by the first one, i.e by the subject of the superimposition; *antar-nigīrṇe—*swallowed up *carame—*when the last one, i.e the object of the superimposition; *sati—*is; *bhavet—*is; *sa-adhyavasānā—*called *sādhyavasānā* (“with a termination \[of the object of the superimposition\]”); *sā—*that *sāropā lakṣaṇā.*

***Sāropā lakṣanā*** **is called** ***sādhyavasānā lakṣanā*** **when the object of the superimposition is swallowed up by the subject of the superimposition.**

*sā sāropā sādhyavasānā bhavet, ādinā āropyamāṇena carame āropa-viṣaye ’ntar-nigīrṇe sati. yathā “amṛtam evedam” ity āropyamāneṇāmṛtena guṇa-śravaṇam āropa-viṣayo ’ntar-nigīrṇaḥ. “gaur evāyam” ity eke.*

The *anvaya* of this verse is *sā sāropā sādhyavasānā bhavet, ādinā āropyamāṇena carame āropa-viṣaye ’ntar-nigīrṇe sati.* For example, in *amṛtam evedam* (“This is just nectar \[i.e just like nectar\]”), the object of the superimposition, i.e hearing of Kṛṣṇa’s qualities, is swallowed up by the subject of the superimposition, i.e nectar. Others give the example *gaur evāyam* (“This is just a bull \[i.e just like a bull”).63

### Text 22-23

*…****bhide dve dvi-vidhe ime***  
***gauṇe śuddhe ca sādṛśyāt sambandhāntarato ’pi ca***

*bhide—*kinds; *dve—*two; *dvi-vidhe—*of two kinds; *ime—*these; *gauṇe—*qualitative; *śuddhe—*pure; *ca—*and; *sādṛśyāt—*because of similarity; *sambandha-antarataḥ—*because of some other kind of relationship; *api—*also; *ca—*and.

**These two kinds \[i.e** ***sāropā lakṣaṇā*** **and** ***sādhyavasānā lakṣaṇā*****\] can be further subdivided into another two kinds: qualitative (*****gauṇī*****) when the superimposition is based on a relationship of similarity or pure (*****śuddhā*****) when the superimposition is based on some other kind of relationship.**

*etau bhedau sādṛśyād gauṇau, sambandhāntarataḥ śuddhau bhavata ity arthaḥ.*

The *anvaya* of this verse is *etau bhedau sādṛśyād gauṇau, sambandhāntarataḥ śuddhau bhavataḥ.*

### Text 24

*sādṛśya-hetukā tūktā, sambandhāntara-hetukā yathā—“bhagavad-bhaktir mahat-saṅgaḥ,” “bhagavad-bhaktir evāyam.” atra kārya-kāraṇa-bhāva-sambandhaḥ. “āyur ghṛtam,” “āyur evedam” ity anye. kvacit tād-arthyād yathā—“kṛṣṇa-sevārtho vyāpāraḥ kṛṣṇa-sevā.” kvacit sva-svāmi-bhāva-sambandhād yathā—“kṛṣṇasya sakhā kṛṣṇaḥ.” kvacit tāt-karmyād yathā—“kṛṣṇo go-pālanād gopaḥ.” sarvatra prayojanam, na rūḍhiḥ.*

*Sāropā lakṣaṇā* and *sadhyāvasānā lakṣaṇā* which are based on a relationship of similarity were already described (see 2.19-21). Examples of when they are based on some other kind of relationship are *bhagavad-bhaktir mahat-saṅgaḥ* (“Assocation of devotees is devotion to the Lord \[i.e the cause of devotion to the Lord\]”) and *bhagavad-bhaktir evāyam* (“This is just devotion to the Lord \[i.e the cause of devotion to the Lord\]”). In these examples there is a relationship of cause and effect.64 Others give the examples *āyur ghṛtam* (“Ghee is longevity \[i.e the cause of longevity\]”) and *āyur evedam* (“This is just longevity \[i.e the cause of longevity\]”).

Sometimes the figurative usage is based on a relationship of something’s being for the sake of something else, i.e An intermediate activity \[i.e taking a bath etc.\] that is performed for the sake of serving Kṛṣṇa is figuratively called service to Kṛṣṇa.65 Sometimes the figurative usage is based on a relationship of property and proprietor, i.e Kṛṣṇa’s friend is figuratively called Kṛṣṇa.66 Sometimes the figurative usage is based on a relationship of having that as one’s work, i.e Kṛṣṇa is figuratively called a cowherd because He protects cows.67 In all these cases the figurative usage is purposeful and not conventional.68

### Text 25

***parākṣepaḥ sva-siddhy-arthaṁ parasmin sva-samarpaṇam***  
***yayos te lakṣaṇe śuddhe prāg upādāna-lakṣaṇe***

*para—*of something else; *ākṣepaḥ—*indication; *sva-siddhy-artham—*for the sake of its accomplishing \[the action\]; *parasmin—*to something else; *sva-samarpaṇam—*imparting its own \[qualities\]; *yayoḥ—*whose; *te—*those; *lakṣaṇe—*two kinds of *lakṣaṇā; śuddhe—*pure; *prāg—*preceding element; *upādāna-lakṣaṇe—*the words *upādāna* and *lakṣaṇa.*

**When the literal meaning indicates something else for the sake of accomplishing the action, that is called** ***upādāna-lakṣaṇā*** **(inclusive indication), and when the literal meaning imparts its own qualities to something else, that is called** ***lakṣaṇa-lakṣaṇā*** **(exclusive indication).69 Both of these kinds of** ***lakṣaṇā*** **are considered pure (*****śuddhā*****).70**

*prāg-vartinī upādāna-lakṣaṇa-pade yayos te. tena upādāna-lakṣaṇā, lakṣaṇa-lakṣaṇā vety arthaḥ. upacāreṇāmiśratvāt śuddhe. pṛthaktvena vartamānayor dvayor aikyāropa upacāraḥ. tatra “veṇur gāyati, vīṇāḥ śrutim anukurvanti” iti veṇv-ādibhiḥ sva-siddhy-arthaṁ sva-sva-vādakānāṁ pareṣāṁ śrī-kṛṣṇa-lalitādīnām ākṣepaḥ kṛta ity upādāna-lakṣaṇā. yatra yatrāvinā-bhāvo ’rthāpattir vā, tatra tatra nopādāna-lakṣaṇā, prayojana-rūḍhyor abhāvāt. yathā—“gaur anubandhyaḥ” iti śruti-coditam anubandhanaṁ kathaṁ syād iti jātyā vyaktir ākṣipyate, na tu śabdenocyate, “viśeṣyaṁ nābhidhā gacchet kṣīṇa-śaktir viśeṣaṇe” iti nyāyāt. uktaṁ ca vākyapadīye—“gauḥ svarūpeṇa na gaur nāpy agaur gotvābhisambandhāt tu gauḥ” iti.*

*evaṁ kriyatām ity atra kartā, kurv ity atra karma, praviśa gṛham, piṇḍaṁ bhakṣayety-ādiṣu ākṣepa eva. “pīno devadatto divā na bhuṅkte, rātrau bhuṅkte” iti tu arthāpattyaiva gamyata ity-ādiṣu nopādāna-lakṣaṇā. kevalaṁ “kuntāḥ praviśanti” ity-ādiṣu sā. “gaṅgāyāṁ ghoṣaḥ” ity-ādau śaitya-pāvanatvādi-sva-guṇa-samarpaṇa-lakṣaṇena lakṣaṇa-lakṣaṇā.*

The word *prāg* in the verse means *prāg-vartinī* and the word *upādāna-lakṣaṇe* means *upādāna-lakṣaṇa-pade.* Thus “those \[*lakṣaṇās*\] which are preceded by the words *upādāna* and *lakṣaṇa*” means “*upādāna-lakṣaṇā* and *lakṣaṇa-lakṣaṇā.*” Both these kinds of *lakṣaṇā* are pure because they do not involve *upacāra. Upacāra* is the superimposition of oneness on two things which exist separately.71

In this regard *veṇur gāyati, vīṇāḥ śrutim anukurvanti* “The flute sings, and the *vīṇās* follow the notes.” is an example of *upādāna-lakṣaṇā* because the flute and the *vīṇās* indicate something else, namely Kṛṣṇa and the *gopīs* like Lalitā who are their players.72

*Upādāna-lakṣaṇā* cannot exist wherever there is necessary connection or inference, since there is no purpose or convention in either case. An example of necessary connection: Upon hearing the injunction *gaur anubandhyaḥ*, a person wonders “The *śruti* has enjoined that cowness should be tied to the sacrificial post, but how can that be accomplished?” He then ascertains that an individual is indicated by the category but not directly expressed by the word, in accordance with the maxim “The literal meaning exhausts its power in referring to the quality \[i.e cowness\]. It cannot then refer to the thing possessing the quality \[i.e the individual cow\].” And in the *Vākya-pādīya* it is said “A cow is not considered a cow merely on account of form \[i.e having a dewlap and so on\]. Nor is it considered as something other than a cow. A cow is considered a cow because of connection with cowness.”

Another example of necessary connection: When someone says *kaṭaḥ kriyatām* (“a straw mat should be made”) a *kartā* \[i.e *tvayā* (by you)\] is understood, when someone says *tvam kuru* (“you should make”) a *karma* \[i.e *kaṭam* (straw mat)\] is understood, when someone says *praviśa* (“enter”) the house is understood, and when someone says *piṇḍam* (“the lump of food”) the imperative verb “eat” is understood. In all such cases there is just necessary connection \[but no *upādāna-lakṣaṇā,* since there is no purpose or convention\].

An example of when the understanding comes about by inference is “Devadatta is fat, but he doesn’t eat in the day. He must eat at night.” In such cases there is no *upādāna-lakṣaṇā. Upādāna-lakṣaṇā* is there only in cases like *kuntāḥ praviśanti* (“the spears enter”). In cases like *gaṅgāyāṁ ghoṣaḥ* (“the cowherd village on the Ganges”) there is *lakṣaṇa-lakṣaṇā* on account of the Ganges imparting its own qualities of coolness, purity, and so on.

### Text 26

***pūrvaiś caturbhir bhedaiḥ sā dvābhyām ābhyāṁ ca ṣaḍ-vidhā***

**Thus** ***lakṣaṇā*** **is of six kinds on account of the previous four kinds \[i.e** ***gauṇī sāropā lakṣaṇā, śuddhā sāropā lakṣaṇā, gauṇī sādhyavasānā lakṣaṇā,*** **and** ***śuddhā sādhyavasānā lakṣaṇā*****\] and these two kinds \[i.e** ***upādāna-lakṣaṇā*** **and** ***lakṣaṇa-lakṣaṇā*****\].**

*pūrvaiḥ sāropādibhir ābhyām upādāna-lakṣaṇā-lakṣaṇa-lakṣaṇābhyām.*

In this verse *pūrvaiḥ* means *sāropādibhiḥ* and *ābhyām* means *upādāna-lakṣaṇā-lakṣaṇa-lakṣaṇābhyām.*

### Text 27

***gūḍha-vyaṅgyā gata-vyaṅgyā vyakta-vyaṅgyeti sā tridhā***

*gūḍha-vyaṅgyā—*where the implied meaning is hidden; *gata-vyaṅgyā—*where the implied meaning is absent; *vyakta-vyaṅgyā—*where the implied meaning is obvious; *iti—*thus; *sā—lakṣaṇā; tridhā—*is of three kinds.

***Lakṣaṇā*** **is further divided into three categories:** ***lakṣaṇā*** **where the suggestion is abstruse,** ***lakṣaṇā*** **where there is no suggestion, and** ***lakṣaṇā*** **where the suggestion is obvious.73**

*sā lakṣaṇā. gata-vyaṅgyeha nādriyate.*

In this verse *sā* means *lakṣaṇā.* In this regard, *lakṣaṇā* where there is no suggestion is not really accepted as *lakṣaṇā.*74

### Text 28

***gūḍha-vyaṅgyā yathā—***

An example of *lakṣaṇā* where the suggestion is abstruse is:

***utkīrṇair iva citritair iva navodbhinnair ivodyad-vayaḥ-***  
***kunde vibhramitair iva smara-kalā-śāṇe niśātair iva***  
***magnonmagnatayālasair iva bhṛśaṁ lāvaṇya-vāpī-jale***  
***keyaṁ keli-kalā-nidhiḥ subala me ceto haraty aṅgakaiḥ***

*utkīrṇaiḥ—*artistically carved; *iva*—as if; *citritaiḥ*—multi-colored; *iva—*as if; *nava-udbhinnaiḥ—*newly sprouted; *iva—*as if; *udyad-vayaḥ-kunde—*for fresh jasmine flowers; *vibhramitaiḥ*—confused; *iva*—as if; *smara-kalā—*of Cupid’s arts; *śāṇe—*on the grindstone *niśātaiḥ—*sharpened; *iva—*as if; *magna-unmagnatayā—*on account of being dipped in and taken out of; *alasaiḥ—*lazy; *iva*—as if; *bhṛśaṁ—*repeatedly; *lāvaṇya—*of loveliness; *vāpī—*of a pond; *jale—*the water; *kā—*who; *iyam—*this girl; *keli-kalā-nidhiḥ—*who is a reservoir of playful arts; *subala—*O Subala; *me—*my; *cetaḥ—*my mind; *harati—*steals; *aṅgakaiḥ—*with her limbs.

**“O Subala, who is this reservoir of playful arts who steals my mind with her limbs. Her limbs are as if artistically carved, as if multi-colored, as if newly sprouted, as if confused for fresh jasmine flowers, as if sharpened on the grindstone of Cupid’s arts, and as if repeatedly dipped in and taken out of a pond of loveliness.”**

### Text 29

*atrotkīrṇādināṁ lākṣaṇikānāṁ padānāṁ vyaṅgyaṁ gūḍhatayā prakāśate. tathā hi—utkīrṇair iti nirmāṇa-viśeṣaḥ, citritair iti nānā-varṇatvāt kara-caraṇa-nayana-bhrū-latādiṣu ye ye varṇās taiś citritvam, navodbhinnair ity aṅkuratvāropeṇa komalatvaṁ dhvanyate. udyad-vayaḥ-kunda iti suvalitatvaṁ, smara-kalā-śāṇa iti hi smara-kṛta-śāṇatayā ceto-bhedakatvam, magnonmagnatayeti lāvaṇyādhikyam. idaṁ tv aspaṣṭam eva.*

Here the suggestions made by the figurative words *utkīrṇaiḥ* and so on are abstruse. For instance, excellent construction is implied by the word *utkīrṇaiḥ,* multi-coloredness on account of the different colors in the hands, feet, eyes, eyebrows, and so on is implied by the word *citritaiḥ,* softness on account of the superimposition of being fresh sprouts is implied by the word *navodbhinnaiḥ,*  the quality of being fully endowed is implied by the word *udyad-vayaḥ-kunda,* the quality of piercing the heart is implied by the word *smara-kalā-śāṇe,* and abundance of beauty is implied by the word *magnonmagnatayā.* But all of these suggestions are abstruse.

*agūḍhā yathā—*

An example of *lakṣaṇā* where the suggestion is obvious is:

*līlā-vilāsa-mahurima-garimā āhīra akumāriāṇaṁ*  
*kaṇhāṇurāa-guruṇā viaḍḍha-bhāa paḍhāido jhatti*  
*(līlā-vilāsa-madhurima-garimā ābhīra-kumārikānām*  
*kṛṣṇānurāga-guruṇā vidagdha-bhāvaṁ pāṭhito jhaṭiti)*

*līlā-vilāsa*—of enjoying playful pastimes; *madhurima*—of sweetness; *garimā*—in which there is greatness; *ābhīra-kumārikānām*—of the cowherd girls; *kṛṣṇa-anurāga*—that is their love for Kṛṣṇa; *guruṇā*—by the *guru; vidagdha-bhāvaḥ*—skillfulness; *pāṭhitaḥ*—taught; *jhaṭiti*—quickly.

**“Love for Kṛṣṇa, the** ***guru*** **of the** ***gopīs,*** **quickly taught them skillfulness in the great sweetness of playful pastimes.”**

*atra pāṭhita ity agūḍha-vyaṅgyam.*

The word *pāṭhita*MAd11 here creates an obvious suggestion.75

### Text 30

***atha kā nāma vyañjaneti vyañjanā-lakṣaṇam āha—***

Now in response to the question “What is *vyañjanā* (suggestion) actually?”, he gives the definition of *vyañjanā.*

***abhidhā-lakṣaṇākṣepa-tātparyāṇāṁ samāptitaḥ***  
***vyāpāro dhvananādir yaḥ śabdasya vyañjanā tu sā***

*abhidhā—*of the literal meaning; *lakṣaṇā—*of the figurative meaning; *ākṣepa—*of the neccesary connection (see 2.13 and 2.25); *tātparyāṇām—*of the meaning of the sentence; *samāptitaḥ—*after the completion; *vyāpāraḥ—*function; *dhvanana—*of the understanding of an implied meaning; *ādiḥ—*the cause; *yaḥ—*which; *śabdasya—*of a word; *vyañjanā—vyañjanā* (suggestion); *tu—*but; *sā—*that function.76

***Vyañjanā*** **(suggestion) is that power of a word which causes the understanding of an implied meaning after the completion of the literal meaning, figurative meaning, necessary connection, and the meaning of the sentence.77**

*“gaṅgāyāṁ ghoṣaḥ” ity atra gaṅgā-śabdaḥ prathamaṁ vācakatvenābhidhā-vṛttikaḥ, anyathānvayābhāva eva na syāt. anantaram abhidhā-samāptau lakṣaṇām āśritya taṭaṁ lakṣayati. tad-anantaraṁ lakṣaṇā-samāptau vyañjanām āśritya śaitya-pāvanatvādikaṁ prayojanaṁ vyanakti. lakṣaṇāyāḥ sa-vyaṅgyāvyaṅgyatayā nirūpitatvāt savyaṅgya-lakṣaṇaiva vyañjanā-jananī.*

In *gaṅgāyāṁ ghoṣaḥ* the word *gaṅgā* first of all expresses the literal meaning (i.e the river Ganges), since it is a *vācaka* (a word that express a thing). If this were not the case, there would be no absence of logical connection between the words.78 After the completion of the literal meaning, the word *gaṅgā* then resorts to the figurative meaning and indicates the shore. And after the completion of the figurative meaning, it resorts to the power of suggestion and implies a purpose such as demonstrating the coolness and purity. Since figurative meaning was previously described as being of two kinds, i.e with an implied meaning and without an implied meaning (see 2.17-18), only figurative usage that has an implied meaning \[i.e *prayojanavatī lakṣaṇā*\] can produce suggestion.

### Text 31

***(kāvya-prakāśe dvitīyollāse 28, 29)—***

***prayojanena sahitaṁ lakṣaṇīyaṁ na lakṣayet***  
***jñānasya viṣayo hy anyaḥ phalam anyad udāhṛtam***

*ity ukteḥ “śabda-buddhi-karmaṇāṁ viramya vyāpārābhāvaḥ” ity ukteś cābhidhā-lakṣaṇayor upakṣīṇatvāt, ākāṅkṣādimat-pada-kadambasya vākyatve vākyārthasyaiva tātparyārthatve vākyaṁ vināpi ekasya ca padasya padāṁśasyāpi vyañjakatvena dhvani-kāvyatvāṅgī-kṛtes tātparyārthasyāpy anādārād vyañjanānāṁ turīyā vṛttir aṅgī-kāryaiva.*

### Text 32

***tathā hi—***

***bhaṇio valla-a-va-iṇā, ajja sutta mahu-purīṁ gantā***  
***ia bhaṇiaṁ ajjāe, pia-i bahū savaṇa-puḍaeṇa***  
***(bhaṇito vallava-patinā adya suto madhu-purīṁ gantā***  
***iti bhaṇitam āryayā pibati vadhūḥ śravaṇa-puṭakena)***

*ity atra pibatīti padaṁ lākṣaṇikam, tena gṛha-śūnyatvam asyā abhilaṣitam ity asyārthasya vyañjakatvāt punar vyañjanāṁ vṛttim āśritya vyañjakaṁ bhavati.*

*ye tu “so ’yam iṣor iva dīrgha-dīrgho ’bhidhā-vyāpāraḥ” ity adhidadhati, ta evaṁ praṣṭavyāḥ—kiṁ bhavadbhir abhidhāyā dīrgha-dīrgha-vyāpāratvena lakṣaṇā-vyañjanayor eva khaṇḍanaṁ kriyate, kiṁ vyañjanāyā eva? ādyaś cet tadā “gaṅgāyāṁ ghoṣaḥ” ity atra gaṅgāyām ity atrānvayāyogāt mukhyārtha-bādhe ’bhidhaiva nāsti, tad-abhāvāt kathaṁ tasyā dīrgha-dīrghatvaṁ yena taṭo lakṣaṇīyaḥ? dvitīyaś cet tadā “pia-i bahū savaṇa-puḍaeṇa” ity atra vacanasya peyatva-rūpa-mukhyārtha-bādhe lākṣaṇikatayā laksaṇayā sādara-śravaṇa-rūpaṁ lakṣyam arthaṁ janayitvā pibatīti śabda upakṣīṇaḥ. gṛha-śūnyatvam asyā abhīṣṭam ity aparo ’rthaḥ kena pratyāyyatām? upakṣīṇatve tu śabda-buddhi-karmaṇāṁ viramya vyāpārābhāva-vādina eva sādhakāḥ.*

*nanv idam anumānenaiva sādhanīyam, kiṁ vyañjanayā? tathā hi—“ia bhaṇiaṁ ajjāe pia-i bahū savaṇa-puḍaeṇa” ity atra iyaṁ gopa-vadhūḥ patyur gṛhāntaritatvābihkāṅkṣiṇī śvaśrūkta-gṛha-pati-pravāsa-śravaṇe sābhilāṣatvāt. yā naivaṁ sā naivaṁ yathā tad-itareti kevala-vyatirekī-hetur iti cen na sābhilāṣatva-hetoḥ pramāṇāntarād aprāpter asiddhatvam, anumānāntarāt tat-prāptau prasādhyāṅgatvaṁ ca.*

*kiṁ ca patyur gṛhāntaritatvābhikāṅkṣitvam evāsyā na sādhyam, api tu gṛha-śūnyatve sati kṛṣṇo ’trābhisārya iti vastv eva. tat kuto labhyatām, punar anumānāntaraṁ kāryam, tathā hi iyaṁ sva-gṛhādhikaraṇaka-kṛṣṇābhisāra-kāṅkṣiṇī, śvaśrūkta-pati-pravāsa-sādara-śravaṇe sati patyur gṛhāntaritatvaṁ prati sābhilāṣatvād iti cen na, gṛha-śūnyatve sābhilāṣatvaṁ hetuḥ, kṛṣṇābhisārākāṅkṣitvaṁ sādhyaṁ prati naikāntikaḥ, tad-anyathāpi tat-sambhavāt. prakaraṇa-vaśād iti cet, pūrva-vad doṣāpattiḥ.*

### Text 33

***kiṁ ca—***

***hia-aṁ ccea-aṇacchaṁ, māṇaṁsiṇi ṇa uṇamade aṅgaṁ***  
***āliṅganti paāṇaṁ, ṇaharā paḍiviṁviaṁ kaṇhaṁ***  
***(hṛdayaṁ caivānagaccha, manasvini na punas te ’ṅgam***  
***āliṅanti padānāṁ, nakharāḥ pratibimbitaṁ kṛṣṇam)***

*ity atrāsyāḥ pāda-nakharā iti mānenāvṛtta-sarvāṅgatvaṁ nimīlita-nayanatvaṁ ca, anyathā caraṇopānta-gatasya kṛṣṇasya darśanāsahiṣṇutopapatteḥ. paścāt sakhī-vacasā sambhramaṁ pada-saṁvaraṇaṁ ca, tad anu ca mānasya śaithilyam, kṛṣṇasya ca praṇaya-ja-vinaya-māhātmyāc caraṇāntikam āgatasyāpi tat-sparśākṣamatvam, sakhyāś ca kṛṣṇa-pakṣa-pātitvam, sva-sakhī-māna-kṣaye sāgrahatvaṁ cety-ādīni vastūni ekayaiva vyañjanayā gamyante. bhavadbhir atra katy anumāna-prayogāḥ kartavyāḥ? tena lāghavād vyañjanā eva śreyasīti sthitam.*

\[OMISSION — Various arguments against *vyañjanā*\]

### Text 34

***artho ’pi vyañjako jñeyaḥ***

**It should be understood that meanings are also suggestive.79**

*artha iti jāty-apekṣayā vācya-lakṣya-vyaṅgyas traya evārthā gṛhyante.*

The word *arthaḥ* is singular here in relation to *jāti* (see HNV 624), but actually all three meanings, i.e the literal meaning, figurative meaning, and implied meaning are accepted.

### Text 35

***krameṇodāharaṇāni—***

Examples, according to the order above, are:

***ajje ghara-araṇijjaṁ, savvaṁ ṇitvāhidaṁ jevva***  
***eṇhiṁ sama-samaṇatthaṁ, ja-uṇāiṁ siṇāṇam adisadu***  
***(ārye gṛha-karaṇīyaṁ sarvaṁ nirvāhitam eva***  
***idānīṁ śrama-śamanārthaṁ yamunāyāṁ snānam ādiśatu****)*

*ārye—*O noble lady; *gṛha-karaṇīyam—*household duties; *sarvam—*all; *nirvāhitam eva—*are finished already; *idānīm—*now; *śrama-śamana-artham—*for the sake of relieving fatigue; *yamunāyām—*in the Yamunā; *snānam—*bathing; *ādiśatu—*please order.

**“O noble lady, I have finished all the household duties. Now please allow me to take bath in the Yamunā so I can relieve my fatigue.”**

*atra niścintāhaṁ yamunā-snāna-cchalena tat-taṭe khelantaṁ kṛṣṇam avalokya tatraiva viśramaṇaṁ karomīti vācyārthenaiva vyajyate.*

Here the following idea is suggested just by the literal meaning: “On the plea of taking bath in the Yamunā, I, without any anxiety, will be able to see Kṛṣṇa playing on the shore of the Yamunā and thus I will relax there.”80

*lakṣyasya yathā—“bhaṇio valla-a-va-iṇā” ity-ādau “pia-i bahū savaṇa-puḍaeṇa” ity atra ca śvaśrūkta-pati-pravāsa-sādarākarṇanaṁ lakṣyam, tena sva-gṛha-śūnyatve sati śrī-kṛṣṇo ’trābhisārya iti vyaṅgyam.*

An example of a suggestive figurative meaning is:

*bhaṇio valla-a-va-iṇā, ajja sutta mahu-purīṁ gantā*  
*ia bhaṇiaṁ ajjāe, pia-i bahū savaṇa-puḍaeṇa*  
*(bhaṇito vallava-patinā adya suto madhu-purīṁ gantā*  
*iti bhaṇitam āryayā pibati vadhūḥ śravaṇa-puṭakena)*

**“‘The king of the cowherds (Nanda) said that today my son (Abhimanyu) will go to Mathurā.’ The young bride (Rādhā) drank up this news, which was spoken by her elder (Jaṭilā), with the cups of her ears.”**

Here, when it says that the young bride (Rādhā) drank with the cups of her ears, the figurative meaning is that she raptly heard the news of her husband’s absence as described by her mother-in-law. Thus what is suggested is that when her house will be empty, she can meet Kṛṣṇa there.

### Text 36

***vyaṅgyasya yathā—***  
***idha vṛndāaṇa-majjhe, ṇīsaṅka-ṇisutta-mora-mia-ṇi-aro***  
***ali-metta-bhutta-kusumo, ramaṇijjo jāmuṇo kuñjo***  
***(iha vṛndāvana-madhye, niḥśaṅka-nisupta-mayūra-mṛga-nikaraḥ***  
***ali-mātra-bhukta-kusumo, ramaṇīyo yāmuna-kuñjaḥ)***

*atra nirjanatvaṁ vyaṅgyam, tena samucitam idam eva saṅketa-sthānam. he sakhi, tad atraiva kṛṣṇaḥ saṅgamanīya iti vyaṅgyāntaram.*

An example of a suggestive implied meaning is:

**“In this forest of Vṛndāvana the shore of the Yamunā, where the peacocks and deer sleep without any fear and the flowers are enjoyed only by the bees, is especially charming.”**

Here the implied meaning is the solitude. The further implication (suggestion) is: “This is a perfect place for a meeting. Therefore, O friend, we should meet Kṛṣṇa here.”

### Text 37-43

*…****nānārthānāṁ ca bhedakāḥ***  
***saṁyogādyāḥ***

**And union and so on limit words which have various meanings.**

*nānārthānāṁ śabdānāṁ niyamaṁ prati saṁyogādaya eva sahāyā bhedakā nirdhārakā bhavanti. ādi-śabdena viyogādayaś ca. tathā hi—*

I.e Union and so on are helpful limiters in regard to restricting words which have many meanings. The words *ādya* in *saṁyogādyāḥ* refers to separation and so on. For example, it is said:

*saṁyogaś ca viyogaś ca virodha-sahacāritā*  
*sānnidhyam anya-śabdasya deśaḥ sāmarthyam aucitī*  
*liṅgam arthaḥ prakaraṇaṁ kālo vyaktir imā diśaḥ*

**“Union, separation, hostility, accompaniment, juxtaposition of another word, place, power, congruity, characteristic, meaning, context, time, and grammatical gender—these give the direction.”**

*krameṇodāharaṇāni yathā—*

Examples, according to the order above, are:

*sa-kaustubho bhāti vidhuḥ śete vidhur akaustubhaḥ*

*saṁyoga-viyogau.*

Union: “Kṛṣṇa appears beautiful along with the Kaustubha jewel.”81

Separation: “Kṛṣṇa lies down without the Kaustubha jewel” 82

*rāmārjunau tathā karṇārjunau saha niyudhyataḥ*

*virodhaḥ.*

Hostility: “Paraśurāma and Kārtavīryārjuna fight with each other, as do Kaṛna and Arjuna.”83

*rādhā-mādhavayoḥ krīḍā madhu-mādhavayor dine*

*saha-cāritā.*

Accompaniment: “The pastimes of Rādhā and Mādhava occur in the days of Caitra and Vaiśākha.”84MAd12

*kṛṣṇasya muni-varyasya*

*anya-śabdasya sānnidhyam*

Juxtaposition of another word: “Of Kṛṣṇa Dvaipāyana Vyāsa, the best of *munis.*”85

*vraje ’sau parameśvaraḥ*

*deśaḥ. asau śrī-nando vraje rājety arthaḥ*

Place: “In Vraja, he is the supreme controller.”

The meaning is “In Vraja, Nanda Mahāraja is the king.”

*madhunā kokilo mattaḥ pramattā madhunā vadhūḥ*

*sāmarthyam aucitī ca.*

Power: “The cuckoo is maddened by spring.”

Congruity: “The girl is maddened by spring.”86

*utpadya hṛdaye tasyāḥ pīḍako makara-dhvajaḥ*

*liṅgam. kāmatve pīḍakatvam eva liṅgam.*

Characterisitic: “Arising in her heart, Cupid torments her.”

The characteristic here is tormenting through lust.87

*sthāṇuḥ kṛṣṇa-guṇāmodī*

*arthaḥ.*

Meaning: “Śiva delights in Kṛṣṇa’s qualities.”88

*devo jānāti me manaḥ*

*atra prakaraṇa-vaśād yuṣmadi.*

Context: “You know my mind.”

Due to the context the word *deva* is used in the sense of the word *yuṣmad.*MAd1389

*citra-bhānur vibhātīti dine ’rko niśi pāvakaḥ*

*kālaḥ.*

Time: If “Citra-bhānu is giving light” is spoken in the day, it refers to the sun. At night it refers to fire.”

*śāstre bhāgavataṁ bhāgavataḥ syād bhagavaj-jane*

*vyaktiḥ.*

Grammatical gender: *Bhāgavata* (neuter) refers to the book and *bhāgavata* (masculine) to the devotee of the Lord.

*eṣv abhidhā-lakṣaṇayor anavasaratvāt saṁyogāder eva vyañjakatā.*

Since there is no room for *abhidhā* and *lakṣaṇā* in these examples, it is only union and so on that suggest \[the appropriate meaning\].90

### Text 44

***yathā—***  
***adyāloki ghana-prabhaḥ sakhi mayā kaścid vihāra-krame***  
***lolat-keśara-mālikā-vilulita-grīvo hariḥ kānane***  
***yaḥ sadyastana-kumbhi-kumbha-nikara-kṣode nakha-bhraṁśibhir***  
***muktaughair dhavalī-karoti yamunā-tīre nikuñja-sthalīḥ***

\[Two interpretations\]

*adya—*today; *āloki—*was seen; *ghana-prabhaḥ—*whose effulgence was dense, or whose bodily complexion was like that of a cloud; *sakhi—*O friend; *mayā—*by me; *kaścit—*a certain; *vihāra-krame—*in the course of going, or in the course of sporting; *lolat—*which is swinging to and fro; *keśara—*of hairs on the shoulder, or of *nāga-keśara* flowers; *mālikā—*by a multitude, or by a garland; *vilulita—*is caressed; *grīvaḥ—*whose neck; *hariḥ—*a lion, or Kṛṣṇa; *kānane—*in the forest, or in Vṛndāvana; *yaḥ—*who; *sadyastana—*instantaneous; *kumbhi-kumbha—*of frontal lobes of the elephants, or of breasts which resemble the frontal lobes of elephants; *nikara—*the multitude; *kṣode—*at the time of tearing open; *nakha—*because of the nails; *bhraṁśibhiḥ—*which fall; *muktā—*of pearls; *oghaiḥ—*with the multitudes; *dhavalī-karoti—*makes white; *yamunā-tīre—*on the shore of the Yamunā; *nikuñja-sthalīḥ*—the forest-bowers.

**O friend, today, in the course of walking in the forest, I saw a lion. His effulgence was dense. His neck was caressed by a mane that was swinging to and fro. And he was making the** ***kuñjas*** **on the Yamunā’s shore white with the multitudes of pearls that instantly fell as he tore open the lobes of the elephants with his nails.91**

**O friend, today, in the course of sporting \[with my friends\] in Vṛndāvana, I saw Kṛṣṇa. His bodily complexion was like that of a cloud. His neck was caressed by a garland of** ***nāga-keśara*** **flowers that was swinging to and fro. And he was making the** ***kuñjas*** **on the Yamunā’s shore white with the multitudes of pearls that instantly fell \[from the broken pearl necklaces\] as he tore away at the breasts of the** ***gopīs.***

*atrānekārtha-śabdānām aneka-saṁsargasyaMAd14 vyañjakatā.*

For example, here the union of many things suggests the appropriate meaning of the words that have many meanings.92

*evam anukaraṇa-śabdānāṁ ca vyaṅgyaṁ prati vākyārtha eva vyañjakaḥ.*

Similarly, even the meaning of a sentence of imitative words (onomatopoeia) can suggest an implied meaning:

### Text 45

***yathā—***  
***āsāṁ rāsa-vilāsa-lāsya-laharīm āsvādya vādya-cchalāt***  
***tā dhik tā dhig iti prabhāṣya murajaḥ svar-nartakīr nindati***  
***te nā nā ga ri mā dha mā iti muhuḥ pāṭha-svaroccāraṇāt***  
***tāsāṁ hanta jugupsate bhagavatī vāṇī ca gāna-kramān***

*āsām—*of these girls (the *gopīs*); *rāsa-vilāsa—*in the *rāsa-līlā; lāsya—*of dancing; *laharīm—*the waves; *āsvādya—*relishing; *vādya-cchalāt—*on the pretext of being a musical instrument; *tā dhik tā dhik—*the beat *tā dhik tā dhik,* or the words “Fie on them. Fie on them” (*tāḥ dhik tāḥ dhik*); *iti*—thus; *prabhāṣya*—speaking; *murajaḥ—*the *muraja* drum (the drum played in the *rāsa-līlā*); *svar-nartakīḥ—*the dancers of heaven (the Apsarās); *nindati*—rebukes; *te nā nā ga ri mā dha mā—*the phrase *te nā nā* (which indicates the particular *rāga* being sung) and the notes *ga ri mā dha mā,* or the words *te nānāgarimādhamāḥ* (“They are lower on account of their many kinds of inferiority”); *iti—*thus; *muhuḥ*—repeatedly; *pāṭha-svara-uccāraṇāt—*because of articulating the phrase and the notes; *tāsām—*of those girls (the *gopīs*); *hanta—*ah!; *jugupsate—*rebukes; *bhagavatī*—goddess; *vāṇī—*Sarasvatī (speech personified); *ca—*and; *gāna-kramān—*the styles of singing \[of the Gandharvas\].

**Relishing the waves of the** ***gopīs’ rāsa*** **dancing, the** ***muraja*** **drum, on the plea of being a musical instrument, resounds with the beat** ***tā dhik tā dhik,*** **thus rebuking the dancers of heaven (i.e** ***tāḥ dhik tāḥ dhik*** **means “Fie on them. Fie on them”). And the goddess of speech, Sarasvatī, by repeating the** ***gopīs’*** **phrase, i.e** ***te nā nā,*** **and their notes, i.e** ***ga ri mā dha mā,*** **rebukes the singing styles of the Gandarvas (i.e** ***te nānāgarimādhamāḥ*** **means “They are lower on account of their many kinds of inferiority”).**

*atra te nā nā iti pāṭhaḥ. ga ri mā dha mā iti gāndhāra-ṛṣabha-madhyama-dhaivata-punar-madhyamā iti tāna-viśeṣa-svarāḥ, tābhyāṁ te nānā-vidho yo ’garimā tenādhamā ity artho vyajyate.*

Here *te nā nā* is the phrase, and *ga ri mā dha mā,* i.e *gāndhāra, ṛṣabha, madhyama, dhaivata,* and again *madhyama,* are the notes of a certain *tāna* (*ālāpa*). By articulating this phrase and these notes, the meaning that is suggested is: “They are lower because of \[their\] inferiority (*agarimā=nīcatvam*), which is of many kindsMAd15.”93

### Text 46-47

*…****athārthānāṁ vyañjakatvasya hetavaḥ***  
***arthānāṁ sāmānyatvena prāg-uktānāṁ vācyādīnāṁ vyañjakatve viśeṣa-hetava ucyante.***  
***boddhavya-vaktṛ-prakṛti-kāku-prakaraṇaiḥ saha***  
***deśa-kālādayaś cārthe vaiśiṣṭyād vyaṅgya-bodhakāḥ***

atra (prathama-kiraṇe 17-saṅkhyaka-padye) “ svayam eva” ity-ādau tad-ānayārthaṁ na gatāsi, api tūpabhogārtham iti boddhavya-vaiśiṣṭyam. yāṁ pratīyam uktiḥ sā boddhavyā yogyā, ayogyā ced bhavati tadādhvany artho na saṅgacchate. vaktṛ-vaiśiṣṭyaṁ prakṛti-vaiśiṣṭyaṁ prakaraṇa-vaiśiṣṭyaṁ cātraiva. vaktrī śrī-rādhā, sā ca sarva-śreṣṭhatva-rūpa-vaiśiṣṭyavatī. prakṛtiś ca tasyāḥ sakhīṁ prati snehāt śrī-kṛṣṇāṅga-saṅga-prāpaṇaṁ vyājena karotīti yat, sakhyāś ca prakṛtir nija-priya-sakhī-dūtyārthaṁ gatāyāḥ kṛṣṇena saha sambhogaḥ katham api na sambhaved iti tathaiva jñeyā. atas tasyā vaiśiṣṭyāt tathā-vidha-prakaraṇaṁ ca tatra mantavyam, tena tad-vaiśiṣṭyāc ca dvitīya-dhvani-pallavaḥ.

### Text 48

***kāku-vaiśiṣṭyaṁ yathā—***

***a-i jāhi jāhi vivinaṁ, riktaṁ ghettūṇa kusuma-bhāaṇaṁ su-muhi***  
***paccāamissasi tumaṁ, na kealaṁ bhāaṇeṇa puṇṇeṇa***  
***(ayi yāhi yāhi vipinaṁ, riktaṁ gṛhītvā kusuma-bhājanaṁ su-mukhi***  
***pratyāgamiṣyasi tvaṁ, na kevalaṁ bhājanena pūrṇena)***

***atra na kevalaṁ kusuma-bhājanenaiva pūrṇenāgamiṣyasi, api tu pūrṇena manorathenāpi, iti nañ kāku-dyotyam.***

### Text 49

***deśa-vaiśiṣṭyaṁ yathā—***

***ja-uṇā-sīara-sisirā, kamala-vaṇopa-aṇa-dhūa-kisala-aggā***  
***jaha vallī-ghara-pallī, dhaṇṇā pekkhanti taṁ desaṁ***  
***(yamunā-śīkara-śiśirā, kamala-vanopavana-dhūta-kisalayāgrā***  
***yatra vallī-gṛha-pallī, dhanyā prekṣante taṁ deśam)***

***atra deśa-vaiśiṣṭya-ślāghayā kṛṣṇena saha tatra māṁ ramayeti sakhīṁ prati kācit sva-manorathaṁ prakāśayati.***

### Text 50

***kāla-vaiśiṣṭyaṁ yathā—***

***eṇhiṁ jala-hara-samaye, ramaṇijjā ra-aṇa-valahītta***  
***ṇivaḍanta-vāri-dhārā, gahīratara-muhura-gabbha-kuharāo***  
***(idānīṁ jala-dhara-samaye, ramaṇīyā ratna-vallabhyaḥ***  
***nipatad-vāri-dhārā, gabhīratara-mukhara-garbha-kuharāḥ)***

### Text 51

***ādi-śabdāt prasiddhi-vaiśiṣṭyaṁ yathā—***

***kara-kiśalaya-līlāmbuja-nimīlanonmīlanāti-kutukinyā***  
***dakṣiṇam akṣi murāreḥ pidhīyate mucyate ca sindhu-jayā***  
***atra murārer dakṣiṇam akṣi prasiddhi-vaiśiṣṭyāt sūryātmakam iti vyajyate.***  
***iti śrīmad-alaṅkāra-kaustubhe śabdārtha-vṛtti-traya-nirūpaṇo nāma dvitīyaḥ kiraṇaḥ.***

## Tṛtīya-kiraṇaḥ (1-53)

## Atha dhvani-nirṇayaḥ

Now an analysis on the subject of *dhvani.*

### Text 1

*atha “dhvanir asavaḥ” iti kāvya-prakaraṇatvena nirūpitasya dhvaner bhedam ākhyātuṁ dhvani-śabdasya vyutpattim āha—*

In order to describe the varieties of *dhvani,* which was mentioned in the section on *kāvya* as “the *prāṇa* of poetry” (1.5), the author mentions the derivation of the word *dhvani.*

***śabdārthādibhir anyaiś ca dhvanyate ’sāv iti dhvaniḥ (1)***

***Dhvani*** **is that which is suggested (i.e conveyed by** ***vyañjanā vṛtti*****) by a word, its meanings (literal, figurative, and implied),** ***śleṣa,*** **and imitative words.**

*dhvananaṁ dhvaniḥ, dhvanyate ’neneti dhvaniḥ, dhvanyate ’sminn iti dhvanir iti bhāva-karaṇādhikaraṇa-sādhano ’pi dhvanir bhavati. tan-nirāsārthaṁ dhvanyate ’sāv iti karma-sādhana eveti pratijānīte.*

This derivation in *karmaṇi prayoga* of the word *dhvani* (“that which is suggested”) is put forward to reject other interpretations, namely: i) *dhvani* is the act of suggesting (*bhāve prayoga*); ii) *dhvani* is that by which the suggesting is done(*karaṇe prayoga*); and iii) *dhvani* is the place where the suggestion takes place (*adhikaraṇe prayoga*).

### Text 2

*tad eva kim ity āha—*

What is *dhvani*? In response to that, the author offers a definition:

***raso bhāvas tad-ābhāso vastv alaṅkāra eva ca***  
***bhāvānām udayaḥ śāntiḥ sandhiḥ śabalatā tathā***  
***sarvaṁ dhvanis taj-janitve kāvyaṁ ca dhvanir ucyate (2)***

**The following terms are synonyms of** ***dhvani: rasa*** **(the five kinds of relationships); emotion; semblance of** ***rasa;*** **semblance of an emotion;** ***vastu*****; literary ornament; arising of a** ***vyabhicāri-bhāva;*** **disappearance of a** ***vyabhicāri-bhāva;*** **mixing of similar or dissimilar** ***vyabhicāri-bhāvas,*** **and; friction of** ***vyabhicāri-bhāvas.*****Since it generates a suggestion,** ***kāvya*** **is also called** ***dhvani.***

*rasākhya-dhvaner anye dhvanayas tu prāṇāḥ, rasākhyas tu dhvanir ātmety adoṣaḥ. rasādayaḥ paścād varṇyante.*

The *dhvanis* other than *rasa* are the life airs, but the *dhvani* known as *rasa* is the soul \[of the *kāvya-puruṣa* (1.5)\]. In this way there is no fault in the statement. *Rasas, bhāvas,* and so on will be described in other chapters.

### Text 3-4

*samprati asu-bhūtānāṁ dhvanīnāṁ bhedā darśyante—*

Now, the varieties of *dhvanis* that are the *prāṇas* will be explained:

***ubhayor abhidhā-mūla-lakṣaṇā-mūlayos tayoḥ***  
***avivakṣita-vācyo ’ntyas tatra vācyaṁ dvidhā bhavet (3)***

**Out of** ***abhidhā-mūla-dhvani*** **(“a suggestion that has its roots in** ***abhidhā vṛtti*****”), and** ***lakṣaṇā-mūla-dhvani*** **(“a suggestion coming from** ***lakṣaṇā vṛtti*****”), the latter involves the absence of the desire to express the literal meaning. In that regard, the literal meaning is of two kinds.**

*tayor dhvanyor antyo lakṣaṇā-mūlo dhvanir avivakṣita-vācyaḥ syāt. tatrāvivakṣita-vācye dhvanau vācyaṁ dvi-vidhā bhavatīty arthaḥ. kiṁ tad dvaidham ity āha—*

\[This is an explanation of the verse:\] *Tayoḥ* means “of the two *dhvanis,*” *antyaḥ* refers to the *dhvani* whose roots are in *lakṣaṇā vṛtti.* In this kind of *dhvani,* there is no desire to express the literal meaning. The literal meaning is of two kinds:

***arthāntaropasaṅkrāntam atyantaṁ vā tiraskṛtam (4)***

**It transforms into another meaning, or else it’s obliterated.**

*evaṁ vācyam, ajahat-svārthatayā aparārthenopasaṅkrāntaṁ bhavati, anyaj jahat-svārthatayā sva-viparītenārthenākrāntaṁ bhavatīti dvaidham.*

This is the meaning of the sentence: either the literal meaning is changed to another meaning on account of being *ajahat-svārtha-lakṣaṇā,* or else it’s overcome by a meaning that is opposite to it on account of being *jahat-svārtha-lakṣaṇā.*

### Text 5

*krameṇodāharaṇāni—*

Now examples are given in order. \[This is an instance of *arthāntaropasaṅkrānta* (where the literal meaning is changed to another meaning):\]

***phalam api phalaṁ mākandānāṁ sitā api tāḥ sitā***  
***amṛtam amṛtaṁ drākṣā drākṣā madhūni madhūny api***  
***saha tulayituṁ tenaiteṣāṁ na kiñcana yujyate***  
***subala yad ayaṁ sāraṅgākṣyā bhavaty adharo ’dharaḥ***

*phalam—*fruit; *api—*even; *phalam—*fruit; *mākandānām—*of the mango tree; *sitāḥ—*candies; *api—*even; *tāḥ—*those well-known; *sitāḥ—*candies; *amṛtam—*nectar; *amṛtam—*nectar; *drākṣā—*grapes; *drākṣā—*grapes; *madhūni—*honey; *madhūni—*honey; *api—*even; *saha—*with; *tulayitum—* to equal; *tena—*(with) the lips; *eteṣām—*among these; *na kiñcana—*nothing; *yujyate—*is able; *subala—*O Subala; *yat—*therefore; *ayam—*these (lips); *sāraṅga—*are like an antelope’s; *akṣyāḥ—*of the girl whose eyes; *bhavati—*are; *adharaḥ—*lips; *adharaḥ—*which lessen (i.e belittle).

**O Subala! Mangos are mangos, candy is candy, nectar is nectar, grapes are grapes, and honey is honey. But nothing can compare with that doe-eyed girl’s lips, which belittle anything sweet.**

*atra dvitīya-phalādi-śabdā nindādy-artha-saṅkrāntāḥ. tathā hi—phalaṁ nānāvasthaṁ pāka eva kadācin madhuraṁ bhavati, tena tan nindyam eva. sitāḥ pāka-paunaḥ-punyenaiva nirmalā bhavanti, na tv ārambha eva. amṛtaṁ devair api pīyate. drākṣā pūrva-vad eva. madhūni saraghocchiṣṭāni. adharas tu adhara eva, sarvāṇy etāny adharayatīty adharaḥ. “saha tulayituṁ tenaiteṣāṁ na kiñcana yujyate” iti viśeṣa-vacanād upameya-dvitīya-pade stuty-artha eva vyaṅgyaḥ, na tūpamāna-dvitīya-pada-vad dheyāṁśatā.*

In this verse, the sense of the repeated words, such as the second *phalam,* is transformed into a meaning of “criticism” and so on. For example fruit, which has many stages, is only sometimes sweet when it’s ripe. In this way fruits are unreliable and thus contemptible.Sugar candy becomes pure only by repeatedly cooking it; it’s not pure in its raw state. Nectar is drunk by the demigods \[therefore it is also contemptible since the demigods are inferior to Śrīmatī Rādhikā\]. The case of the grapes follows the example of fruits in general. Honey is contemptible because it’s spit out by bees. But the lips *are* the lips (i.e there is no censure expressed by the second *adhara* toward the concept of *adhara*); the lips belittle all of these.

Because of the special statement, *saha tulayituṁ tenaiteṣāṁ na kiñcana yujyate:* “Nothing is able to be on a par with those lips,” the idea of praising the lips is suggested in the second word *adharaḥ,* unlike the case of the other repeated words in the verse, which have the sense of “rejection.”

### Text 6

*yathā vā—*

Another example of *arthāntaropasaṅkrānta:*

***premṇā vidrutam eka-vad yad ubhayos tan mānasaṁ mānasaṁ***  
***sarvāsv eva daśāsu yan nava-navaṁ tat sauhṛdaṁ sauhṛdam***  
***yat kṛṣṇasya vinoda-bhūr ahar ahas tad yauvanaṁ yauvanaṁ***  
***tad-viccheda-vidhau na yat-paricayas taj jīvanaṁ jīvanam***

*premṇā—*due to love; *vidrutam—*being melted; *eka-vat—*(appears) as if one; *yat—*which (heart); *ubhayoḥ—*of the two (lovers); *tat—*that; *mānasam—*heart; *mānasam—*real heart; *sarvāsu—*at every; *eva—*indeed; *daśāsu—*stage; *yat—*which (love); *nava-navam—*is ever fresh; *tat—*that; *sauhṛdam—*friendship (i.e love); *sauhṛdam—*real love; *yat—*which (youth); *kṛṣṇasya—*Kṛṣṇa’s; *vinoda—*of happiness; *bhūḥ—*the abode; *ahaḥ ahaḥ—*every day; *tat—*that *yauvanam—*youth; *yauvanam—*real youth; *tat—*from Kṛṣṇa; *viccheda—*of separation; *vidhau—*on the occasion; *na—*not; *yat—*of which (life); *paricayaḥ—*existence; *tat—*that; *jīvanam—*life; *jīvanam—*is real life.

**\[Agitated because Kṛṣṇa has gone to Mathurā, Śrī Rādhā says to Lalitā:\] That is a real heart when two lovers’ hearts melt as if one out of love. That is real love when the love between two persons is ever fresh in every situation. That is real youth when during each day of one’s youth Kṛṣṇa takes pleasure. That is real life when on the occasion of separation from Kṛṣṇa one ceases to live.**

### Text 7

*sva-viparītārthenākrāntaṁ yathā—*

Now an example of *atyanta tiraskṛta,* where the literal meaning is obliterated by an opposite meaning:

***saubhāgyam etad adhikaṁ mama nātha kṛṣṇa***  
***prāṇair mamātani sukhaṁ praṇayena kīrtiḥ***  
***dṛṣṭaś cirād asi kṛpāpi taveyam uccair***  
***na smaryate na bhavatātma-gṛhasya mārgaḥ***

*saubhāgyam—*good fortune; *etat—*this; *adhikam—*great; *mama—*my; *nātha—*O master (of my life airs); *kṛṣṇa—*O Kṛṣṇa; *prāṇaiḥ—*by the life airs; *mama—*my; *atani—*was increased; *sukham—*happiness; *praṇayena—*because of your love for me; *kīrtiḥ—*fame; *dṛṣṭaḥ—*have been seen; *cirād—*after a long time; *asi—*you; *kṛpā—*mercy; *api—*also; *tava—*your; *iyam—*this; *uccair—*great; *na smaryate—*not remembered; *na—*not; *bhavatā—*by you; *ātma-gṛhasya—*of my house; *mārgaḥ—*the path.

**\[A** ***gopī,*** **angry at Kṛṣṇa for being late to meet her at her house, speaks with irony:\] O Kṛṣṇa, my love! Your arrival is my great good fortune! My life airs have increased my happiness, and my prestige has expanded because of Your love for me. I can see You now after so long because of Your great mercy. It’s not that You have forgotten the way to your house.**

*atra saubhāgyam ity asaubhāgyam, sukham iti duḥkham, kīrtir akīrtiḥ, kṛpākṛpā, ātma-gṛhasyeti para-gṛhasyeti, sva-viparītenākrāntam.*

In this verse, good fortune means misfortune, happiness means misery, fame means infamy, mercy means cruelty, and “my house” means “another’s house.” In this way, the literal meaning is overcome by the opposite meaning.

### Text 8-10

***dhvanir yas tv abhidhā-mūlas tatra vācyaṁ vivakṣitam (4)***

**But there is a desire to express the literal meaning when the suggestionarises from** ***abhidhā.***

***tathāpi vyaṅgya-niṣṭhaṁ syāt sa ca dvai-vidhyam ṛcchati***  
***ko ’pi lakṣya-krama-vyaṅgyo ’lakṣya-vyaṅgya-kramo ’paraḥ (5)***

*tathā api —*still; *vyaṅgya—*in a suggested meaning; *niṣṭham—*which culminates; *syāt—*is; *saḥ—*that \[*abhidhā-mūla-dhvani*\]; *ca—*and; *dvai-vidhyam—*the state of being twofold; *ṛcchati—*attains; *kaḥ api—*one of them; *lakṣya—*that is perceivable; *krama—*has a sequence of appearance and disappearance; *vyaṅgyaḥ—*is one in which the suggested meaning; *alakṣya—*is not perceivable; *vyaṅgya—*of the suggested meaning; *kramaḥ—*is one where the sequence of appearance and disappearance; *aparaḥ—*the other one.

**Still that** ***abhidhā-mūla-dhvani,*** **which culminates in a suggestion, is twofold: i)** ***lakṣya-krama-vyaṅgya,*** **and ii)** ***alakṣya-vyaṅgya-krama.***

*abhidhā-mūla-dhvanau tu vivakṣitam api vācyaṁ vyaṅgya-niṣṭham. sa ca dhvanir lakṣya-krama-vyaṅgyo ’lakṣya-vyaṅgya-kramaś ceti dvi-vidhaḥ. lakṣyaṁ krama-prāptaṁ vyaṅgyaṁ yatra sa tathā, alakṣyo vyaṅgyasya kramo yatra sa tathā. kramas tu vibhāvādibhir vyajyamāna eva rasaḥ, na tu vibhāvādaya iti. lāghavāc chatapatra-patra-śatī-yugapad-vedhābhimāna-vad yatra kramo na lakṣyate, sa tāvad akramaḥ.*

However in *abhidhā-mūla-dhvani,* the literal meaning, which is desired to be expressed, will also lead to a suggestion. And that *dhvani* is of two kinds: i) *lakṣya-krama-vyaṅgya,* and ii) *alakṣya-vyaṅgya-krama.*The first kind is where the suggestion, which is obtained by the *krama* (“the sequence of appearance and disappearance”), is perceivable.

The second kind is where the *krama* of the suggestion isn’t perceivable.In this second variety, the *krama* is the *rasa,* which is suggested only by *vibhāva* and so on. The *rasa* isn’t the *vibhāva* and so on themselves.That in which the sequence of things is not perceived is called *akrama.* It is just like how one considers the piercing of one hundred lotus petals with a needle to be simultaneous because of quickness.

***raso bhāvas tad-ābhāso bhāva-śānty-ādir akramaḥ (6)***

***Akrama*** **consists of** ***rasa;*** **emotion; semblance of** ***rasa;*** **semblance of emotion; arising of a** ***vyabhicāri-bhāva;*** **disappearance of a** ***vyabhicāri-bhāva;*** **mixing of similar or dissimilar** ***vyabhicāri-bhāvas;*** **and friction of** ***vyabhicāri-bhāvas.***

*rasādayas tv akramo ’lakṣya-vyaṅgyaś ca ity arthaḥ. ādi-śabdād bhāvodaya-bhāva-śābalya-bhāva-sandhayaḥ. ayam alakṣya-vyaṅgya-kramo rasa-nirūpaṇe darśayiṣyate.*

All of these are *akrama,* and what is suggested is not perceived by everyone.The word *ādi* refers to *bhāvodaya* (the arising of a *vyabhic ri-bh va*), *bhāva-śābalya* (the friction among *vyabhic ri-bh vas*), and *bhāva-sandhi* (the mixing of *vyabhic ri-bh vas*), This kind of *abhidhā-mūla-dhvani,* which is called *alakṣya-vyaṅgya-krama,* will be described later in the context of an analysis of *rasa.*

### Text 11

***yatrānudhvaninā vyaṅgyaṁ lakṣyate krama-pūrvakam***  
***sa tu lakṣya-krama-vyaṅgyaḥ śabdārthobhaya-śakti-bhūḥ (7)***

*yatra—*where; *anudhvaninā—*by means of an *anudhvani; vyaṅgyam—*what is suggested; *lakṣyate—*is perceived; *krama-pūrvakam—*by means of a *krama; saḥ—*that; *tu—*but; *lakṣya-krama-vyaṅgyaḥ—*where the suggested meaning has a sequence of rising and disappearance that is perceived by all; *śabda—*of the word; *artha—*of the meaning; *ubhaya—*or of both the word and its meaning; *śakti­*—from the potency; *bhūḥ—*arises.

***Lakṣya-krama-vyaṅgya,*** **the first kind of** ***abhidhā-mūla-dhvani,*** **is where what is suggested by means of** ***krama*** **is perceived by means of an** ***anudhvani*** **(a continuity of the sound or an echo). That** ***lakṣya-krama-vyaṅgya*** **arises: i) from** ***śabda-śakti*** **(the potency of the word), ii) from** ***artha-śakti*** **(the potency of the meaning), or iii) from** ***śabda-artha-ubhaya-śakti*** **(the potency of both the word and its meaning).**

*anudhvanir anuraṇanaṁ, dhvaner eva dīrgha-dīrgha-bhāvaḥ, pratidhvanir iva vā. sa tu lakṣya-krama-vyaṅgyaḥ śabda-śakti-bhūḥ, artha-śakti-bhūḥ, śabdārthobhaya-śakti-bhūś ceti tridhā.*

*Anudhvani* means *anuraṇana,* which means either: i) the elongation of a sound (i.e continually making a sound, like constantly ringing a bell), and ii) an echo. That *lakṣya-krama-vyaṅgya* arises: i) from the potency of the word, ii) from the potency of the meaning, or iii) from the potency of both the word and its meaning. Therefore it is threefold.

### Text 12

***ādyo dvidhaivālaṅkāra-vastunor dyotanād bhavet (8)***

**The first variety (i.e** ***śabda-śakti-bhū*****) is of two kinds on account of suggesting an** ***alaṅkāra*** **or suggesting a** ***vastu.***

*ādyaḥ śabda-śakti-bhūr dvidhaiva bhavet. eva-kāras tu artha-śakti-bhava-vat śabda-śakti-bhavo ’pi dvādaśadheti, para-mataṁ vyāvartayati, athavālaṅkārasyaiva, vastuna eva, nānyatara-viśiṣṭasyaivety arthaḥ.*

Here *ādya* refers to *śabda-śakti-bhū,* which is of two kinds. The use of the word *eva* is for the purpose of rejecting the opinion of others that *śabda-śakti-bhū* is of twelve kinds just like *artha-śakti-bhū.* Or else, the word *eva* may be connected with the words *alaṅkāra* and *vastu* separately, meaning “because of suggesting just an *alaṅkāra* or just a *vastu,*” that is to say “not an *alaṅkāra* or a *vastu* that are characterized by something else.”

### Text 13

*atrālaṅkāra-dyotakaḥ śabda-śakti-bhūr yathā—*

Now an example of an *abhidhā-mūla-dhvani* that is *lakṣya-krama-vyaṅgya,* where the suggestion arises from the potency of the word and where whatis suggested is an *alaṅkāra:*

***āśā-mātre vilasad-udayaḥ padminī-cakra-bandhuḥ***  
***siddhābhogaḥ satatam aniśāmoda-maitrī-kaṣāyaḥ***  
***rādhāśleṣādiṣu niravadhi-vyāpṛtaḥ śoṇa-pādo***  
***rociḥ-pūrair haratu bhajatāṁ śītatāṁ kṛṣṇa-candraḥ***

*āśā-mātre—*when there is mere desire (or, in any direction \[i.e at any time, for those who worship Him\]), (or, \[the moon arises\] merely in one direction); *vilasat—*splendidly; *udayaḥ—*whose arising; *padminī—*of beautiful women (*padminīs* are one of four types of women), (or, lotuses); *cakra—*of multitudes; *bandhuḥ—*who is the friend; *siddha—*is accomplished; *ābhogaḥ—*whose enjoyment, or fullness; *satatam—*always; *aniśa—*is continuous, or *aniśā:* at times other than night; *āmoda—*and bliss; *maitrī—*friendship; *kaṣāyaḥ—*whose sweetness; *rādhā-śleṣa—*embracing Rādhā; *ādiṣu—*in activities such as, (or, *rādhā-āśleṣā-ādiṣu:* toward Rādhā, Āśleṣā, and other constellations ); *niravadhi—*always; *vyāpṛtaḥ—*engaged; *śoṇa—*are reddish, indicating softness; *pādaḥ—*whose feet (or rays); *rociḥ—*of beauty (or radiance); *pūraiḥ—*with its flood; *haratu—* may he take away; *bhajatām—*for those who worship; *śītatām—*dullness (or coolness); *kṛṣṇa-candraḥ—*the Kṛṣṇa moon.

**May the Kṛṣṇa moon, by its flood of effulgence, take away dullness for those who worship Him \[whereas the moon only gives coolness\]. He is the friend of beautiful women \[whereas the moon is not the friend of lotuses\]. Kṛṣṇa has soft reddish feet \[whereas the moon’s rays are white\]. He appears splendidly whenever His devotees desire Him \[whereas the moon only arises in the eastern direction\]. He is always enjoying \[whereas the moon is full only once a month\]. His bliss, friendship, and sweetness are perpetual \[whereas the moon’s occur only at night\]. The Kṛṣṇa moon is always embracing Rādhā \[whereas the moon is only periodically connected with the the constellations such as** ***rādhā*** **and** ***āśleṣā*****\].**

*atra śabda-śakty-udbhutaś candra iti prasiddha-candrād vyatiriktair akhilair eva guṇair vyatirekālaṅkāro dhvanitaḥ. yady apy asau dhvanitvenālaṅkārya eva, tathāpi brāhmaṇa-śramaṇa-nyāyenālaṅkāra eva. evam anye ’pi.*

In this verse, the *vyatireka-alaṅkāra* (ornament of contrast) arises by the power of the word *candra* since all the qualities mentioned are different than the famous moon’s. Even though this contrast is simply the basis of the ornament on account of being a *dhvani,* still the *dhvani* itself is called an *alaṅkāra* in accordance with the logic in the statement “*brāhmaṇa-śramaṇa.*”This should be understood in other cases also.

### Text 14

\[Now an example of an *abhidhā-mūla-dhvani* that is *lakṣya-krama-vyaṅgya,* where the suggestion arises by the potency of the word and where what is suggested is a *vastu.*\]

***dadhatī sa-madhupa-rāgaṁ parimalam ekānta-sukumārā***  
***guṇa-kalitā lalite mama bhūṣita-kaṇṭhā tvam eva vana-mālā***

*dadhatī—*possessing); *sa-madhupa—*for the bees (or, \[for Me\] who am drinking the nectar \[of your lips\]); *rāgam—*\[the fragrance\] which is accompanied by affection, (or, \[erotic bliss\] accompanied by love); *parimalam—*the fragrance (or erotic bliss); *ekānta—*totally; *su-kumārā—*very fresh (or very soft); *guṇa—*with a thread (or with qualities); *kalitā—*endowed; *lalite—*O Lalitā; *mama—*My; *bhūṣita-kaṇṭhā—*by whom the neck is ornamented; *tvam—*you; *eva—*only; *vana-mālā—*are a garland of sylvan flowers.

**\[Kṛṣṇa speaks to Lalitā:\]**

\[First interpretation:\]

**O Lalitā! My garland of forest flowers has a thread and is extremely fresh and soft. It possesses a fragrance that attracts the bees. Only you are that garland ornamenting My neck.**

\[Second interpretation:\]

**O Lalitā! Only you are the garland of forest flowers decorating My neck. You have many qualities and your body is very soft. You possess erotic bliss endowed with love for Me who am the drinker of the nectar of your lips.**

*atra śabda-śaktyā kaver vana-mālā-lalitayoḥ sādharmyaṁ nirūpya prayāse vyuparate sati vastu-bhūtaḥ kaścid arthaḥ sphurati. sa ca tvam eva vana-mālā, nānyeti, tvāṁ praty eva me samādaraḥ, na tu tasyām, tena tvad-āliṅganam eva me preya iti vastu.*

When the poet’s endeavor has stopped after describing the similarities between the garland and Lalitā, then by the *śabda-śakti* a certain meaning manifests. That suggested meaning is a *vastu.* The *vastu* (“the idea suggested”) is: “You alone are My garland, not any other girl. I have regard for you alone, not toward any other girl. Therefore I only want your embrace.”

### Text 15

***artha-śakty-udbhavo ’rthas tu vyañjakaḥ svayam-udbhavī***  
***kaveḥ prauḍhokti-niṣpanno vaktus tat-kalpitasya ca (9)***

*artha—*of the meaning; *śakti—*from the power; *udbhavaḥ—*arising; *arthaḥ—*the meaning; *tu—*but; *vyañjakaḥ—*suggestive; *svayam—*by itself; *udbhavī—*arising; *kaveḥ—*of the poet; *prauḍha—*bold (i.e uncommon); *ukti—*words; *niṣpannaḥ—*effected by; *vaktuḥ—*of the speaker; *tat—*him (the poet); *kalpitasya—*who is invented by; *ca—*and, (drags in the words *prauḍhokti-niṣpannaḥ* from the second kind into the third kind also).

**But the suggestive meaning that arises from** ***artha-śakti*** **(“power of the meaning”) is of three kinds: i)** ***svayam-udbhavī*** **(“it arises naturally”), ii)** ***kaveḥ prauḍhokti-niṣpanna*** **(“it is created by the uncommon words of the poet”), and iii)** ***vaktus tat-kalpitasya prauḍhokti-niṣpanna*** **(“it is created by the uncommon words of the speaker who is invented by the poet”).**

*artha-śakty-udbhavo yo dhvaniḥ, sa tridhā bhavati—svataḥ-sambhavī, kavi-prauḍhokti-niṣpanna-śarīraḥ, tan-nibaddha-vaktṛ-prauḍhokti-niṣpanna-śarīraś ceti. tatra svataḥ-sambhavī na kevalaṁ kavi-bhaṇita-mātra-niṣpannaḥ, vyavahāre ’pi samucitatvena sambhāvyamānaḥ.*

The *dhvani* that arises from the power of the meaning is of three kinds: i) *svataḥ-sambhavī* (“manifesting of its own accord”), ii) *kavi-prauḍhokti-niṣpanna-śarīra* (“\[the *dhvani*\] whose body (i.e meaningbody) is created by the uncommon words of the poet”), and iii) *tan-nibaddha-vaktṛ-prauḍhokti-niṣpanna-śarīra* (“created by the uncommon words of the speaker who is invented by the poet”).

Among those three, *svataḥ-sambhavī* is not only manufactured by the mere words of the poet. It’s also considered proper even in common usage (i.e day-to-day life).

### Text 16-17

*anyau kavinā tan-nibaddha-vaktrā ca pratibhāna-mātreṇa vyavahārāsiddhāv api nirmitau.*

Even though the other two kinds (i.e *kavi-prauḍhokti-niṣpanna,* and *tan-nibaddha-vaktṛ-prauḍhokti-niṣpanna*) aren’t found in practical life, they are manufactured by the poet or by a character invented by the poet.

***vastutvālaṅkṛtatvābhyāṁ te dvai-vidhyena ṣaṭ smṛtāḥ (10)***

*vastutvā-alaṅkṛtatvābhyām—*because of being a *vastu* or being an *alaṅkāra; te—*those \[three varieties\]; *dvai-vidhyena—*on account of being twofold; *ṣaṭ—*six; *smṛtāḥ—*are considered.

**These three varieties are considered as six on since the suggestion can either be a** ***vastu*** **or an** ***alaṅkāra.***

*tatra yo bhedā, vastu-rūpatayā, alaṅkāra-rūpatayā ca ṣaṭ prakārāḥ syuḥ.*

The varieties of *artha-śakti-bhū dhvani* become six-fold since the suggestion can either come in the form of a *vastu* (idea) or an *alaṅkāra* (ornament).

***vastunā vastv-alaṅkārāv alaṅkāreṇa teṣu cet***  
***vyajyete ’py alaṅkāra-vastunī dvādaśāpi tat (11)***

***vastunā vastv-alaṅkārāv alaṅkāreṇa teṣu cet***  
***vyajyete apy alaṅkāra-vastunī dvādaśāpi tat***

*teṣu ṣaḍ-vidheṣu ced yadi vastunā vastu cālaṅkāraś ca vyajyate, alaṅkāreṇa alaṅkāro vastu ca vyajyate, tat—tadā dvādaśāpi bhavantīty arthaḥ.*

In this verse the word *cet* means “*yadi*” and the word *tat* means “*tadā.*” If among those six varieties, a *vastu* or an *alaṅkāra* is suggested by a *vastu,* or if a *vastu* or an *alaṅkāra* is suggested by an *alaṅkāra,* then those six kinds become twelve kinds.

### Text 18

*krameṇodāharaṇāni—*

The examples are presented in order. \[This verse is an instance of an *artha-śakti-bhū dhvani* that is *svayam-udbhāvī,* where the suggestion is a *vastu* that gives rise to another *vastu.*\]

***ga-ula-mahinda-ṇandaṇa suṇṇa-ghare ettha mā pavisa***  
***ujja sahīe sāmī gomī dūraṁ gao goṭṭham***  
***(gokula-mahendra-nandana śūnya-gṛhe ’tra mā praviśa***  
***adya sakhyāḥ svāmī gomān dūraṁ gato goṣṭham)***

*gokula—*of Gokula; *mahā-indra—*of the king; *nandana—*O son; *śūnya-gṛhe—*empty house; *atra—*in this; *mā—*don’t; *praviśa—*enter; *adya—*today; *sakhyāḥ—*of my friend; *svāmī—*the husband; *gomān—*who possesses many cows; *dūram—*far; *gataḥ—*has gone; *goṣṭham—*to the pasturing grounds.

**O Son of the king of Gokula! Don’t enter in this house. Today my friend’s husband, who possesses many cows, has gone far away to the pasturing grounds.**

*atra svāmī, na tu priyaḥ. gomī bahvīnāṁ gavāṁ patiḥ, tena śīghraṁ nāyāsyati. dūram, na tu nedīyaḥ, ataś ciraṁ vyāpyāsyā gṛhaṁ śūnyaṁ bhāvi. tenātra niḥśaṅkam eva praviśya vilasyatām iti vastu.*

In this verse,the word *svāmī* (literally: owner or master) implies that he is not dear. *Gomān* means that he is the master of many cows, so he won’t be able to return quickly. *Dūram* signifies that he’s not near. Therefore the house of this *sakhī* will be empty for a long time (this is the first *vastu*).Because of that, the lover can fearlessly enter the house and enjoy (this is the second *vastu*).

### Text 19

\[This verse is an instance of an *artha-śakti-bhū dhvani* that is *svayam-udbhavī,* where the suggestion is a *vastu* that gives rise to an *alaṅkāra.*\]

***śrūyate parimale mala-śabdo***  
***mekhalādiṣu khalādy-abhiyogaḥ***  
***candanādi-rasa eva hi paṅko***  
***nīvi-keśa-raśanādiṣu bandhaḥ***

*śrūyate—*is heard; *parimale—*\[in the word\] *parimala* (fragrance, or erotic bliss); *mala-śabdaḥ—*the word *mala* (dirt, impurity); *mekhala—*in the word *mekhala* (belt); *ādiṣu—*and so on; *khala—*of the word *khala* (wicked man); *ādi—*and so on; *abhiyogaḥ—*the usage; *candana—*\[such as\] sandalwood paste; *ādi—*and so on; *rase—*in reference to ointments; *eva—*only; *hi—*but; *paṅkaḥ—*\[the word\] *paṅka* (dirt, mud); *nīvi—*petticoat; *keśa—*hair; *raśanā—*rope; *ādiṣu—*in reference to \[…\] and so on; *bandhaḥ—*\[the word\] *bandha* (“bondage”).

**In Vraja, the word** ***mala*** **is only heard in the word** ***parimala,*** **and the usage of the words** ***khala*** **and so on is only heard in connection with the words** ***mekhala*** **and so on. Similarly, the word** ***paṅka*** **is only used in reference to ointments such as sandalwood paste, and the word** ***bandha*** **is only heard in reference to items such as a petticoat, hair, rope, and so on.**

*atra kaver aprayāsa-racana-balād vraja-loke malādy-abhāvo vastu-bhūto ’rthaḥ svataḥ-sambhavī, tena svabhāvokty-alaṅkāra iti dvedhā.*

In this verse the meaning, namely the absence of impurities and so on in Vraja, is *svataḥ-sambhavī* (i.e arising of its own accord) because the poet didn’t have to make much of an effort composing. This suggestion, which is a *vastu* (i.e an idea), gives rise to another suggestion, which is an *alaṅkāra* called *svabhāvokti* (“words describing the special characteristic of a thing (or in this case a place)”). Thus two kinds of *svataḥ-sambhavī* have been explained so far.

### Text 20

\[The first half of this verse is an instance of an *artha-śakti-bhū dhvani* that is *svayam-udbhavī,* where the suggestion is an *alaṅkāra* that gives rise to a *vastu,* and the second half of this verse is an instance of an *artha-śakti-bhū dhvani* that is *svayam-udbhavī,* where the suggestion is an *alaṅkāra* that gives rise to another *alaṅkāra.*\]

***gañjanān na hi bibheṣi gurūṇāṁ***  
***khañjanākṣi yamunām adhunāgāḥ***  
***añjanābha iha kuñjara ekaḥ***  
***kañja-nāla-dala-bhañjana-kārī***

*gañjanāt—*(from) the chastisement; *na—*don’t; *hi—*certainly; *bibheṣi—*you fear; *gurūṇām—*of the superiors; *khañjana-akṣi—*O you whose eyes move back and forth like the tail of a wagtail bird; *yamunām—*to the Yamunā; *adhunā—*right now; *agāḥ—*you have gone; *añjana—*\[is like the color of\] eyeliner; *ābhaḥ—*whose bodily complexion; *iha—*in this place; *kuñjaraḥ—*elephant; *ekaḥ—*one; *kañja-nāla—*lotus; *dala—*petals; *bhañjana-kārī—*whose habit is to damage.

**O wagtail-eyed girl! You don’t fear the chastisement of your elders. Now you have gone to the Yamunā. In that place there’s an elephant the color of eyeliner whose habit is to crush lotus petals.**

*atra sakhīṁ prati sakhī vadati. kañjanākṣīti sambodhana-maryādayā sakhī-rūpo vaktā. adhuneti akāle ’pi yad agās tena snātuṁ nāgāḥ, kṛṣṇa-saṅgāyaivāgā iti kāvya-liṅgālaṅkāras tena tvaṁ gurūṇāṁ gañjanān na bibheṣīti guru-gañjane tava bhayaṁ nāsti, yathā kṛṣṇāṅga-saṅga-virahe iti vastu dhvanyate. evaṁ kuñjara iti kṛṣṇa-nāmāpahnavenāpahnuty-alaṅkāreṇa kṛṣṇa-kuñjarayoḥ sādṛśya-vyañjanād upamālaṅkāraś ceti, svataḥ-sambhavinaḥ śloka-trayeṇa cātur-vidhyam.*

In this verse, one *sakhī* speaks to another friend. We can infer that the speaker is a female friend because the word *kañjanākṣi* is a particular customary *sambodhana.* The word *adhunā* (“\[you went\] now”) creates the following suggestion: “The fact that you have gone at an inappropriate time means that you didn’t go for bathing; you went for the purpose of meeting Kṛṣṇa.” In this way there’s an *alaṅkāra* called *kāvya-liṅga* (an ornament of meaning where a statement, i.e *yamunām adhunā agāḥ* is clarified by giving a reason or a purpose).By the statement “you don’t fear the chastisement of your elders,” a *vastu* is suggested, which is the idea that in separation from the touch of Kṛṣṇa’s body, you have no fear of their rebuke.

Similarly there is an *alaṅkāra* called *apahnuti* (concealment) since Kṛṣṇa’s name is concealed and only the word *kuñjara* (elephant) is used. Thus from the *apahnuti alaṅkāra* arises the *alaṅkāra* called *upamā* (simile) because of the suggestion of similarity between Kṛṣṇa and an elephant. Thus the four kinds of *svataḥ-sambhavī* have been explained through these three verses.

### Text 21

*kavi-prauḍhokteś catur-vidhyaṁ yathā—*

The following verses are examples of the four kinds of *artha-śakti-bhū dhvani* that is *kavi-prauḍhokti* (created by the uncommonuncommon words of the poet).

\[In the first half of this verse the suggestion is a *vastu* that gives rise to another *vastu,* and in the second half of this verse the suggestion is an *alaṅkāra* that gives rise to another *alaṅkāra.*\]

***spandate yadi padādi tadāsāṁ***  
***syandate madhurimāmṛta-dhārā***  
***saṅgataḥ pavana-jād vratatīnām***  
***aṅgato madhu-kaṇā iva bhūmau***

*spandate—*moves; *yadi—*if; *pada-ādi—*a limb such as a foot; *tadā—*then; *āsām—*of these \[*gopīs*\]; *syandate—*rains down; *madhurimā—*of sweetness; *amṛta-dhārā—*a nectarean shower; *saṅgataḥ—*because of the contact; *pavana-jāt—*which manifests because of the wind; *vratatīnām—*of the vines; *aṅgataḥ—*from a part; *madhu-kaṇāḥ—*drops of nectar; *iva—*just as; *bhūmau—*on the ground.

**Just as drops of nectar fall on the ground from blossoming vines because the wind makes them shake, similarly a nectarean shower of sweetness rains down when the** ***gopīs*** **move their feet.**

*atra kavi-prauḍhoktir eva, na pūrvavat svataḥ-sambhavī sa cārthaḥ. yāsāṁ padādi-spandana-mātreṇāpy evaṁ mādhurya-rasa-pratipattir ity aho āsāṁ lokottarateti vastu-bhūto ’rthaḥ. sa ca tāsāṁ rāsādi-nṛtya-vidhau vā kīdṛśa iti vastu vyanaktīti vastunā vastu. uttarārdhe vratatīnāṁ pavana-jāt saṅgāt madhu-kaṇā iveti tāsām api vratatibhir upamety upamālaṅkāreṇa svabhāvokty-alaṅkāro dhvanita iti dvedhā.*

In this verse, the suggestion also arises from the meaning, but the meaning is created by the uncommonuncommon words of a poet, it is not naturally occuring as described before.

Since just by the mere movements of their feet the *gopīs* create such *mādhurya-rasa,* there is a meaning, which is a *vastu,* that these *gopīs* are out of this world. “So how much impressive must that sweetness be while they dance in the *rāsa-līlā*!” In this way a *vastu* gives rise to another *vastu.*

In the second half of the verse, since the shower of sweetness caused by the movement of the gopīs’ feet is compared to drops of nectar caused by the vines’ contacting each other due to the wind. This suggests a comparison between *gopīs* and the vines and thus there is the *alaṅkāra* called *upamā* (simile). This *alaṅkāra* further gives rise to the *alaṅkāra* called *svabhāvokti* (description of the special characteristic of a thing).

Thus two kinds of *kavi-prauḍhokti* have been explained so far.

### Text 22

\[This verse is an instance of an *artha-śakti-bhū dhvani* that is *kavi-prauḍhokti,* where the suggestion is a *vastu* that gives rise to an *alaṅkāra.*\]

***gokule kulaja-bāla-vadhūnāṁ***  
***śyāma-dhāmani manoratha-bhājām***  
***nojjagāma na jagāma virāmaṁ***  
***sauhṛdaṁ hṛdaya eva jughūrṇe***

*gokule—*in Gokula; *kula-ja—*born in respectable families; *bāla—*young; *vadhūnām—*of the girls; *śyāma—*is dark blue; *dhāmani—*toward Him whose bodily complexion; *manoratha—*ardent desires; *bhājām—*of those who possess; *na ujjagāma—*didn’t arise; *na jagāma—*didn’t attain; *virāmam—*cessation; *sauhṛdam—*love; *hṛdaye—*in the heart; *eva—*only; *jughūrṇe—*bounced around.

**In Gokula, the love of the respectable young girls didn’t arise toward that blackish fellow, nor did it just cease. Rather it kept bouncing around in their hearts.**

*atra kavi-prauḍhoktiḥ. nojjagāmeti lajjādhikyaṁ vastu, na jagāma virāmam iti hṛdaya-kṣobhātiśayo ghūrṇatvenotprekṣita iti utprekṣālaṅkāraś ca.*

In this verse we see the uncommon words of a poet \[because love cannot perform physical actions since it is not a physical substance\]. The fact that love didn’t arise suggests the idea (*vastu*) of excessive shyness. Moreover, the fact that their affection didn’t stop suggests an extreme agitation of the heart, which is imagined to be moving around wildly. This is the *alaṅkāra* called *utprekṣā* (“fancifully imagining one thing to be another”).

### Text 23

\[This verse is an instance of an *artha-śakti-bhū dhvani* that is *kavi-prauḍhokti,* where the suggestion is an *alaṅkāra* that gives rise to a *vastu.*\]

***stumaḥ kiṁ tvām ambhodhara-subhaga śambhor adhi-śiraḥ***  
***padāmbhojasyāmbho yad akṛta padaṁ bhos tava tataḥ***  
***umāyai dattvārdhaṁ vapur apadam ardhaṁ ca bhavate***  
***guṇebhyo nirmuktaḥ sa param abhavad brahma parama***

*stumaḥ—*we praise; *kim—*whether; *tvām—*You; *ambhaḥ-dhara—*like a cloud (“bearing water”); *subhaga—*O You who are beautiful; *śambhuḥ—*of Śambhu; *adhi-śiraḥ—*on the head; *pada ambhojasya—*of His lotus feet; *ambhaḥ—*the water (i.e Gaṅgā); *yat—*because; *akṛta—*was made; *padam—*an abode; *bhoḥ—*O; *tava—*Your; *tataḥ—*therefore; *umāyai—*unto Umā; *dattvā—*having given; *ardham—*half; *vapuḥ—*his body; *aparam—*the other; *ardham—*half; *ca—*and; *bhavate—*unto You; *guṇebhyaḥ—*from the three modes of material nature; *nirmuktaḥ—*freed; *saḥ—*he (Śiva); *param—*supreme; *abhavat—*became; *brahma—*Brahman; *parama—*O supreme person.

**O Kṛṣṇa, You are beautiful like a raincloud. The Gaṅgā, which is the water that has washed Your lotus feet, made her abode on Śambhu’s head. Moreover, having offered half of his body to Umā and half of his body to You, he became thoroughly freed from the modes of nature and merged in the supreme Brahman. Therefore, O supreme Lord, shall we not praise You?**

*atra kavi-prauḍhoktiḥ. tatra śambhor adhi śiro yad yasmāt tava padāmbhaḥ padam akṛta, tena tvam anya eva ko ’pi sarvopari-vartamānaḥ stava-viṣayo nety atiśayokty-alaṅkāraḥ. tena umāyai dattvārdhaṁ vapur aparam ardhaṁ ca bhavata ity-ādinā sa vapur-viraheṇa paraṁ brahmābhavat, tvaṁ tu vapuṣaiva paraṁ brahma iti vastu, iti kavi-prauḍhoktiś catur-vidhā.*

In this verse we see the uncommon words of a poet.Furthermore, there is an *alaṅkāra* called *atiśayokti* (“a statement of superexcellence”): “Since Your foot water made its abode on Śiva’s head, You alone are a worthy object of praise. No one else exists above all.”

The *vastu* that comes from the statement “having given half of his body to Umā and half of his body to You” is that Śiva attained the formless supreme Brahman by separating himself from his body, but You are the supreme Brahman in living form!

Thus the four kinds of *kavi-prauḍhokti* have been shown.

### Text 24

*kavi-nibaddha-vaktṛ-prauḍhoktir yathā—*

The following verses are examples of the four kinds of *artha-śakti-bhū dhvani* that is *kavi-nibaddha-vaktṛ-prauḍhokti* (created by the uncommon words of a speaker that is described by the poet).

\[In this verse the suggestion is a *vastu* that gives rise to an *alaṅkāra.*\]

***Prākṛta:***  
***paripuṭṭe paripuṭṭaṁ jhīṇe jhīṇaṁ samammi samaṁ***  
***māhava tīe aṅgaṁ tujjha siṇeheṇa ghaḍiaṁ va***  
***Saṁskṛta:***  
***paripuṣṭe paripuṣṭaṁ kṣīṇe kṣīṇaṁ same samam***  
***mādhava tasyā aṅgaṁ tava snehena ghaṭitam eva***

*paripuṣṭe—*when \[Your love for her\] increases; *paripuṣṭam—*\[her body is\] expanded; *kṣīṇe—*when \[Your love for her\] decreases; *kṣīṇam—*\[her body is\] emaciated; *same—*when \[Your love for her\] is normal; *samam—*\[her body is\] normal; *mādhava—*O Mādhava; *tasyāḥ—*her; *aṅgam—*body; *tava snehena—*according to Your love; *ghaṭitam—*is fashioned; *eva—*certainly.

\[A messenger speaks to Kṛṣṇa:\]

**O Mādhava! That girl’s body is fashioned according to Your love for her. When Your love increasess, her body becomes plump; when Your love decreases, her body becomes thin; and when Your love is normal, her body stays normal.**

*atra sā āyuṣā jīvatīti na, api tu tava snehenaiveti vastu. “tujja siṇeheṇa ghaḍiaṁ va” iti tasyā aṅgaṁ tvat-snehopādānam iti aṅgāntarād vyatiriktaṁ tad-aṅgam iti vyatirekālaṅkāraḥ, iti vastunālaṅkāraḥ.*

In this verse, the *vastu* is: “This girl is living not by the measure of a lifespan but because of Your love.” The statement “Her body is fashioned according to Your love” suggests that since Kṛṣṇa’s love is the fundamental ingredient of her body, her body is different than others’. This is the *alaṅkāra* called *vyatireka* (contrast). In this way a *vastu* gives rise to an *alaṅkāra.*

### Text 25-26

\[All four kinds of *kavi-nibaddha-vaktṛ-prauḍhokti* are shown in this verse:\]

***śruti-yugam abhidhatte śrīla-vṛndāvane ’sau***  
***’ty anu diśam iti netra-dvandvam ātmā hṛdīti***  
***kva nu bhavasi mahātman brūhi kaṣṭāsavo ’mī***  
***tvad-anusaraṇa-pānthāḥ kaṇṭha eva bhramanti***

*śruti—*of ears; *yugam—*the pair; *abhidhatte—*says; *śrīla-vṛndāvane—*in Śrī Vṛndāvana; *asau—*He; *iti—*thus; *anu diśam—*in every direction; *iti—*thus; *netra-dvandvam—*the pair of eyes; *ātmā—*intelligence; *hṛdi—*is in the heart; *iti—*thus; *kva—*where; *nu—*actually; *bhavasi—*are You; *mahātman—*O great soul; *brūhi—*please tell; *kaṣṭa—*paining; *asavaḥ—*life airs; *amī—*these; *tvat—*You; *anusaraṇa—*follows; *pānthāḥ—*\[the life airs\] whose path; *kaṇṭhe—*in the throat; *eva—*only; *bhramanti—*wander.

\[Very agitated due to Kṛṣṇa’s staying in Mathurā, a *gopī* fancifully addresses Him:\]

**My ears say You’re in Vṛndāvana, my eyes say You’re in every direction, and my intelligence says You’re in the heart. O magnanimous one! Where actually are You? Please tell me. My paining life airs, which follow after You, are bewildered and are just wandering about in my throat.**

*atra kavi-nibaddhānurāginī bālā vaktrī. asyāḥ prauḍhoktau śabda-pratyakṣānubhava-rūpaṁ pramāṇa-trayaṁ paraspara-vyāhatam api sarvam eva pramā-karaṇam, na tu kutrāpi aprāmāṇyam iti vastu, tena ca tvaṁ vyāpako ’sīti vastu, tena ekasya sarvānugatatvād virodhe virodhālaṅkāro vyatireko vā. kva nu bhavasīti praśnena sandehālaṅkāraḥ, tena mahātmann iti kadācid yadi na brūṣe, tadā kapaṭī tvam iti hetv-alaṅkāraḥ. tena kaṣṭāsavo ’mī tvad-anusaraṇa-pānthāḥ kaṇṭha eva bhramantīti mad-asavaḥ saṁsthānaṁ tyaktavanta eva nirṇayam avijñāya kaṇṭha eva ghūrṇante, ato nirṇīya kathayatām iti vastu. idaṁ padyam asmad-guroḥ. iti kavi-nibaddha-vaktṛ-prauḍhoktiś caturdhā.*

In this verse the speaker is a passionate young girl who has been imagined by the poet.In this girl’s uncommon speech, the three *pramāṇas* (i.e *śabda, pratyakṣa,* and *anumāna*), although conflicting with each other, remain means of obtaining correct knowledge. The *vastu* that is suggested is that nothing is impossible in Kṛṣṇa. Therefore the further idea that is suggested is that Kṛṣṇa is all pervading. \[This is a *vastu* coming from a *vastu.*\]

Further, the *alaṅkāra* called *virodha* (“contradiction”) manifests since there is a contradiction established because of one person being everywhere at the same time. Or \[if we consider that Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes are transcendental\], then there is only the *alaṅkāra* called *vyatireka* (“contrast”). \[This is an *alaṅkāra* coming from a *vastu.* I.e the second *vastu* mentioned above gives rise to either of these *alaṅkāras*\]

Moreover, there is the *alaṅkāra* called *sandeha* (“doubt”) that arises due to the question “Where actually are You?” It follows that there is the *alaṅkāra* called *hetu* (“purpose”), since the implied meaning in *mahātman* is that, “\[If you tell me, then you’re a magnaminous person, but\] if somehow you don’t tell me, then you’re not a magnanimous person, you’re just a cheater.” \[This is an *alaṅkāra* coming from an *alaṅkāra.*\]

Because of that *hetv-alaṅkāra,* the statement “My paining life airs, which follow after You, are just wandering about in my throat” suggests the idea that “My life airs, having given up their proper places, are not certain where You are; therefore You should tell me.” \[This is a *vastu* coming from an *alaṅkāra.*\]

This verse was written by my *guru,* Śrīnātha Cakravartī. Thus the four kinds of *kavi-nibaddha-vaktṛ-prauḍhokti* have been shown.

***śabdārtha-bhūr eka eva (12)***

**There is only one variety of the** ***dhvani*** **called** ***śabdārtha-bhū*** **(also known as** ***ubhaya-śakti-bhū,*** **which arises from both sound and meaning).**

*śabdārthobhaya-śakty-uttho dhvanir eka eva. keṣāñcin mate ’trāpi vastv-alaṅkāra-sad-bhāvād aneka-vidhatvaṁ syāt, tan-nirāsāya eva-kāraḥ. kintu tan mataṁ na saṅgacchata iti na tena likhiṣyamāṇa-bhedādhikyam api bhedānām.*

There is only one suggestion that comes from the power of both the sound and the meaning.But some people think that *ubhaya-śakti-bhū dhvani* can be manifold because of the existence of *vastus* and *alaṅkāras.* In this *kārikā,* the word *eva* is used to defeat their argument. However, it’s not that their opinion doesn’t make sense. Therefore more varieties of these kinds will be written later on.

### Text 27

*yathā—* \[This is an example of *ubhaya-śakti-bhū dhvani:*\]

***aśeṣa-santāpa-haras tanu-bhṛtāṁ***  
***sadābalā-kāmada-medura-dyutiḥ***  
***tviṣāṁ cayair mādhava jīvana-prado***  
***bhavān bhuvaṁ śyāmayate ghano nabhaḥ***

*aśeṣa—*all; *santāpa—*suffering; *haraḥ—*takes away; *tanu-bhṛtām—*of those who have bodies; *sadā—*always; *abalā-kāma-da—*who gives lusty desires to ladies (or *balākā-mada—*which maddens the cranes); *medura—*is glossy; *dyutiḥ—*whose effulgence; *tviṣām—*of effulgence; *cayaiḥ—*with masses; *mādhava—*O Mādhava; *jīvana-pradaḥ—*giver of life (or giver of water); *bhavān—*You; *bhuvam—*the ground; *śyāmayate—*makes dark); *ghanaḥ—*the cloud; *nabhaḥ—*the sky.

**O Mādhava! You dispel everyone’s suffering, and clouds do as well. Your effulgence is glossy and You always make ladies lusty, while clouds have a glossy luster that makes the cranes constantly excited. Your give life to all living beings, while the clouds send forth showers. With much effulgence You make the ground dark blue, while the clouds make the sky dark with their masses of effulgence.**

*para-mate ’trāpi vastv-alaṅkāra-sad-bhāvaḥ.*

In regard to the opinion of others \[in the *vṛtti* of *kārikā* 12 above\], in this category also other poets consider that there are *vastus* and *alaṅkāras.*

### Text 28

*yathā vā— \[This is another example of ubhaya-śakti-bhū dhvani:\]*

***madhv-anta-kṛt sumanasām āmodair ghrāṇa-tarpaṇaḥ***  
***rādhādy-apara-paryāyo mādhavaḥ kasya na priyaḥ***

\[Two interpretations\]
