***māso māso marīcyaḥ sa-madhura-madhura-preyasī-preya-sīmā-***  
***vṛndā vṛndāvana-śrīr upavana-pavana-bhrāntir abhrānti-ramyā***  
***nandānandāgra-kandaḥ smara-samara-samo vyūha-hārīha hārī***  
***saṅgī saṅgīta-devyā ahar ahar aharad rāsa-rādhāṁ sa rādhām***

*māsaḥ—*of the moon; *mā—*brilliance; *asaḥ—*which emit (*as\[u\] kṣepane + \[k\]vi\[p\]*); *marīcyaḥ—*the rays; *sa-madhura—*who are endowed with *madhura-rasa; madhura—*sweet; *preyasī—*with beloved girls; *preya—*deserve to be filled; *sīmā—*limits ; *vṛndā—*whose many; *vṛndāvana—*of Vṛndāvana; *śrīḥ—*the beauty; *upavana—*in the groves; *pavana—*of the wind; *bhrāntiḥ—*the roving; *abhrānti—*on account of carefulness; *ramyā—*delightful; *nanda—*of Nanda Mahārāja; *ānanda—*of the bliss; *agra—*foremost; *kandaḥ—*(Kṛṣṇa) who is the root; *smara—*of Cupid; *samara—*in the battle; *sa-maḥ—*along with effulgence (*mā*); *vyūha—*the army (of Cupid); *hārī—*who destroys; *iha—*at this time (i.e at night); *hārī—*(Kṛṣṇa) who wears a garland; *saṅgī—*he is fond of; *saṅgīta—*of music; *devyāḥ—*the goddess; *ahar ahar—*every day; *aharat—*took away; *rāsa—*of the *rāsa* dance; *rādhām—*(Rādhā) because of whom there is the perfection; *sa—*He (Kṛṣṇa); *rādhām—*Rādhā.

**The rays of the moon were emitting so much light. Vṛndāvana was so beautiful that it deserved to be filled to its limits with sweet loving girls. And the movement of the breezes in the groves was pleasant on account of their carefulness \[i.e the breezes are thinking that it is appropriate for us to move slowly while in these groves of Vṛndāvana\]. It was at this time that Kṛṣṇa, the foremost cause of Nanda’s bliss, who wears a garland and is fond of music, took away Rādhā, who daily brings the** ***rāsa*** **dance to perfection. Kṛṣṇa appeared beautiful in the battle of Cupid as he destroyed Cupid’s soldiers.**

### Text 64

*antādi-yamakaṁ yathā—*

Now an example of a *antādi-yamaka:*

***na mānam ādhehi manasy adaḥ syadaḥ śubhaṁ śubhaṁyoḥ syati devi te ’vite***  
***aho aho-rātra-kṛtāruṣā ruṣāyaśo yaśodā-bhuvi vidyate ’dya te***

*na—*don’t; *mānam—*loving anger; *ādhehi—*place; *manasi—*in the heart; *adaḥ—*that; *syadaḥ—*impulse; *śubham—*the good fortune; *śubhaṁyoḥ—*who possesses fame ; *syati—*destroys; *devi—*O goddess; *te—*of you; *avite—*O you who are protected; *aho—*what a wonder; *aho-rātra—*day and night; *kṛta—*is produced; *aruṣā—*because of which \[anger\] mental pain; *ruṣā—*by anger; *ayaśaḥ—*infamy; *yaśodā-bhuvi—*toward Kṛṣṇa (who takes birth from Yaśodā); *vidyate—*is; *adya—*now; *te—*your.

**O beautiful girl! You have such a good reputation. Don’t let** ***māna*** **stay in your heart, for that impulse destroy your good fortune. How amazing, even now you remain shelled off by your anger, which causes you pain day and night. By now the news of your ill humor must have reached Yaśodā’s son; your reputation will suffer.**

### Text 65

*pratipāda-sarva-yamakaṁ yathā—*

Now an example of a *pratipāda-sarva-yamaka* (a *yamaka* where everything in each line is a repetition):

***mama tvayāmamatvayā***  
***na vehitaṁ nave hitam***  
***smarādhike sma rādhike***  
***cara kṣamāṁ ca rakṣa mām***

*mama—*my; *tvayā—*because of you; *amamatvayā—*who lacks possessiveness \[toward me\]; *na—*is not; *vā—*or; *īhitam—*desired; *nave—*O young girl; *hitam—*well-being; *smara—*on account of remembering; *ādhi-ke—*O you because of whom there is mental pain (i.e the rising of lust); *sma—*(term used to fill the meter); *rādhike—*O Rādhikā; *cara—*please practice; *kṣamām—*forgiveness; *ca—*and; *rakṣa—*protect; *mām—*me.

**\[Kṛṣṇa says:\] O young girl, you must not desire my well-being, because You don’t feel any possessiveness toward Me. O Rādhikā, I am tormented by Cupid just by remembering you. Please forgive Me and care for Me.**

### Text 66

*sarva-yamakaṁ yathā—*

***sasāra sā sa-sārasāsa-sāra-sāsa-sāra-sā***  
***sasārasāsa-sārasā sa sārasāsa-sāra-sā***

Now an example of a *sarva-yamaka* (a *yamaka* where the whole line is repeated):

*sasāra—*wandered, (from the *dhātu sṛ gatau*); *sā—*she (Rādhā); *sa-sārasā—*with a lotus \[in Her hand\]; *āsa—*there is complete bewilderment, (*as\[u\] kṣepaṇe*); *sāra—*in regard to whose movements, (*sṛ gatau*); *sāsa—*of Nidrā-devī, (*ṣas\[ū\] svapne*); *sāra—*the power; *sā—*who destroys (i.e puts to shame), (*ṣo ’nta-karmaṇi*); *sasa—*become idle (i.e stunned); *ārasa—*sound nicely, (*ras\[a\] śabde*); *asa—*shine (i.e appear beautiful), (*as\[a\] dīptau*); *sārasā—*Rādhā, because of whom the *cakravāka* birds; *saḥ—*he (Kṛṣṇa); *sārasa—*of the cranes; *āsa—*of the sitting, (*ās\[a\] upaveśane*); *sāra—*the firmness; *sā—*who destroys (i.e belittles), (*ṣo ’nta-karmaṇi*).

**Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa stroll together in a forest. She holds a lotus flower. Her movements put to shame Nidrā-devī’s power of bewilderment; upon seeing Rādhikā the cranes and the herons cry, become stunned, and look resplendent. Even Kṛṣṇa belittles the pose of these** ***cakravākas.***

### Text 67

*pratyakṣara-yamakam api kecid icchanti. tad yathā—*

Some poets also acknowledge *pratyakṣara-yamaka* (a repetition of each syllable). For example:

***vi-vitata-nānā-māmā vividha-dhanā nāv avaśyāśyā***  
***sā sādhu dhūtatarārā mama vavale lehi-hita-tanu nu***

*vi—*with birds; *vitata—*is filled; *nānā—*variegated; *mā—*whose beauty; *amā—*is incomparable; *vividha—*is manifold; *dhanā—*whose opulence; *nau—*by us two; *avaśya—*certainly; *āśyā—*should be pervaded; *sā—*that \[meeting place in the forest\] ; *sādhu—*nicely; *dhūta-tara—*is very broken; *ārā—*the path of which; *mama—*for me; *vavale—*was concealed; *lehi—*relishes; *hita—*and auspicious; *tanu—*you whose body; *nu—*O.

**That secret meeting place is very colorful and populated by many birds. It possesses many kinds of opulence and is incomparable. The path leading there is very broken, and the entrance is nicely concealed. We must go there, O you whose auspicious body tastes the sweetness of my mouth.**

*ity-ādi.*

*evaṁ sapta-viṁśatir bhedāḥ. dvi-khaṇḍa-tri-khaṇḍādikaṁ ca uktodāharaṇeṣv antar-bhavatīti pṛthag na darśitam, tathāpi (64 śloke) “aho aho-rātra-kṛtāruṣā ruṣā” ity-ādau dvi-khaṇḍam, (63 śloke) “māso māso marīcyaḥ sa-madhura-madhura-preyasī” ity-ādau tri-khaṇḍam, (67 śloke) “vi-vitata-nānā-māmā” ity-ādau catuḥ-khaṇḍam. evam anyāny apy ūhyāni. gadyeṣu tu nāyaṁ kramaḥ. tatra yayā sadāmoda-re dāmodare guṇa-sārādhikā sā rādhikānurajyati smeti.*

In this way twenty-seven kinds of *yamakas* have been illustrated. Instances of special varieties of *yamakas* wherethere istwo, three, or more repetitions in every line have not been shown separately since they are included in the previous examples. For example, in verse 64, which begins with the words *aho aho-rātra-kṛtāruṣā ruṣā,* there is two repetitions in every line; in verse 63, which begins with the words *māso māso marīcyaḥ sa-madhura-madhura-preyasī,* there is three repetitions in every line; and in verse 64, which begins witht the words *vi-vitata-nānā-māmā ,* there is four repetitions in every line. In this way other categories (where there are five or more repetitions in every line) should be inferred.

Obviously, this method \[of counting varieties of *yamakas* according to various combinations of *pādas*\] is not used in prose. For example, *yayā sadāmoda-re dāmodare guṇa-sārādhikā sā rādhikānurajyati smeti:* “Rādhikā loved Dāmodara. She had many exalted qualities, and He was always delightful.”

### Text 68

***bhinnā apy artha-bhedena yugapad bhāṣaṇa-kṣamāḥ***  
***tyajanti bhinna-rūpatvaṁ śabdā yac chleṣa eva saḥ***

*bhinnāḥ—*separate; *api—*although; *artha—*in meaning; *bhedena—*on account of a difference; *yugapad—*the simultaneous; *bhāṣaṇa—*expression; *kṣamāḥ—*tolerate; *tyajanti—*give up; *bhinna-rūpatvam—*the state of being different; *śabdāḥ—*words; *yat—*if; *śleṣaḥ—*double meaning; *eva—*only; *saḥ—*that.

**If words that are different on account of a difference in meaning can tolerate the expression of another meaning, they abandon their dissimilarity. This is called a** ***śleṣa*** **(“double meaning”).**

*artha-bhedena śabda-bhede iti nyāyāt. svara-bhedena bhedo nārtha-bhedenety āśaṅkyāha—kāvye svarasyānupayogena svara-gata-bhedābhāvāc ca, kevalārtha-bhedena bhinnā api śabdā yugapad ekadā yad yady arthāntara- bhāṣaṇa-kṣamāḥ santo ’pi bhinna-rūpatvaṁ tyajanti. artha-dvaya-śleṣaṇāt śleṣaḥ.*

This is based on the principle that \[similar-sounding\] words are different on account of a difference in meaning. If, however, someone argues that \[similar-sounding\] words are different on account of a difference in *svara*rather than a difference in meaning, the author replies with the verse above, which means: “If (*yat=yadi*) the words, although different purely on account of a difference in meaning and not a difference in *svara,* since *svaras* are not even used in *kāvya,* can simultaneously (*yugapat=ekadā*) tolerate the expression of another meaning, they abandon the state of being different. This is called a *śleṣa* (junction) on account of the junction of two meanings.”

### Text 69

***sa prakṛti-liṅga-varṇa-pratyaya-bhāṣā-vibhakti-pada-vacanaiḥ***  
***aṣṭa-vidho nirapekṣas tulyobhaya-vācya eva navamaḥ syāt***

*saḥ—*that (*śleṣa*); *prakṛti—*the word base; *liṅga—*the gender; *varṇa—*the phoneme; *pratyaya—*the suffix; *bhāṣā—*the language; *vibhakti—*the inflection; *pada—*the word; *vacanaiḥ—*according to the number (singular, dual, or plural); *aṣṭa-vidhaḥ—*is eightfold; *nirapekṣaḥ—*independent; *tulya—*is similar; *ubhaya—*of both; *vācyaḥ—*where the literal meaning; *eva—*only; *navamaḥ—*the ninth; *syāt—*is.

**There are eight basic varieties of** ***śleṣa*** **according to: i) the** ***prakṛti*** **(the word base), ii) the** ***liṅga*** **(the gender), iii) the** ***varṇa*** **(phoneme), iv) the** ***pratyaya*** **(suffix), v) the** ***bhāṣā*** **(language), vi) the** ***vibhakti*** **(inflection), vii) the** ***pada*** **(word), and viii) the** ***vacana*** **(“singular, dual, or plural number”).**

**The ninth variety of** ***śleṣa*** **is called** ***nirapekṣa,*** **where the literal meanings of both words are similar.**

*nirapekṣaḥ prakaraṇādi-vyatirekeṇāpi tulya-vācya-dvayaḥ.*

*Nirapekṣa* is a *śleṣa* where the two literal meanings are similar in spite of the differences in context and so on (see 2.37-43).

### Text 70

*krameṇodāharaṇāni—*

Now examples are given in order.

***manas tudantī kṣaṇa-dā vateṣṭā vidhau viruddhe tamasi pravṛddhe***  
***tasmin prasanne haritaḥ prasādaṁ dhanyā labhante tamasi praṇaṣṭe***

*manaḥ—*the mind; *tudantī—*\[is\] striking (or, *tudanti—*strike); *kṣaṇa- dā—*the night (which is so named because it provides a festival); *vata—*Oh, what a joy! (or, alas!); *iṣṭā—*that is desired (or, *iṣṭāḥ*—that are desired); *vidhau—*when the moon (or, when the creator); *viruddhe—*obstructed (or, averse); *tamasi—*when the darkness (or, when the mode of ignorance); *pravṛddhe—*increased; *tasmin—*when that (the moon or the creator); *prasanne—*is bright (or, is pleased); *haritaḥ—*the directions (or because of Hari); *prasādam—*clearness (or serenity); *dhanyāḥ—*fortunate; *labhante—*attain; *tamasi—*when the mode of ignorance; *praṇaṣṭe—*is destroyed.

**Oh, what a joy! When the moon is obstructed and the darkness has increased, the long-awaited night provides a festival and thus strikes the mind. When, however, the moon is bright and darkness is destroyed, the fortunate directions become clear.**

**\[Or by double meaning:\] Alas! When the creator is averse and the mode of ignorance increases, desirable objects make one blind and wound the mind. On the other hand, when the creator is pleased and ignorance is dispelled, fortunate people become serene by Hari’s grace.**

*atra īkṣaṇa-deti dāp lavane, kṣaṇa-deti ḍudāñ dāne iti prakṛti-bhedaḥ. iṣṭā iti puṁ-liṅgam iṣṭeti strī-liṅgam iti liṅga-bhedaḥ. punar iṣṭā iti bahu-vacanam, iṣṭety eka-vacanam. tena ca vacana-bhedaḥ. vidhāv itī-kārokāra-sārūpye varṇa-bhedaḥ. tudantīti śatṛ-pratyayaḥ, tudanti ity ākhyāta-pratyayaḥ, tena pratyaya-bhedaḥ. harita iti pañcamī, harita iti, jas tena vibhakti-bhedaḥ. anenaikena ṣaḍ-bhedāḥ pradarśitāḥ.*

Six kinds of *śleṣas* are shown in this verse: i) *prakṛti-śleṣa: īkṣaṇa-dāḥ* (“cutting the eyes”) is made from the *dhātu dā\[p\] lavane,* while *kṣaṇa-dā* (“providing a festival”) is made from the *dhātu \[ḍu\]dā\[ñ\] dāne,* ii) *liṅga-śleṣa: iṣṭāḥ* is masculine, and *iṣṭā* is feminine, iii) *vacana-śleṣa: iṣṭāḥ* is plural, and *iṣṭā* is singular, iv) *varṇa-śleṣa:* the word *vidhau* can stand for a *saptamī viṣṇubhakti* of *vidhi* (“the creator”) or *vidhu* (“the moon”), v) *pratyaya-śleṣa: tudantī* is a *\[ś\]at\[ṛ\] pratyaya,*and *tudanti* is an *ākhyāta pratyaya,* and vi) *vibhakti-śleṣa: haritaḥ* is a 5.1 form of *hari* as well as a 1.3 form of *harit* (“direction”).

### Text 71

*punar api bhaṅgy-antareṇa liṅga-vacana-bhedaṁ darśayati—*

Now the author shows *liṅga-śleṣa* and *vacana-śleṣa* again with another play on words. \[In this verse, there is *abhaṅga-śleṣa* (see 7.1-2) and the adjectives in the first three lines are either neuter 1.2 or feminine 1.1 and the verb *vidhattām* is either dual or singular, depending on whether they modify the words *nayane* or *tanuḥ*,.\]

***nīlāmbhoruha-gañjinī rati-raṇa-krīḍā-śramodgārinī***  
***nidrodbheda-vilāsinī sva-vaśatāsaṅkoca-sañcāriṇī***  
***anyonya-praṇaya-prakāśana-vidhāv anyonya-saṁhlādinī***  
***rādhāyā nayane harer api tanūḥ kṣemaṁ vidhattām tava***

*nīla—*blue; *ambhaḥ-ruha—*lotus (“rising on water”); *gañjinī—* putting to shame; *rati—*of love; *raṇa—*and battles; *krīḍā—*during games; *śrama—*during exertion; *udgārinī—*discharg; *nidrā—*of sleep; *udbheda—*when there is the manifestation; *vilāsinī—*playing; *sva—*their \[the eyes'\] (or, its \[the body's\]); *vaśatā—*control \[over Kṛṣṇa or, over the world\]; *asaṅkoca—*the expansion; *sañcāriṇī—*causing; *anyonya—*for each other; *praṇaya—*of love; *prakāśana—*the manifestation; *vidhau—*while doing; *anyonya—*to one another; *saṁhlādinī—*giving pleasure; *rādhāyāḥ—*of Rādhā; *nayane—*the two eyes; *hareḥ—*of Hari; *api—*also; *tanuḥ—*the body; *kṣemam—*happiness; *vidhattām—*may it create; *tava—*for you.

**Rādhā’s eyes and Kṛṣṇa’s body belittle blue lotuses. Due to exertion during the battle of love, Her eyes shed tears and His body perspires. Both are playful at night. Her eyes expand their control over Kṛṣṇa and His body expands its control over the world. Both give each other pleasure while manifesting their love for each other. May Rādhā's eyes and Kṛṣṇa's body bestow happiness upon you.**

### Text 72

*bhāṣā-śleṣaḥ, yathā—*

In this verse the double meaning arises by also considering an interpretation in a *prākṛta* language, in this case *śaurasenī,* the former dialect of Vraja.

***Saṁskṛta:***  
***uddāma-kāma-kaṇḍūla-kara-maṇḍala-caṇḍimā***  
***kālindī-kuñjaro dhatte vihāraṁ vāri-mañjulam***

*uddāma—*boundless; *kāma—*for enjoyment; *kaṇḍūla—*itching; *kara—*of hands; *maṇḍala—*due to a multitude; *caṇḍimā—*whose passion; *kālindī—*in the Yamunā; *kuñjaraḥ—*an elephant; *dhatte—*performs; *vihāram—*pastimes; *vāri—*on account of the water; *mañjulam—*that are lovely.

*Prākṛta:*  
*uddāma-kāmakaṁ ḍūla-karamaṁ ḍala caṇḍi mā*  
*kālindī-kuñja-rodhatte vihāraṁ vārimaṁ julam*

*uddāma*—great; *kāmakam—*for which the desire; *ḍūla—*that has already been begun; *karamam—*work; *ḍala—*destroy; *caṇḍi—*O angry lady; *mā—*don’t; *kālindī—*near the Yamunā; *kuñja—*and in the arbor; *rodhatte—*on the shore; *vihāram—*pastimes; *vārimam—*I will stop; *julam—*the two.

\[Interpretation in *saṁskṛta:*\]

**There is an elephantin the Yamunā \[i.e Kṛṣṇa\] performing charming water pastimes. He is very agitated because his hands are itching for boundless enjoyment.**

\[Interpretation in *prākṛta:*\]

**O angry lady! I myself shall stop the pastimes in the arbor near the Yamunā and the pastimes on the shore of the Yamunā. Don't interrupt your work for which you have such a strong desire.**

### Text 73-74

*pada-śleṣo yathā—*

These two verses are an example of *pada-śleṣa:*

***samarālā ruṣeveyaṁ***  
***rādhike sarva-dāruṇā***  
***matiś ca tava dṛṣṭiś ca***  
***sama eva babhūvatuḥ***

***kṛṣṇa-pakṣe balavatī***  
***doṣākara-parāṅ-mukhī***  
***same dve tāmasī rātriḥ***  
***sāttvikī ca satāṁ matiḥ***

*samara-ālā—*accepts a battle (or, *sam-arālā—*is completely crooked); *ruṣā—*due to anger; *iva—*as if; *iyam—*this; *rādhike—*O Rādhikā; *sarva—*at all times; *dāruṇā—*harsh (or, *sarvadā aruṇā—*red in every situation); *matiḥ—*mind; *ca—*and; *tava—*Your; *dṛṣṭiḥ—*glance; *ca—*and; *same—*the same; *eva—*certainly; *babhūvatuḥ—*have become; *kṛṣṇa-pakṣe—*during the waning phase (or, in regard to Kṛṣṇa’s side); *balavatī—*strong; *doṣā-kara—*to the moon (or, *doṣa-ākara—*the origin of faults); *parāk-mukhī—*averse; *same—*are the same; *dve—*both; *tāmasī—*dark; *rātriḥ—*the night; *sāttvikī—*in the mode of goodness; *ca—*and; *satām—*of the devotees; *matiḥ—*mind.

**\[Kṛṣṇa speaks to angry Rādhā: \] O Rādhikā! Your mind and Your glance have become similar. Your mind is eager for battle (*****samarālā*****) and always harsh (*****sarvadāruṇā*****) and Your eyes are completely crooked (*****samarālā*****) and always red (*****sarvadāruṇā*****). The dark night and the devotees’ pure mind are similar also. The dark night is strong during the waning half of the month (*****kṛṣṇa-pakṣe*****) and is thus averse to the moon (*****doṣākara-parāṅmukhī*****) and the devotees’ pure mind is strong on Kṛṣṇa's opinion (*****kṛṣṇa-pakṣe*****) and is averse to things that cause faults (*****doṣākara-parāṅmukhī*****).**

*vākya-gatatvenāyaṁ samobhaya-vācyodāharaṇeṣu draṣṭavyaḥ. śabdārtha-śleṣayor ayaṁ bhedaḥ—yatra śabda-parivartanenāpi na śleṣatva-bhaṅgaḥ, so ’rtha-śleṣaḥ. anyas tu sa-bhaṅgābhaṅgatvābhyām eva śabda-śleṣaḥ. sa coktodāharaṇeṣu śabda-parivṛtty-abhāvāt.*

Since it is included in the sentence, this *śleṣa* is to be considered among the examples of where the literal meanings of both words are similar (see *nirapekṣa* in 7.69). The difference between *śabda-śleṣa* and *artha-śleṣa* is this: An *artha-śleṣa* is where the double meaning isn’t lost even by the use of different words. A *śabda-śleṣa* is the opposite; it occurs by by breaking up the words differently (*sa-bhaṅga*) or by not breaking up the words differently (*abhaṅga*). . *Śabda-śleṣa* was seen in all the previous examples since in those cases the double meaning would have been lost by the use of different words.

### Text 75

*artha-śleṣo yathā—*

An example of an *artha-śleṣa* is:

***vilola-samphulla-kadamba-mālaḥ samullasan-mañjula-barhi-barhaḥ***  
***aśeṣa-santāpa-haro janānāṁ kṛṣṇaś ca meghaś ca sahojjihīte***

*vilola—*moving to and fro; *samphulla—*fully expanded; *kadamba—*of *kadamba* flowers; *mālaḥ—*whose garland (or, because of which \[clouds\] a multitude); *samullasat—*brilliant (or appear); *mañjula—*lovely; *barhi-barhaḥ—*who has a peacock feather (or, because of which \[clouds\] the tail feathers of the peacock); *aśeṣa—*unlimited; *santāpa—*affliction; *haraḥ—*takes away; *janānām—*of people; *kṛṣṇaḥ—*Kṛṣṇa; *ca—*and; *meghaḥ—*cloud; *ca—*and; *saha—*simultaneously; *ujjihīte—*goes up.

**Kṛṣṇa and the cloud are alike. Both Kṛṣṇa and the cloud soothe the pain of living entities. Kṛṣṇa has a garland of fully expanded** ***kadamba*** **flowers that swings to and fro; clouds cause** ***kadamba*** **flowers to grow and move with the wind. Kṛṣṇa has a brilliant and lovely peacock feather; clouds cause the lovely tail feathers of the peacock to appear.**

*atra kadambādi-śabdānāṁ parivṛttyāpi na śleṣatva-hānir ity artha-śleṣa eva.*

In this verse, the double meaning wouldn't be lost even by substituting the words *kadamba* and so on with different words. Therefore this is called an *artha-śleṣa.*

### Text 76-77

*atha śabdālaṅkāra-prastāve prāptāvasaratayā citra-kāvyam api pradarśyate.*

Now we will also discuss *citra-kāvya,* since it is related to current topic of *śabdālaṅkāras*.

*tatra yady api—*

***naṭānāṁ ca kavīnāṁ ca***  
***mārgaḥ karkaśa eva yaḥ***  
***rasābhi-vyaktaye nāsau***  
***śakti-jñaptyai sa kevalam***

***citraṁ nīrasam evāhur***  
***bhagavad-viṣayaṁ yadi***  
***tadā kiñcic ca rasavad***  
***yathekṣoḥ parva-carvaṇam***

*naṭānām—*for actors; *ca—*and; *kavīnām—*for poets; *ca—*also; *mārgaḥ—*path; *karkaśaḥ—*tough; *eva—*certainly; *yaḥ—*which; *rasa-abhivyaktaye—*for the sake of manifesting *rasa; na—*not; *asau—*that; *śakti—*potency; *jñaptyai—*for the sake of informing (i.e for showing off); *saḥ—*that; *kevalam—*only; *citram—citra-kāvya*; *niḥrasam—*is tasteless; *eva—*only; *āhuḥ—*they say; *bhagavat—*is focused on the Lord; *viṣayam—*the topic; *yadi—*if; *tadā—*then; *kiñcit—*slightly; *ca—*and; *rasavat—*relishable; *yathā—*just like; *ikṣoḥ—*of sugarcane; *parva—*the knots; *carvaṇam—*chewing.

**Even though the previous authorities have said that** ***citra-kāvya,*** **which is very difficult for poets to compose and for actors to recite, is tasteless since it is not meant for manifesting** ***rasa*** **but just for showing off one's poetical ability, still it can become slightly relishable if the topic is centered on the Lord, just like chewing on the knotty parts of the sugarcane.**

### Text 78

*tatra kiñcit pradarśyate. tatra prathamaṁ pratilomānuloma-pādo yathā—*

Some examples will now be shown. First a verse where every line reads the same forwards and backwards:

***rādhā sāra-rasādhārā***  
***māra-māra-ramā-ramā***  
***kāśodāra-radāśokā***  
***sā lalāsa sa-lālasā***

*rādhā—*Rādhā; *sāra-rasa—*the essence of *rasa* (i.e the *mādhurya-rasa*); *ādhārā—*She produces; *māra—*due to Her ardent passion; *mā—*luster (i.e beauty); *ra—*She gives; *ramā—*of the Lakṣmīs; *ramā—*She is the Lakṣmī; *kāśa—*because of their effulgence; *udāra—*are majestic; *radā—*whose teeth; *aśokā—*is without sorrow; *sā—*She; *lalāsa—*appeared beautiful; *sa-lālasā—*who has a longing.

**Śrī Rādhā, the fountainhead of all Lakṣmīs, appears very beautiful. She generates the** ***mādhurya-rasa*** **and emanates beauty due to Her ardent passion. . Her smile is noble on account of the brilliance of Her teeth. She is free from sorrowyet She has an inner longing.**

### Text 79

*pratilomānuloma-śloko yathā—*

Now an example of when the whole versereads the same forwards and backwards:

***kādhi-dā sa-svabhā rādhe māno māstu ramā-dhave***  
***vedha-māras tu mā no mādher ābhā sva-sadādhikā***

*kā—*which; *ādhi—*of mental pain; *dā—*giver; *sa-sva-bhā—*with her luster; *rādhe—*O Rādhā; *mānaḥ—*loving anger; *mā astu—*don’t let it be; *ramā-dhave—*toward Kṛṣṇa; *vedha—*of activity; *māraḥ—*the destruction; *tu—*but; *mā—*don’t \[let it be\]; *naḥ—*our; *mā—*don’t \[let it become\]; *ādheḥ—*of mental pain; *ābhā—*the manifestation; *sva-sada—*of ours; *adhikā—*an abundance.

**O Rādhā! Has any lady ever looked resplendent by giving others pain? Calm Your** ***māna*** **toward Kṛṣṇa. Don't disturb our activities and don't increase our pain.**

### Text 80-81

***pratilomānulomau ślokau yathā—***  
***māna-sāra-rasādhārā***  
***sāyaṁtī vana-mālinā***  
***saṁlalāsa mahāmodāsāśv***  
***āmāda-nisādhvasā***

***sādhv asāni damāśvāsā***  
***dā mohām asalālasam***  
***nāli mānavatīyaṁ sā***  
***rādhā sāra-rasānamā***

*māna—*of loving anger; *sāra—*which is the essence; *rasa—*the *rasa; adhārā—*does not maintain; *sā—*She; *ayantī*—while going; *vana-mālinā—*with Kṛṣṇa, who has a garland of sylvan flowers; *saṁlalāsa—*frolicked; *mahā-āmoda—*on account of intense bliss; *āsā—*resplendent; *āśu—*briskly; *āmāda—*due to intoxication; *nisādhvasā—*fearless; *sādhu—*well; *asāni—*I remain; *dama—*on account of the subduing \[of Rādhā’s *māna*\]; *āśvāsā—*one whose breathing \[has returned\]; *dāḥ—*give; *mā—*don’t; *ūhām—*a supposition; *a-sa-lālasam—*without eagerness; *na—*is not; *āli—*O friend; *mānavatī—*self-conceited; *iyam sā—*this famous; *rādhā—*Rādhā; *sāra—*of the essential part \[in Her heart of hearts\]; *rasa—*on account of its being the mood; *ānamā—*is submissive.

\[A *sakhī* responds to a friend who inquired, “Rādhā is always in loving anger. What is She doing now?”:\]

**Rādhā isn’t huffy anymore. She was briskly frolicking with Vanamālī as They were walking along. She became intoxicated by His fragrance and lost all timidity. Rādhā is resplendent with intense bliss. Now I can breathe at ease knowing She has suppressed Her** ***māna.***

\[The other *sakhī* responds: “Now speak truthfully. Even I could not pacify such *māna.*” The first *sakhī* retorts:\]

**Girl, stop doubting so negatively. Rādhā is naturally submissive.**

### Text 82

*mahā-sarvato-bhadraṁ yathā—*

Now an example of *citra-kāvya* using the diagram called “good on every side.”

***sādhārāśrīḥ śrī-rādhā sā***  
***dhāmākāmā mā-kāmādhāt***  
***rākā-dhī-mā mādhīkārā***  
***śrī-mā mānenemām āśrīḥ***

*sādha—*accomplishment; *ārā—*attains; *āśrīḥ—*whose beauty is complete; *śrī-rādhā—*Śrī Rādhā; *sā—*that well-known; *dhāma—*in an abode (i.e in an arbor); *a—*Kṛṣṇa; *kāmā—*She loves; *mā—*does not exist; *kāmā—*whose selfish desire; *adhāt—*\[Lakṣmī\] thought; *rākā—*are like a full moon; *dhī—*and wisdom; *mā—*whose effulgence; *mā—*does not exist; *ādhi—*She whose anguish; *i—*of Cupid; *ka—*the happiness; *ārā—*completely takes; *śrī-mā—*Lakṣmī; *mānena—*with much respect; *imām—*to Her; *āśrīḥ—*Lakṣmī (“who is resorted to”).

**Rādhā is no longer anguished. She is steeped in erotic bliss. She is making love to Kṛṣṇa in a grove, but not for selfish reasons. Śrī Rādhā is accomplished in every way. Her beauty is sublime. Her effulgence and wisdom are like a full moon.**

**Although Lakṣmī is opulent and gives shelter to all, she reflects on Rādhā reverentially.**

### Text 83

*sarvato-bhadraṁ yathā—*

The diagram for this verse is called “good on every side.”

***dhārāsāra-rasā rādhā***  
***rāsa-lāsyasya lāsa-rā***  
***sālākārara-kālāsā***  
***rasya-rasyasya rasya-ra***

*dhāra—*for Viṣṇu (i.e Kṛṣṇa: “who holds up”); *āsāra—*is like a confluence of streams; *rasā—*the *rasa* of whom; *rādhā—*Rādhā; *rāsa—*in the *rāsa* dance; *lāsyasya—*in relation to dancing; *lāsa—*an embrace; *rā—*who gives; *sāla—*like a tree; *ākāra—*a shape; *ra—*\[time\] that gives; *kāla—*during the time \[of play\]; *āsā—*whose effulgence is complete; *rasya—*who is juicy; *rasi—*by an impassioned \[*gopī*\]; *asya—*O Kṛṣṇa (“who should be accompanied”); *rasya—*relishable pastimes; *ra—*O Kṛṣṇa (“who provides”).

**Śrī Rādhā’s** ***rasa*** **is a confluence of streams meant for Kṛṣṇa, who never lets His weapon down. First She embraces Him during a dance in the** ***rāsa*** **pastimes. Then She shines during foreplay and causes Kṛṣṇa’s flower tree to bloom. O Kṛṣṇa! You grant exquisite pastimes. You ought to be accompanied by an impassioned, juicy young girl.**

### Text 84

*yathā vā—*

***nālīkānanakālīnālīva sāra-rasāvalī***  
***kāsā rasāsāra-sākāna-rasā mama sāra na***

*nālīka—*is like a lotus; *ānanakā—*whose face; *alīna—*that is not devoted; *alī—*a bee; *iva—*like; *sāra-rasa—*of honey (“fragrant liquid of the essence”); *āvalī—*She is like a mass; *ka-āsā—*She is in *māna*; *rasa—*of the bliss of *madhura-rasa; āsāra—*the hard rain; *sā—*destroys; *aka—*because of unhappiness; *āna—*breathing; *rasā—*She for whom \[breathing\] is a poison; *mama—*My; *sā—*She; *āra na—*has not come.

\[During the *rāsa* festival of spring, Kṛṣṇa, not seeing Rādhikā, thought as follows:\]

**Rādhā hasn’t arrived. In separation from Me however, breathing is like poison to Her. She must be in** ***māna.*** **Her lotus face is like a sculpture of crystallized honey, therefore She has become a bee that is not devoted to sweetness anymore. But now She is liquidating My torrent of blissful feelings.**

### Text 85

*chatra-bandho yathā—* The diagram for this verse is “the umbrella.”

***tanutāṁ tanutāṁ rādhā-***  
***kṛṣṇayoś carita-śrutiḥ***  
***hṛt-tāpanaṁ sudhā-sindhu-***  
***dhārā tāṁ nu tatāṁ nuta***

*tanutām—*to a state of thinness; *tanutām—*should expand (or should make); *rādhā-kṛṣṇayoḥ—*of Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa; *carita—*of the deeds; *śrutiḥ—*the listening; *hṛt—*of the heart; *tāpanam—*the torment; *sudhā—*of nectar; *sindhu—*the ocean; *dhārāḥ—*O you all who contain; *tām—*that \[listening\]; *nu—*(a particle used out of respect); *tatām—*which is expanded; *nuta—*you all should praise.

**Listening to the narrations of the pastimes of Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa makes the afflictions of the heart insignificant. These narrations should be expanded. O all of you who swim in this ocean of nectar: Praise these topics!**

### Text 86

*khaḍga-bandho yathā—*

In this instance, the diagram called “the sword” is used. \[Verses 86 and 87 are used jointly in one diagram.\]

***rādhā-mādhavayoḥ keliḥ***  
***śruti-hṛt-sukha-dāyikā***  
***kāmaṁ tanotu vaḥ kṣemaṁ***  
***premānandaugha-nirbharā***

*rādhā-mādhavayoḥ—*of Rādhā and Mādhava; *keliḥ—*the pastimes; *śruti—*of the ears; *hṛt—*and of the heart; *sukha—*the happiness; *dāyikā—*gives; *kāmam—*freely; *tanotu—*may it spread; *vaḥ—*to you all; *kṣemam—*auspiciousness; *prema—*of pure love; *ānanda—*of the bliss; *ogha—*flood (or abundance); *nirbharā—*contains entirely.

**The deeds of Rādhā and Mādhava are flooded with the bliss of pure love. Reciting these pastimes delights the ears and the heart. May these discourses bring complete auspiciousness to you all.**

### Text 87

***rāsārambhe nṛtya-gīta-***  
***vāditrādi-manoharā***  
***rābhasya-sārā saubhāgyo-***  
***’dharī-kṛta-parāparā***

*rāsa—*of the *rāsa* dance; *ārambhe—*at the beginning; *nṛtya—*\[on account of\] the dancing; *gīta—*the singing; *vāditra—*the music; *ādi—*beginning with; *manoharā—*is charming; *rābhasya—*of eagerness; *sārā—*in which there is firmness; *saubhāgya—*because of good fortune (i.e happiness); *adharī-kṛta—*are made lower; *para-aparā—*\[the pastimes\] by which the highest \[bliss\] as well as other types \[of bliss\].

**At the beginning of the** ***rāsa*** **dance, the music, singing, and dancing are exceptionally charming. Everyone participating is always eager to increase the merriment. These pastimes are so enchanting that they belittle all other kinds of happiness.**

*muraja-bandho yathā (66 śloke) “sasāra sā” ity-ādi. eṣa eva go-mūtrikā-bandhaḥ, eṣa eva baddha-kavāṭa-bandhaḥ, eṣa eva mukta-kavāṭaḥ. eṣa eva śṛṅkhalā-bandhaḥ, eṣa eva dvy-akṣaraḥ.*

Verse 66, beginning *sasāra sā,* is an example of the use of only two different consonants in one verse.It can be drawn in the following diagrams: i) *muraja* (“the drum”), ii) *go-mūtrikā* (“zig-zag”), iii) *baddha-kavāṭa* (“closed door”), iv) *mukta-kavāṭa* (“open door”), and v) *śṛṅkhalā* (“the chain”).

### Text 88

*śaṅkha-bandho yathā—*

The diagram used for this verse is called “the conch.”

***dheya-mādhurya-maryādā***  
***rādhā mādhavam āra sā***  
***sāra-mā-badha-mā-dhārā***  
***dheya-mādhurya-saubhagā***

*dheya—*should be put in practice \[by everyone\]; *mādhurya—*sweet; *maryādā—*the moral principles of whom; *rādhā—*Rādhā; *mādhavam—*to Mādhava; *āra—*went; *sā—*She; *sāra—*the best; *mā—*knowledge; *badha—*which belittles; *mā—*effulgence; *dhārā—*She brings forth; *dheya—*should be nourished; *mādhurya—*and sweetness; *saubhagā—*whose happiness.

**Rādhā’s charming etiquette should be followed by everyone. Her sweetness and happiness should be facilitated. Seeing Her splendor when She meets Mādhava, one forgets everything else.**

### Text 89

*patākā-bandho yathā— The diagram for this verse is “the flag.”*

***rāsa-taṁsa-rasārambhe rādhā sāra ramā-dhavam***  
***bandha-māra-rasādhārābhe ’raṁ sārasa-taṁsa-rā***

*rāsa—*in the form of the *rāsa* dance; *taṁsa—*of the best; *rasa—*delight; *ārambhe—*at the beginning; *rādhā—*Rādhā; *sā—*She; *āra—*went (i.e met); *ramā—*of the goddess of fortune; *dhavam—*the husband; *bandha—*bondage of repeated birth and death, or: attachment toward anything or anyone except Kṛṣṇa; *māra—*that destroys; *rasa-ādhāra—*the sun (“toward which moisture goes”); *ābhe—*which is like; *aram—*quickly; *sārasa—*lotus; *taṁsa—*the best; *rā—*gives.

**The** ***rāsa*** **dance is the utmost delight. It annihilates attachment for anything except Kṛṣṇa. In this way it resembles the sun as a path of liberation. At the beginning of the dance, Rādhā quickly unites with Mādhava and gives Him Her best toy lotus.**

*eṣa eva gadā-bandhaś ca.*

This verse can also be drawn in the diagram called “the club.”

### Text 90

*garbhākṣaro yathā—*

This verse is composed in the *āryā* meter, where each line has the same number of *mātrās,* in this case seventeen.

***kāma-trapālī lāsya-rādhikā***  
***sādhvani śāte nā-ray arājate***

*kāma—*out of love; *trapā—*of bashfulness; *ālī—*Rādhā (“who has an abundance”); *lāsya—*the dancing; *rādhikā—*She makes perfect; *sā—*She; *adhvani—*on the path; *śāte—*\[the path\] that is the form of happiness; *nā—*Kṛṣṇa; *re—*\[the path\] which gives; *arājate—*is not manifest.

**Rādhā is very bashful out of love yet She brings Her dance with Kṛṣṇa to new heights of excellence. But tonight that lighthearted girl isn’t on the providential path that goes to Her rendezvous.**

### Text 91

*padma-bandho yathā— The diagram for this verse is “the lotus.”*

***rādhikā rucirākārā rākā-rāsa-sthalī-sarā***  
***rāsa-līlā-parā sārārāsārā gīḥ-pikādhi-rā***

*rādhikā—*Rādhikā; *rucira—*is radiant; *ākārā—*whose body; *rākā—*to the full moon; *rāsa—*of the *rāsa* dance; *sthalī—*\[the full moon, in the form of\] the place; *sarā—*who goes; *rāsa-līlā—*the *rāsa* pastimes; *parā—*whose is dedicated to; *sāra—*in such a way that it is the best; *ārāsa—*high-pitched sounds; *ārā—*She attains; *gīḥ—*by Her voice; *pika—*to the cuckoos; *ādhi—*mental pain; *rā—*She gives.

**Rādhikā is devoted to the** ***rāsa*** **pastimes. Her form is radiant, and She always goes to the moonlike circle of the** ***rāsa*** **dance. Rādhā sings high-pitched notes so perfectly that She torments the cuckoos.**

### Text 92

*cakra-bandho yathā— The diagram for this verse is “the wheel.”*

***yasya śrī-tulanāṁ na kaścana gamī bhaktaugha-tāpārdano***  
***dhvastānākya-bala-cchaviḥ sa na hi māṁ tvaṁ muñca moda-kṣamaḥ***  
***san-nātha vraja-keli-śabdana-manaḥ-śraddhā-nadī-kautukī***  
***kīla-prāya ihādhvanīha satate no mat-samaḥ pātakī***

*yasya—*with whom; *śrī—*in terms of opulence; *tulanām—*equality; *na—*not; *kaścana—*someone; *gamī—*will attain; *bhakta—*of devotees; *ogha—*of a multitude; *tāpa—*the suffering; *ardanaḥ—*who destroys; *dhvasta—*is destroyed; *anākya—*of the demons; *bala—*by whom the strength; *chaviḥ—*and the luster; *saḥ—*he; *na hi—*don’t; *mām—*me; *tvam—*you; *muñca—*abandon; *moda—*of delighting; *kṣamaḥ—*capable; *sat-nātha—*O Lord of the saintly; *vraja—*of Vraja; *keli—*the pastimes; *śabdana—*caused by talking about; *manaḥ—*in the mind; *śraddhā—*of faith; *nadī—*in the river; *kautukī—*eager \[to bathe\]; *kīla—*a sharp piece of wood; *prāyaḥ—*like; *iha—*in this world; *adhvani iha—*on this path; *satate—*perpetual; *no—*not; *mat-samaḥ—*equal to me; *pātakī—*a sinner.

**O Lord of the devotees! No one will ever be equal to You in opulence, for You destroy the devotees’ miseries and diminish the demons’ strength and luster. You alone are capable of giving pleasure to your devotees. In this world there is no sinner like me. I am like a splinter on the eternal path of devotional service. But please don’t abandon me for I long to bathe in the river of faith that flows in the mind by talking about the pastimes of Vraja.**

### Text 93

*atra—*

***śrī-nātha-pāda-kautukya-vratāmodī kaviḥ śamī***  
***yasya dhvastācchaviḥ sannāśraddhā kaścana tat-samaḥ***

*śrī-nātha—*the Lord of Lakṣmī, (or: Śrīnātha Cakravartī, the guru of Kavi Karṇapura); *pāda—*the feet; *kautukya—*of desiring \[to serve\]; *vrata—*in the vow; *āmodī—*takes pleasure; *kaviḥ—*poet; *śamī—*peaceful; *yasya—*whose; *dhvastā—*is destroyed; *acchaviḥ—*dejectedness; *sannā—*is dissolved; *aśraddhā—*disbelief; *kaścana*—someone; *tat—*to him; *samaḥ*—equal to him.

**Poets become uninterested in mundane life when they take pleasure in the vow of desiring to serve Mādhava’s feet. Their melancholy and disbeliefs vanish subsequently. No one is equal to them.**

\[Alternatively, the first two *pādas* of this verse can refer to Kavi Karṇapura himself:\]

**This peaceful Kavi takes pleasure in the vow of only desiring to serve the feet of his** ***guru,*** **Śrīnātha Cakravartī.**

### Text 94

*śārṅga-bandho yathā—* The diagram for these verses is “the bow.”

***śrī-kṛṣṇo gāthā nāmeyaṁ***  
***karmaṇā ca kadācana***  
***nāsādyate pāvanikā***  
***vinā tasya dayāṁ hareḥ***

*śrī-kṛṣṇaḥ—*Śrī Kṛṣṇa; *gāthā—*song; *nāma—*named; *iyam—*this; *karmaṇā—*by deeds; *ca—*and; *kadācana na—*never; *āsādyate—*can be obtained; *pāvanikā—*which purifies \[the three worlds\]; *vinā—*without; *tasya—*of that well-known \[Hari\]; *dayām—*the mercy; *hareḥ—*of Hari.

**The song of Śrī Kṛṣṇa’s name purifies the three worlds. It can only be heard by His grace, not by any other means.**

### Text 95

***katham asya kṛpā-sindhor***  
***janeṣu ca mitho ratiḥ***  
***janyate bahu-janmānte***  
***su-kṛtaiḥ kāraṇāyitaiḥ***

*katham—*how \[is it possible\]; *asya—*of His; *kṛpā—*of mercy; *sindhoḥ—*of Kṛṣṇa (“who is an ocean”); *janeṣu—*toward people; *ca—*and; *mithaḥ—*\[love\] together with \[His name\]; *ratiḥ—*love; *janyate—*manifests; *bahu—*of many; *janma—*births; *ante—*at the end; *su-kṛtaiḥ—*by good deeds; *kāraṇāyitaiḥ—*which act like causes.

**Does Kṛṣṇa, an ocean of mercy, show His love for people and reveal His name merely because they have performed good deeds for many births?**

### Text 96

***caraṇāsava-lābhena***  
***dāruṇaṁ karuṇātmatām***  
***mohaṁ hitvā kila praiti***  
***tam amuṁ satataṁ smara***

*caraṇa—*of His feet; *āsava—*the nectar; *lābhena—*on account of obtaining; *dāruṇam—*harshness (i.e hard-heartedness); *karuṇā—*is endowed with compassion; *ātmatām—*the state of being one whose nature; *moham—*illusion; *hitvā—*having rejected; *kila—*indeed; *praiti—*attains; *tam—*that well-known; *amum—*Him; *satatam—*constantly; *smara—*remember.

**After obtaining the nectar of Śrī Kṛṣṇa’s feet, one certainly becomes compassionate, soft-hearted, and free from illusion. Always remember Him.**

### Text 97

***tasya rūpaṁ cetasi ca***  
***mantravat satataṁ likha***  
***tena sādhutayā kṛṣṇe***  
***bhaviṣyati samāgamaḥ***

*tasya—*His; *rūpam—*form; *cetasi—*in the mind (or heart); *ca—*and; *mantra-vat—*has a mantra; *satatam—*always; *likha—*write; *tena—*by this; *sādhutayā—*state of goodness; *kṛṣṇe—*Kṛṣṇa; *bhaviṣyati—*will manifest; *samāgamaḥ—*a meeting.

**Kṛṣṇa’s form has a mantra of its own. Always visualize Him. By performing this excellent technique, you will meet Him eventually.**

### Text 98

*eṣu—* In regard to these verses:

***śrī-nātha-pāda-pāthoja-***  
***rāsa-lālasa-cetasā***  
***kṛteyaṁ tata-modā ca***  
***sva-jane kavinā kathā***

*śrī-nātha—*of Śrī Nātha \[Cakravartī\]; *pāda—*feet; *pāthaḥ-ja—*because of the lotus (“born from water”); *rāsa—*for the *rāsa* pastimes; *lālasa—*is endowed with the desire; *cetasā—*whose heart; *kṛtā—*is done; *iyam—*this; *tata—*is widespread; *modā—*the delight of which \[talk\]; *ca—*and; *sva-jane—*toward his own people; *kavinā—*by the poet; *kathā—*talk.

**A certain poet has created this delightful composition of** ***citra-kāvya*** **for his circle of friends. His desire to participate in the** ***rāsa*** **pastimes has arisen as a result of serving the lotus feet of Śrī Nātha.**

*iti ślokāntaram.* Therefore another verse is presented.

### Text 99

*yathā vā—*

***śrīśa-prītiḥ sva-nāmākṛti-kathana vinābhāva-pakṣe na vidyā***  
***’moda-śraddhā-kalāpād api sukhada-mitho-bhāva-sāmrājyataś ca***  
***ramyā ramya-sthalastha-prasara-mada-kalā-moda-lakṣmī-sameta-***  
***premāsanna-pragīta-praṇayini ruruce tāta tad-bhā visābhā***

*śrī-īśa—*for Kṛṣṇa (“the master of opulence”); *prītiḥ—*love; *sva—*own; *nāma—*of His name; *ākṛti—*and of His form; *kathanam—*the glorification; *vinā—*without; *abhāva—*of liberation (“absence of continuity of transmigration”); *pakṣe—*on the side; *na—*is not; *vidyā—*of knowledge; *āmoda—*for the fragrance; *śraddhā—*of belief (i.e strong desire); *kalāpāt—*because of an abundance; *api—*also \[is not\]; *sukha-da—*which makes happy; *mithaḥ—*of mutual; *bhāva—*love; *sāmrājyataḥ—*because of the supreme authority; *ca—*and \[it is not\]; *ramyā—*\[effulgence\] which is beautiful; *ramya-sthala—*in Vṛndāvana (“a beautiful place”); *stha—*who stay; *prasāra—*of the multitude; *mada—*intoxication of love; *kalā—*expertise; *moda—*joy; *lakṣmī—*grace; *sameta—*which is endowed with; *prema—*the love; *āsanna—*follows; *pragīta—*whose singing; *praṇayini—*those who are affectionate; *ruruce—*pleased; *tāta—*O my dear disciple; *tat—*of them; *bhā—*the effulgence; *visa—*a lotus stalk; *ābhā—*resembles.

**O my dear disciple! Love for the Lord that is devoid of the glorification of His name and form is not on the path of liberation. It doesn’t arise for those who want fame, nor for those who expect a reciprocation of their attachment to Him. Love for Kṛṣṇa truly pleases the affectionate devotees. Their splendor is beautiful like the sheen of a lotus stem. They sing about the Vṛndāvana residents’ love for Him,which is endowed with grace, joy, expertise, and intoxicated rapture.**

### Text 100

***atra ca—***  
***śrī-nātha-pāda-pāthoja-***  
***rasa-lālasa-cetasā***  
***bhāvitā tata-moda-stha-***  
***rasā su-kavinā kṛtiḥ***

*śrī-nātha—*of Śrī Nātha \[Cakravartī\]; *pāda—*feet; *pāthaḥ-ja—*because of the lotus (“born from water”); *rasa—*for *rasa; lālasa—*is endowed with the desire; *cetasā—*whose mind; *bhāvitā—*will be caused to become; *tata—*expanded; *moda—*in bliss; *stha—*abides; *rasā—*that in which the *rasa; su-kavinā—*by the expert poet; *kṛtiḥ—*the work (i.e this book).

**The expert** ***kavi*** **shall expand the bliss of the** ***rasa*** **in his** ***kāvya*** **scripture. This poet’s desire to experience** ***rasa*** **has arisen as a result of serving the lotus feet of Śrī Nātha.**

*iti ślokāntaram.* Therefore another verse is presented.

### Text 101

*ekākṣara-pādo yathā—* Each line has only one consonant.

***śaṁ-śīḥ śaśī śaśāśāśāṁ***  
***pāpo ’papāpa-paḥ papiḥ***  
***lolo lalāla līlā-līṁ***  
***yayā yaṁ yo ’yayā yayau***

*śam—*toward \[her\] happiness; *śīḥ—*\[the moon,\] which shows spite; *śaśī—*the moon (“which has the mark of a hare”); *śaśāśa—*jumped; *āśām—*to \[another\] direction (or, wish); *pāpaḥ—*wicked; *apapāpa—*sinless people (“those from whom sin has departed”); *paḥ—*withers(i.e renders incapable of action); *papiḥ—*the moon; *lolaḥ—*Kṛṣṇa(“who thirsts for the *gopīs*”); *lalāla—*desired; *līlā—*during pastimes; *līm—*\[He desired\] a girl who embraces; *yayā—*because of which \[wish\]; *yam—*\[caused\] whom (i.e Kṛṣṇa); *yaḥ—*which \[moon\]; *ayayā—*with her (“who wasn’t going”); *yayau—*caused \[Kṛṣṇa\] to attain \[union\].

\[A *gopī* just about to go meet Kṛṣṇa at night sees the moon rising unexpectedly. The narrator comments as follows:\]

**The moon has suddenly shifted to an another direction. It shows contempt for this** ***gopī’s*** **happiness. The wicked moon renders her incapable of action, and it annihilates her innermost aspiration. Kṛṣṇa craves the touch of young girls. He desires a** ***gopī*** **who will embrace Him during play. On this night however, the moon is causing Kṛṣṇa to unite with this yearning** ***gopī*** **in her heart.**

### Text 102

***ekākṣaro yathā—***  
***nā nānā nānino ’nenā***  
***nānānenānanaṁ nu nuḥ***  
***nūnaṁ nonān nṝn anūnān***  
***anu na nun-nun nūnninīḥ***

*nā—*the Supreme Person; *nānā—*many; *na—*not; *aninaḥ—*has no master; *anenāḥ—*is without sin; *nānā—*various; *anena—*by him; *ananam—*life; *nu—*indeed; *nuḥ—*of the living entity; *nūnam—*assuredly; *na—*not; *ūnān—*tiny; *nṝn—*persons; *anūnān—*not tiny; *anu—*coming from; *na—*not; *nut—*the glorification; *nut—*dispels (i.e ignores); *nu—*uncertain; *unninīḥ—*constantly leads upwards, (*ūrdhvaṁ nitarāṁ nayati*).

**The Supreme Person is not manifold. He has no master. Everyone exists because of Him. He is not at fault for creating this diversified universe. Certainly the Lord doesn’t ignore humans or demigods praising Him. There is no doubt that the Lord guides them to higher destinations.**

### Text 103

*siṁhāvaloka-ślokāntara-garbho yathā—*

This is an example of a verse containing a verse, according to the logic named “the lion’s glance” (i.e taking a look back). \[The verse it contains is shown afterwards.\]

***tejaḥ kiñcana tat-tad asya satataṁ navyāmbudābhaṁ bhaja***  
***snigdhaṁ locana-lobhadaṁ caturatā-līlā-vilāsāvali***  
***antaś-cintayatāṁ rasasya saraṇiṁ śrī-rādhikā-prauḍhima-***  
***premārdraṁ rucira-cchavi smaravatīṁ krīḍāṁ dadhad dhāmasu***

*tejaḥ—*the effulgence; *kiñcana—*to some degree; *tat—*which is His; *tat—*is Brahman; *asya—*His \[effulgence\]; *satatam—*always; *navya—*new; *ambuda—*of a cloud; *ābham—*which has the sheen; *bhaja—*worship; *snigdham—*in such a way that there is affection; *locana—*to the eyes; *lobha-dam—*gives greed; *caturatā—*endowed with expertise; *līlā-vilāsa—*of playful pastimes; *āvali—*because of which \[effulgence\] there is a multitude; *antaḥ-cintayatām—*for people (“who are thinking internally (i.e with their hearts)”); *rasasya—*of *rasa; saraṇim—*is the path; *śrī-rādhikā—*of Śrī Rādhikā; *prauḍhima—*on account of the boldness; *prema—*love; *ārdram—*flowing with; *rucira—*is attractive; *chavi—*the splendor of which; *smaravatīm—*which contain Cupid; *krīḍām—*pastimes; *dadhat—*is nourishing; *dhāmasu—*in abodes (i.e in arbors).

**Always worship Kṛṣṇa’s effulgence with love. It resembles the sheen of a new raincloud. It’s the source of the** ***brahmajyoti.*** **Hari’s splendor attracts one’s eyes. It is the way of** ***rasa*** **for those who think with their hearts. It’s very beautiful and the source of many playful pastimes. It nourishes the love games in the arbors. His brilliance flows with love on account of the boldness of Śrīmatī Rādhikā.**

### Text 104

***atra—***  
***navāmbudābhaṁ bhaja tat-tad asya līlā-vilāsāvali-lobhaś ca***  
***śrī-rādhikā-prauḍhi-matāṁ rasasya krīḍāṁ dadhad dhāma sura-cchavi sma***

*nava—*new; *ambuda—*of a cloud; *ābham—*that which has the splendor; *bhaja—*worship; *tat—*which is His; *tat—*is Brahman; *asya—*His \[effulgence\]; *līlā-vilāsa—*of playful pastimes; *āvali—*for a multitude; *lobhaḥ—*because of which \[effulgence\] there is greed; *ca—*and; *śrī-rādhikā—*of Śrī Rādhikā; *prauḍhi—*because of the boldness; *matām—*which are celebrated; *rasasya—*of *rasa; krīḍām—*pastimes; *dadhat—*which is nourishing; *dhāma—*the effulgence; *sura—*of demigods; *chavi—*because of which \[effulgence\] there is the splendor; *sma—*was (*bhavati sma*).

**Worship Kṛṣṇa’s effulgence. The sun god and other demigods shine because of it. It’s the source of the** ***brahmajyoti.*** **It resembles the sheen of a new raincloud. By seeing Kṛṣṇa’s splendor, one becomes greedy to participate in His playful pastimes. His beauty sustains the games of** ***rasa,*** **which are celebrated because of Śrīmatī Rādhikā’s display of boldness.**

***iti ślokāntara-garbhaḥ.***

Here ends a verse that contains another verse.

### Text 105

*punar-uktavad-ābhāsaḥ punar-uktavad eva yaḥ.*

**What is called** ***punar-uktavad-ābhāsa*** **(“a semblance of a repetition”) is just like a repetition.**

### Text 106

\[In this verse the author introduces a factor, called *punar-uktavad-ābhāsa,* in the production of an *arthālaṅkāra,* which he will expand upon in the next verse.\]

***tava tanu-śarīra-sadṛśīṁ kāñcana kanakasya vīrudhaṁ nekṣe***  
***rādhe su-mukhi bhavatyā mukhavad-anaṅko ’pi śubhrāṁśuḥ***

*tava—*to Yours; *tanu—*thin; *śarīra—*body; *sadṛśīm—*similar; *kāñcana—*any; *kanakasya—*of gold; *vīrudham—*creeper; *na īkṣe—*I don’t see; *rādhe—*O Rādhā; *su-mukhi—*O girl with a beautiful face; *bhavatyāḥ—*of Your good self; *mukha-vad—*like the face; *anaṅkaḥ—*stainless (i.e immaculate); *api—*even; *śubhra-aṁśuḥ—*the moon (“whose rays are beautiful”).

**O Rādhā! I don’t see any golden creeper that resembles Your thin body. O beloved, Your visage is so beautiful! Even the moon doesn’t compare with Your immaculate face.**

### Text 107

*ayaṁ śabdārthaṁ niṣṭho ’pi bhavati. yathā—*

*Punar-uktavad-ābhāsa* (“a semblance of a repetition”) occurs when it’s based on the meanings of the words (i.e when adjacent synonyms are not used as such). For example:

***ghana-jalada-rucira-sundara-dhāma mahaḥ-sañcayaugha-liptāśaḥ***  
***vidhur amṛta-kara-kalā-nidhir eṣa nabhaḥ-puṣkarākāśaḥ***

\[Two interpretations\]

*ghana—*dense; *jalada—*like a cloud (or with clouds); *rucira—*and radiant; *sundara—*is beautiful; *dhāma—*O you whose body; *mahaḥ—*of majesty (or of splendor); *sañcaya—*of a multitude; *ogha—*with a flood; *lipta—*are covered (or are connected); *āśaḥ—*whose aspirations (or, by whom the directions); *vidhuḥ—*Kṛṣṇa (or the moon); *amṛta-kara—*whose hand is nectar (or, whose rays are nectar); *kalā-nidhiḥ—*an ocean of cleverness (or, the receptacle of sixteen digits ); *eṣaḥ—*\[you are\] this \[*vidhu*\]; *nabhaḥ—*during Śrāvaṇa (i.e the rainy season), (or, in the sky); *puṣkara—*is like the sky (or, is like a \[white\] lotus); *ākāśaḥ—*whose perfect manifestation (or, whose perfect radiance \[in the sky\]).

**O Kṛṣṇa! Your body is beautiful and radiant like a rain cloud; it’s a perfect manifestation that resembles the sky during the rainy season. Your overwhelming majestic mood prevents You from revealing Your desires. O Lord, relieve me of my unhappiness! Your hands are the form of nectar, and You are an ocean of cleverness.**

**Dear moon! You appear beautiful and radiant amidst the dense clouds.**

**You flood the directions with your splendor. You change through the sixteen digits, and your rays are like nectar. Your reflection in a clear lake resembles a white lotus.**

*atra śabdavad-artho ’pi punar-uktavad ābhāsate, na tv asya paunar-uktyam, nabhāḥ śrāvaṇas tat-sambandhi puṣkaraṁ vyoma, tadvat ā samyak kāśaḥ prakāśo yasya sa tatheti, kṛṣṇa-pakṣe ’nenaiva śyāmatvopalabdheḥ. punar ghana-jaladety-ādinā punar-uktavat śyāmatvam ity arthaḥ pratibhāsate, na tu punar-uktaḥ. vastutas tu nabhaḥ-puṣkarākāśatvasya sādhyasya ghana-jaladety-ādi hetu-bhūtam. evaṁ candra-pakṣe ’pi. tenokta-rūpa-śabda-parivṛtti-vṛttāv api vidhur iti nānārthasya śabdasya sthitau tathā-vidhārthālaṅkārasya sthitatvād arthālaṅkārasya prādhānyam.*

In this verse, although a meaning conveyed by a word appears to be repeated, it is not. For instance, *nabha* means the sky, as well as the month of Śrāvaṇa in the sense of what is connected to it (i.e the rainy season); *puṣkara* also means the sky; and the following word *ākāśa* also means the sky, but these words aren’t used in the same sense at the same time. *Ākāśa* means “He whose manifestation (*kāśa*) is perfect (*ā*).”

With this in mind, it’s understood that when *ākāśa* refers to Kṛṣṇa it signifies His being dark blue. Moreover it appears that the meaning of His being dark blue is repeated by the use of the words *ghana* and *jalada,* both meaning “rain cloud,” but there is no repetition. Actually, the words *ghana, jalada,* and so on are the causes which effect the particular meanings of *nabha, puṣkara,* and *ākāśa.* The same understanding applies in regard to the meanings related to the moon.

Therefore when there is usage of a word, such as *vidhu,* that has multiple meanings and there is an *arthālaṅkāra* that is established in the same way as was described \[in 7.75 and *vṛtti* 7.74\], that *arthālaṅkāra* is predominant, even when there is a substitution of a word whose distinctive feature was previously mentioned.

***iti śrīmad-alaṅkāra-kaustubhe śabdālaṅkāra-nirṇayo nāma saptamaḥ kiraṇaḥ.***

**Here ends the seventh chapter of** ***Alaṅkāra-kaustubha*** **called “a discussion on the ornaments of sound.”**

## Appendix

The following verse from *Gopāla-campū* (*pūrva* 31.45) shows examples of *sa-bhaṅga-śleṣa* (*Alaṅkāra-kaustubha* 7.4):

***kadācic ca kasyāścid dūtyā saha kṛṣṇasya vākovākyam—***  
***iyaṁ kā strī strītvaṁ bhavati katham īṣat-padam idam***  
***kim asthāne siddhaṁ tava vacanam asthānakam idam***  
***na kākor udbhūtaṁ bhavati tad aho kākur iha kā***  
***tad evaṁ dūtī-vāg jayati hariṇākṣyā harim anu***

*iyam kā—*who is this; *strī—*woman; *strītvam—*womanhood; *bhavati—*is; *katham—*why; *īṣat—*signifying smallness; *padam idam—*this word; *kim—*what; *asthāne—*improperly; *siddham—*was done; *tava—*of yours; *vacanam—*speech; *asthānakam—*unsuitable; *idam—*this; *na—*not; *kākoḥ—*from *kāku* (“intonation”); *udbhūtam bhavati—*has arisen; *tat—*that; *aho—*how amazing; *kākuḥ—*the intonation; *iha—*here; *kā—*what is; *tat—*that (dialogue); *evam—*was in this way; *dūtī—*of the messenger; *vāk—*the speech; *jayati—*may it be glorious; *hariṇa-akṣyāḥ—*of the messenger (“whose eyes are like a doe’s”); *harim anu—*to Hari.

Or, by double meaning:***iyaṁ kā-strī****—*who is this little girl?; ***kimaḥ sthāne siddham****—*it is made properly from the word *kim*; ***na kā koḥ udbhūtam bhavati****—*the word *kā* has not come from the word *ku.*

**Once, a dialogue occured between Kṛṣṇa and the messenger of a particular** ***gopī:***

**Kṛṣṇa: Who is this lady?**

**Messenger (thinking He said,** ***iyaṁ kā-strī:*** **“Who is this little girl?”): She is a girl, all right, but why do you use a diminutive term?**

**Kṛṣṇa: I made the word** ***kā*** **from** ***kim.***

**Messenger (thinking He said,** ***kim asthāne siddham:*** **“What did I do wrong?”): Your statement is out of place.**

**Kṛṣṇa: But the word** ***kā*** **has not come from the** ***avyaya ku*****!**

**Messenger (thinking He said,** ***na kākor udbhūtaṁ bhavati:*** **“It has not arisen from** ***kāku*****”): Well that’s surprising. Still, I wonder what the intonation is in this case!**

**All glories to the words spoken to Hari by the doe-eyed messenger.**

This is in reference to Mammaṭa, the author of *Kāvya-prakāśa,* who says *dhvaniḥ kāvyam* (“*dhvani* is *kāvya*”). Kavi Karṇapūra argues that this definition must be interpreted either in *karaṇe prayoga* or through *lakṣaṇā vṛtti* (see 1.12).

Viśvanātha says that, if we accept the first interpretation, then *dhvani* would refer to knowledge of the suggested meaning, if we accept the second interpretation, then *dhvani* would refer to *kāvya,* and if we accept the third interpretation, then *dhvani* would refer to the person who is the location of the knowledge of the suggested meaning (i.e the reader).

*Vastu* is “the thing which is talked about.” For example, in *gaṅgāyāṁ ghoṣaḥ, śaityatva*(coolness) and *pāvanatva* (purity) are the *vastus* that are suggested. Thus *vastu* means “idea.”

*Śabalatvaṁ tu bhāvānāṁ sammardaḥ syāt parasparam,* “When many *bhāvas* are mixed with each other, the state is called *bhāva-śābalya*” (*Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu* 2.4.244).

*Tat-jani* is a *yasmāt saḥ bahuvrīhi* (“that from which there is a production of a suggestion”), and *tat-janitve* means *tat-janitve iti sthite* (“when it is established that (i.e given the fact that) *kāvya* is that from which there is a production of a suggestion”). Thus Kavi Karṇapūra agrees with Mammaṭa’s definition of *kāvya,* through *lakṣaṇā vṛtti.*

*Jahat-svārthā* is a figurative usage where the word loses its original meaning, and *ajahat-svārthā* is a figurative usage where the word doesn’t lose its original meaning (2.14-15).

In this verse, each word that is repeated has a different meaning. The meter is *hariṇī,* in which there is a pause after the sixth and the tenth syllables.

The plural is used out of high regard (HNV 622).

The second word *adhara* is formed in the following way: *tat karoti \[ṇ\]i* is applied after *adhara* in the sense of “making something inferior,” *pac-āder a\[t\]* is then added after the new *dhātu adhari,* and *\[ṇ\]i* disappears by the *sūtra ṇer haro ’ṇid-ādau rāma-dhātuke.* The unmentioned *dhvani* here is that Rādhikā may sometimes belittle Kṛṣṇa.

The meaning of each first word is literal. However, there is no desire to express the literal meaning of *phala* (“fruit”) in the second word*.* By *vyañjanā vṛtti,* the second *phala* does not refer to a fruit but to a suggestion of contempt, which arises from a figurative meaning of *phala* (i.e that fruits are not always sweet). Thus the sense of each repeated word in this verse goes from *abhidhā vṛtti* to *lakṣaṇā vṛtti* to *vyañjanā vṛtti.*

The first word *adhara* is the *upameya* (“that which is compared, the subject of comparison”). The words *phala, sitā* and so on are the *upamānas* (“that which the *upameya* is compared to, the object of comparison”). The suggestion of praise arises from a figurative meaning of the second word *adhara* (“that which makes everything lower”), thus this *dhvani* is called *lakṣaṇā-mūla* (“which has its roots in *lakṣaṇā vṛtti*”).

Viśvanātha: The first word *mānasa* has a literal sense (“heart”); the second word is figuratively used in the sense of *mānasa-pada-vācya* (“that which is actually referred to by the word *mānasa*”). The idea is “*that* heart is a real heart.” There is no desire to express the literal meaning in the second *mānasa.* The suggestion, which arises from *lakṣaṇā vṛtti,* is that such a heart is praiseworthy.

The form *atāni* is not used in consideration of the meter. The form *atāni* (*bhūteśa karmaṇi* 1.1 of *tan\[u\]* ) is mentioned in *Bṛhad-dhātu-kusumākara.*

The difference between *viparīta-lakṣaṇa* and *kāku* is that in the former the literal meaning doesn’t make sense, and therefore irony is always expressed. Moreover, Viśvanātha comments on this verse as follows: The *gopī* is actually saying: “You don’t feel at home in my house; it’s like another’s house for You.” Viśvanātha adds that what is suggested by all these opposite meanings is that Kṛṣṇa has no love for that *gopī,* and this *vyañjanā vṛtti* arises from a figurative sense, which is totally opposite to the literal meaning since the latter makes no sense in this context. Thus it is clear that the literal meaning, i.e good fortune and so on, is obliterated. This is *jahat-svārtha-lakṣaṇā.*

Viśvanātha: *vastv-alaṅkārādi-rūpa-vyaṅgyārthānāṁ hṛdaye utpatty-antardhāna-rūpaḥ kramaḥ sarveṣāṁ lakṣya ity arthaḥ:* “This *krama,* in the form of the rising and the disappearance in the heart of the suggested meanings in the form of a *vastu* or an *alaṅkāra* is perceivable by everyone.”

Viśvanātha: *rasādi-rūpa-vyaṅgyārthānāṁ hṛdaye utpatty-antardhāna-rūpa-kramo na lakṣya ity arthaḥ:* “This *krama,* in the form of the rising and the disappearance in the heart of the suggested meanings in the form of *rasa* and so on, isn’t perceivable.” The suggestion of the meaning is so fast that the rising and the disappearance in the heart isn’t perceived.

Viśvanātha: *vibhavādibhir vyajyamāno yasyotpatty-antardhāna-kramaḥ, sa rasa eva, na tu vibhāvādaya ity arthaḥ:* “That whose order of appearance and disappearance is suggested by the *vibhāva* and so on is *rasa;* it’s not the *vibhāva* and so on themselves.” In this regard, *vibhāva* is twofold: i) *uddīpana* (“stimuli” (i.e “that which causes emotions to flare up”)), and ii) *ālambana* (“the support” (i.e the *viṣaya* and the *āśraya* of a relationship)).

Their coming and going are so quick that they (i.e the coming and the going) are not perceived. Thus *akrama,* which corresponds to *alakṣya-vyaṅgya-krama,* refers to all the types of *dhvanis* except *vastu* and *alaṅkāra* (*lakṣya-krama-vyaṅgya*). The latter is explained in this chapter whereas the former will be explained in chapter five.

This is Kavi Karṇapūra’s way of saying that the term *alakṣya-vyaṅgya-krama,* which was previously analyzed as a *ṣaṣṭhī-kṛṣṇapuruṣa* turned into a *pītāmbara,* can be taken as a *rāma-kṛṣṇa* compound turned into a *pītāmbara.* In this case, the *na\[ñ\]* in *alakṣya* modifies both *lakṣya* and *krama* on account of the compound being originally a *rāma-kṛṣṇa.*

*Śramaṇa* is a synonym of “*avadhūta.*” Since *avadhūtas* are outside of the *varṇāśrama* system, it seems contradictory to call a *śramaṇa* a “*brāhmaṇa.*” But by considering that he was previously a *brāhmaṇa,* the designation makes sense. The conclusion is that *dhvani* is merely the container of the *alaṅkāra;* it’s not the *alaṅkāra* itself, yet it is called an *alaṅkāra.* The *dhvani* leads to an *alaṅkāra.* In this connection, Viśvanātha glosses *ālaṅkārya* as *alaṅkārāśraya.*

Viśvanātha says *preyaḥ* means *abhīṣṭam.*

In the second case the poet himself is speaking. In the third case some character created by the poet is speaking.

*Śarīraṁ śabdārthau,* “Words and their meanings are the body of *kāvya*” (1.5).

If the *dhvani* is based on a common scenario where nothing is out of the ordinary, thenit is of the first kind (*svayam-udbhāvī*). But when it is based on a situation that exceeds the limits of reality then it is of the second or third kind, depending on whether the poets is speaking through his own voice or through the voice of a character he is describing.

*Kāvya* is superexcellent when one implied meaning gives rise to another implied meaning (see *vṛtti* 1.17). Now, instead of giving separate examples of the six kinds that involve a single dhvani, Kavi Karṇapūra will just give examples of the twelve kinds where a *dhvani* arises out of a *dhvani.*

This first *dhvani* arises from the the above-mentioned implied meanings of the words *svāmī* and *gomān.* Thus it is an example of what was taught in 2.34 and 2.36. Similarly, the second *dhvani* arises from the first *dhvani* and thus it is also an example of the same.

*Kuṅkuma-paṅka* and *candana-paṅka* are types of ointments. A *nīvi-bandha* is a petticoat but it also refers to the ends of that cloth tied into a knot in front of the body. A *keśa-bandha* is a hair-band, and a *raśanā-bandha* is a knot made with the ends of a girdle.

Lotuses are called *kañja* because they grow in water (*kam-ja*). The compound *kañja-nāla-dala* literally means “the petals on the stalks of lotuses.” In this way, Kṛṣṇa is compared to an elephant since He damages the lotus-petal like lips of the *gopīs* when He kisses them.

The four kinds of *svataḥ-sambhavī* are as follows: i) a *dhvani* that is a *vastu* gives rise to a *dhvani* that is an *alaṅkāra,* ii) a *dhvani* that is a *vastu* gives rise to a *dhvani* that is another *vastu,* iii) a *dhvani* that is an *alaṅkāra* gives rise to a *dhvani* that is a *vastu,* and iv) a *dhvani* that is an *alaṅkāra* gives rise to a *dhvani* that is another *alaṅkāra.* The same four categories should be inferred for the other two categories, i.e *kaveḥ prauḍhokti-niṣpanna* and *vaktus tat-kalpitasya prauḍhokti-niṣpanna* (3.15), making a total of twelve categories.

*Viśvanātha:* Sweetness is not a substance; it cannot fall down in showers. Therefore, since this statement wouldn’t make sense in day-to-day life, it isn’t *svataḥ-sambhavī* (“something that occurs naturally”). It’s just the *kavi’s* concoction.

Here *kim* is used in the sense of *vitarka. Medinī-koṣa* says, *kim kutsāyāṁ vitarke ca niṣedha-praśnayor api:* “*Kim* is used in the sense of contempt, conjecture, negation, and asking a question.”

*Akṛta* is the *bhūteśa ātmapada* 1.1 form of *\[ḍu\]kṛ\[ñ\] karaṇe* in *kartari prayoga.*

This half-woman, half-man form of Lord Śiva is known as Ardha-nārīśvara.

This half-Viṣṇu, half-Śiva form is known as Hari-Hara.

*Viśvanātha: nanu deha-sattve mahādevasya parameśvaratvena dehasya nityatvān na nāśa-sambhavaḥ, ataḥ kavi-prauḍhoktiḥ,* “This verse constitutes the uncommon words of a poet because, since Śiva is an aspect of the Lord and his body is eternal, his body cannot perish.” (Moreover, transcendental pastimes don’t occur in ordinary life, therefore statements of that nature are automatically in the category of *kavi-prauḍhokti.* Kavi Karṇapūra didn’t create a separate category for transcendental pastimes because he is using the parameters of his predecessors, such as Mammaṭa, Viśvanātha Kavirāja, and Bharata Muni.)

The statements in this verse are considered uncommon simply because transcendental bodies made of love don’t exist in ordinary life.

The apostrophe indicates poetic license. This type of deletion in consideration of the meter is common in Bengali poetry, but not usually allowable in Sanskrit.

In this case *anu* is a *kṛṣṇa-pravacanīya* used in the sense of *vīpsā* (“pervasion”) (HNV 672).

*Viśva-prakāśa* says, *ātmā deha-mano-brahma-svabhāva-dhṛti-buddhiṣu prayatne ca:* “The word *ātmā* is used means body; mind; Brahman; one’s nature; firmness; intelligence; and effort.” *Amara-koṣa* (3.3.109) says, *ātmā yatno dhṛtir buddhiḥ svabhāvo brahma varṣma ca,* where the word *varṣman* means “body.”

*Viśvanātha:* The statement in this verse is *prauḍhokti* because, by worldly estimation, it’s impossible for one person to be everywhere simultaneously.

Monier-Williams Dictionary says that the *alaṅkāras* called *hetu* and *kāvya-liṅga* are synonymous. The poetic ornament *kāvya-liṅga* was mentioned in *vṛtti* 3.20.

Thus there is no *uttamottama dhvani* (“a suggestion arising from another suggestion”) in this category.

*Śyāmayate* is made with *tat-karoti \[ṇ\]i*, and the *ātmapada* endings are used here since *ṇy-anta-dhātus* are *ubhayapadī.*

*Viśvanātha:* What is suggested in this verse is the *alaṅkāra* called *upamā,* a comparison between clouds and Kṛṣṇa, and there is no further suggestion. (The comparison is merely suggested because the word *iva* hasn’t been used in the verse.) This suggestion arises because of the words’ power, since the suggesting is done through words that cannot tolerate being substituted, i.e *balākā, jīvana, tviṣām,* and *śyāmayate.* The suggestion also arises by the power of the meanings because the suggesting is done by meanings that are produced from adjectives, which can be changed, such as *aśeṣa-santāpa-hara* and *tanu-bhṛtām.* With the words beginning *para-mate* in the *vṛtti,* Kavi Karṇapūra refers to some poets who, with difficulty, posit the existence of *vastus* and *alaṅkāras* in this case also.

Months are named after the *nakṣatra* in which the full moon occurs. Thus the month of Vaiśākha (April—May) is when the full moon occurs during the constellation *viśākhā* (See HNV 1131-1132). It is well-known that the *viśākhā* constellation is also called *rādhā. Amara-koṣa* (1.4.16) says, *vaiśākhe mādhavo rādhaḥ:* “Both Mādhava and Rādha mean Vaiśākha.” The *pratyaya keśava \[ṇ\]a* is applied after *rādhā,* and in this way Rādha is a synonym of Vaiśākha.

Once again there is only one suggestion. Because of *śleṣa* (“double meaning”), which arises from *śabda-śakti,* there is a comparison (*upamālaṅkāra*) between Kṛṣṇa and the month of Vaiśākha. This suggestion arises by the power of these words, substituting which cannot be tolerated: *madhu, sumanas, rādhādy-apara-paryāya,* and *mādhava.* The suggestion also arises by the power of the meanings (*artha-śakti*) since the suggesting is done by meanings that are produced from adjectives, which can be changed, such as *anta-kṛt, āmoda, ghrāṇa,* and so on.

This category will be dealt with in chapter five.

The three categories of *artha-śakti-bhū dhvani* are: i) *svataḥ-sambhavī,* ii) *kavi-prauḍhokti,* and iii) *kavi-nibaddha-vaktṛ-prauḍhokti* (3.15). Each one is fourfold: i) a *dhvani* that is a *vastu* gives rise to a *dhvani* that is an *alaṅkāra,* ii) a *dhvani* that is a *vastu* gives rise to a *dhvani* that is another *vastu,* iii) a *dhvani* that is an *alaṅkāra* gives rise to a *dhvani* that is a *vastu,* and iv) a *dhvani* that is an *alaṅkāra* gives rise to a *dhvani* that is another *alaṅkāra.*

*Ketakī* flowers are very fragrant.

This verse is an example of *lakṣaṇa-mūla-dhvani* that is *arthāntara-saṅkramita,* i.e the literal meaning transforms into another meaning (3.3-4), where the *dhvani* is based on a word, not on a sentence, i.e each word that is repeated has a different meaning. The difference with the verse beginning *phalam api phalam* (3.5) is that in that verse there was a comparison with Rādhikā’s lips, in the context of a *vākya* (“statement”).

*Viśvanātha:* What is suggested by the figurative meaning of *pārṣada* (i.e always at the side) is that the Lord’s associates are the best of all kinds of associates. Similarly, in all cases the figurative meaning leads to an implied meaning of “better than anything of its kind.”

*Viśvanātha:* By *viparīta-lakṣaṇā* (the figurative meaning of a word that it is directly opposite to its literally meaning) the word anukampā means “lack of mercy,” and thus it is suggested that, since lack of mercy is unique to Kṛṣṇa, He is a bad person on account of his hard-heartedness. Similarly, by *viparīta-lakṣaṇā* (also called *viruddha-lakṣaṇā*) *daurjanyam* means “being a good person,” and thus it is suggested that, since goodness is unique only to her, she is better. Similarly, by *viparīta-lakṣaṇā* her love is deep, His love is shallow, and she is not dear to Him, which suggests He shouldn’t come near her.

The word *mada* has a specific meaning in relation to elephants; the frontal lobes of a male elephant contain *mada,* which is the fluid that exudes from the temples of an elephant in rut. In due course of time, this *mada,* under the influence of the moon, hardens into pearls.

The first, third, and fourth lines of this verse are taken as one statement (*vākya*). There is no need to refer to the other statement (“don’t put your faith in Him”) to obtain the contrasts just mentioned in the *vṛtti.*

Concerning the meaning of *aneka-pa* in the sense of *anekena pibati:* the *kṛt-pratyaya \[k\]a* is applied after the *dhātu pā pāne* (HNV 828), but because *kṛt-pratyayas* are applied variously (*bahula*), the *pūrva-pada* is a *karaṇa* here instead of a *karma*.

*Viśvanātha:* One of the suggestions is that, upon seeing her innocence, the Kṛṣṇa elephant will become bold and naughty, but Kṛṣṇa isn’t always like that. In this way, what is described in the *sambodhana* (*mugdhe*) is the *sādhana* (“the means”) that causes Kṛṣṇa’s special quality of boldness to manifest. Similarly, hearing that she is like a lotus, Kṛṣṇa will become like an elephant. Because of this *vastu* (i.e her qualities are the *sādhanas* that effect the *sādhya* (i.e the manifestation of particular qualities of His)), there is another *vastu:* since Kṛṣṇa is loosely behaved, He will certainly crush her \[in amorous play\]. In this way there is also a *hetv-alaṅkāra.*

I.e The words *padmini* and *aneka-pa* cannot be changed; the *dhvanis* are dependent on them. Thus, this verse is an instance of *uttamottama dhvani:* one *vastu* suggests another *vastu* and *alaṅkāras.* The designation *padminī* refers to the best of the four categories of women. It also means “a female elephant.”

This is because the other girls are aware of their bodies during union. The implication is that this group leader must be experiencing much more bliss than the other girls since she forgets her body during union.

It’s *svataḥ-sambhavī* because there is no statement that is out of the ordinary. This verse expresses a simple *dhvani* that is a *vastu.*

Kavi Karṇapūra is not giving examples of all seventeen kinds of *pada-dhvani* because he has already given examples of all eighteen kinds of *vākya-dhvani.*

There are eighteen varieties of *vākya-dhvani.* Because a suggestion arising from *ubhaya-śakti* can occur only in a sentence, there are seventeen kinds of *pada-dhvani* (3.29-31).

*Viśvanātha:* A *prabandha* is a fictional story of four or five verses.

The twelve categories of suggestions arising from *artha-śakti* are as follows. First, there are three main categories: i) *svataḥ-sambhavī,* ii) *kaveḥ prauḍhokti-niṣpanna,* and iii) *vaktus tat-kalpitasya prauḍhokti-niṣpanna* (3.15). Each main category is fourfold: i) a *dhvani* that is a *vastu* gives rise to a *dhvani* that is an *alaṅkāra,* ii) a *dhvani* that is a *vastu* gives rise to a *dhvani* that is another *vastu,* iii) a *dhvani* that is an *alaṅkāra* gives rise to a *dhvani* that is a *vastu,* and iv) a *dhvani* that is an *alaṅkāra* gives rise to a *dhvani* that is another *alaṅkāra.*

Here the *prabandha* is a fictional story of four verses. In each verse, the speaker is different.

*Viśvanātha:* The four conversations are as follows. First, the young brides speak to the grand-daughter of the old nurse. Second, the grand-daughter of the old nurse and the old lady speak. Third, the grand-daughter of the old nurse relates the message to Mother Yaśodā. Fourth, Kṛṣṇa speaks to Yaśodā. (The conversation that occurs when the grand-daughter of the old nurse relates to the young brides Kṛṣṇa’s words to Yaśodā is included in the first category.)

The *gopīs* are hiding their intentions. They want to meet Kṛṣṇa and thus, on the plea of sending news about the milking problem, they are arranging with Kṛṣṇa the proper time to meet Him.

*Viśvanātha:* There is an instance of an *atiśayokty-alaṅkāra* because first the cows give milk, then some time later Kṛṣṇa milks them. Thus there is a reverse order between the cause and the effect.

This *prabandha* is an example of suggestions that arise naturally by the power of the meanings (*artha-śakti-bhū svataḥ-sambhavī dhvani*).

By the word *varṇa,* specifically the three groups of letters are implied: i) those which suggest sweetness (*mādhurya*), ii) those which suggest vigor (*ojas*), and iii) those which suggest tranquillity (*prasāda*). This will be elaborated upon in chapter six.

Different styles such as favoring the use of compounds and so on. This will be explained in chapter nine.

*Viśvanātha:* The word *rasa* in this context doesn’t refer just to *rasa* but to the category of *asaṁlakṣya-krama* in general. In this way, only *rasa* is *asaṁlakṣya-krama* in general. The understanding of the *rasa* is conveyed through sentences, a word, a part of a word, a particular phoneme or style of composition, but *vastus* and *alaṅkāras* are *asaṁlakṣya-krama* only when the understanding is conveyed by a part of a word, a particular phoneme or style of composition \[on account of the subtle nature of the suggestion\]. The sequence of appearance and disappearance of *vastus* and *alaṅkāras* is perceivable when the understanding is conveyed through a sentence (*vākya*) or a word (*pada*).

There are two kinds of *prakṛti: nāmas* and *dhātus.*

The four kinds of relationships are: i) *sva-svāmī,* ii) *janya-janaka,* iii) *avayavāvayavī,* and iv) *sthāny-ādeśa* (HNV *vṛtti* 627).

*Eka-vacana, dvi-vacana,* and *bahu-vacana.*

For example, instead of the clause *vayaṁ gacchāmaḥ,* the words would be *vayaṁ gacchanti.* This type of incongruity is often seen in the Vedas.

An *adhikaraṇa* becomes a *karma* by one of these two *sūtras,* i) *adhi-śīṅ-sthāsām ādhāraḥ karma, abhiniviśo vā* (HNV 650), and ii) *upānv-adhy-āṅbhyo vasa ādhāraḥ karma* (HNV 651).

Now, Kavi Karṇapūra shows how the *dhvani* that comes from part of a word (*padāṁśa*), in this case a base (*prakṛti*), has a sequence of appearance and disappearance that isn’t perceivable (*asaṁlakṣya-krama*). The topic is still suggestions in the form of *vastus* and *alaṅkāras.*

*Viśvanātha:* The first suggestion is the idea (*vastu*) that Rādhā’s *māna* is purposely produced out of a desire to joke with Kṛṣṇa. Therefore the second idea that arises is that this māna is not natural but artificial, since Kṛṣṇa hasn’t committed any offense. Because of these two *vastus,* there is the ornament of meaning called *vibhāvanā,* which is the arising of a result without a cause. The understanding of such *vastus* and so on comes abaout by searching for such hidden meanings. Therefore these suggestions are *asaṁlakṣya-krama.*

Viśvanātha says *śleṣa* and *kāku* are aspects of *vyañjanā vṛtti.*

\[81\] Viśvanātha writes: *janān ardayati śleṣeṇa jana-pīḍakaḥ:* “The etymology of the word *janārdana* is *janān ardayati.* By double meaning, the name Janārdana can signify: “He who gives pain to people.” \[Th e original meaning of Janārdana is: “He who vanquishes the demons” (Puranic encyclopedia (from *Mahābhārata*)).\]

Viśvanātha says the words *bhramara* (“a bee”) and *varvara* (“a barbarian”) are implied here. According to the *ācārya,* a cowherd is a *varvara: tvaṁ varvaro ’pi, yo hi gopālakaḥ, sa hi varvaro bhavatīti prasiddheḥ.*

Viśvanātha writes: *idānīṁ mānasya dārḍhyaṁ rūpaṁ nijam abhimatam īhyatāṁ kriyatām ity arthaḥ.*

Viśvanātha: Will the sound of the *muralī* not occur? Certainly it will. At that time, being agitated, and giving up your indignation, You will run to Him. Then we shall see Your excitement.

There is a total of six *anuprāsas.* See text 7.29 for the details.

The words *cheka* means “expert.” *Chekānuprāsa* is so called because it is used by experts (see *Sāhitya-darpaṇa* 10.4).

According to *Amara-koṣa* (3.3.124), *dhāma* can mean “body,” *gṛha-deha-tviṭ-prabhāvā dhāmāni:* “*Dhāma* means house, body, splendor, and power.” The usual meaning of “abode” is found in *Viśva-prakāśa, dhāma dehe gṛhe raśmau sthāne janma-prabhāvayoḥ:* “*Dhāma* means body, house, a ray, place, birth, and power.” In *Subodhinī,* Śrīdhara Svāmī gives the word *dhāma* the meaning of *svarūpa* (see 8.21 and 15.6). Rūpa Gosvāmī uses the term *dhāma* in that sense also (*Laghu-bhāgavatāmṝta* 1.5.32 and 1.5.245). But this explanation is refuted by Jīva Gosvāmī, *dhāma-śabdena prabhāva ucyate prakāśo vā. gṛha-deha-tviṭ-prabhāvā dhāmāni ity-amarādi-nānārtha-vargāt. na tu svarūpam:* “The word *dhāma* means “power” and “splendor.” *Amara-koṣa* says: *gṛha-deha-tviṭ-prabhāvā dhāmāni.* This definition is confirmed by *Nānārtha-varga* and other dictionaries. But *dhāma* does not mean *svarūpa*” (*Paramātma-sandarbha* 105.35).

Viśvanātha writes: *sarveṣāṁ dṛśoḥ prema premāspadam ity arthaḥ.* Next he says *yaśo-mayam* means *yaśaḥ-svarūpam.*

This is according to Viśvanātha Cakravartī’s commentary.

Viśvanātha writes: *kāma-kaṇḍūyāyāṁ viśiṣṭa-bāhu-maṇḍalasya caṇḍimā yasya.*

*A glossary of Indian Figures of Speech* says that *lāṭānuprāsa* is characterized by an absence of conjuncts and a predominence of semivowels and nasals.

Viśvanātha writes: *mūrchā-rūpa-sakhyāḥ.*

This sentence is taken from the following *vṛtti.*

Viśvanātha says: *madhu-rākāyāṁ vasanta-kālīna-pūrṇimāyām.*

According to Monier-Williams Sanskrit-English Dictionary, a *yamaka* is a paronomasia, which is a fancy word for a pun. Webster’s English Dictionary’s definition of paronomasia is very close to the definition of *yamaka* stated in this *vṛtti.*

Viśvanātha: *yad vā, namati namre, anamat su-dṛḍhaṁ tulyam eka-rasaṁ kāruṇyaṁ yasya saḥ.*

Viśvanātha says *nandaya nandayeti vīpsā.* In this regard, *Kāśikā* (8.1.4) says, *vyāpti-viśeṣa-viṣayā prayoktur icchā vīpsā:* “*Vīpsā* is the agent’s desire for a particular kind of pervasion.”

The word *vā* is used here in the sense of *vitarka* (“conjecture”). *Medinī-kośa* (*svara-varga* 74) says: *vā syād vikalpopameyor vitarke pāda-pūraṇe samuccaye ca visrambhe nānārthātītayor api.*

According to HNV 1003, since the word *kāntā* is a *priyādi,* the form should be *nava-payodharā-kānta,* following Viśvanātha’s gloss. Pāṇini as well lists *kāntā* as a *priyādi.*
