According to Viśvanātha, the *ha* in the *kṛt-samāsa kala-ha* can refer to two *dhātus: \[o\]hā\[ṅ\] gatau* (3A), (“It obtains sweet sounds”), or *han* (2P), (i.e “It strikes sweet sounds”).

Viśvanātha glosses *kala-haṁsakāḥ* as follows: *kaṁ sukhaṁ lāntīti tathā-bhūtā haṁsakāḥ.* Thus *ka-la* is interpreted as a *kṛt-samāsa* that is formed by applying *\[k\]a* after the *dhātu lā dāne* (2P, “to give”).

The word *araṇa* is made by applying the *kṛt* suffix *ana* after the *dhātu ṛ\[a\] gatau.*

The *dhātu* used here in *amadana* is *mad\[ī\] harṣe* (4P, “to feel joy”).

Varuṇa controls water (*ka*); he is its presiding deity. The word *keśi* is in *saptamī.* The *dhātu īś\[a\] aiśvarye* is applied after *ka,* then *\[k\]vi\[p\]* is added and deleted.

Viśvanātha: *kaṁ sukham amituṁ prāptuṁ śīlaṁ yasya tena,* “*Kāminā* is in *tṛtīyā. Kāmin* means “He whose nature is to obtain happiness.””

This is the *bhūteśa karmaṇi* 1.1 form of *am\[a\] gatau.*

Viśvanātha: *amā no nā pratiṣedhe,* “*Amā, no,* and *nā* are used as a particle expressing negation.” The reference is unknown.

*Amara-koṣa* define *āsecanaka* as follows: *tad āsecanakaṁ tṛpter nāstyanto yasya darśanāt* (“There is no end to the satisfaction one gets by seeing it”).

Viśvanātha quotes Pāṇini, *vartamāna-sāmīpye vartamānavad vā:* “The present tense may be used to indicate near past or near future” (*Aṣṭādhyāyī* 3.3.131). This corresponds to HNV 709.

Viśvanātha says *mā* means *śobhā* (“brilliance”). I.e *mā* signifies Lakṣmī, otherwise known as *śrī,* and *śrī* is a well-known synonym of *śobhā* (“brilliance”).

*Preya* is a *viṣṇukṛtya* of the *dhātu prā pūraṇe,* whereas *preyasī* is made from the *dhātu prī\[ṅ\] prītau .* The *pratyaya ka* is added (HNV 825), then *priya* changes to *pra,* and finally *īyas\[u\]* is applied (HNV 615).

Viśvanātha explains that the first two lines of this verse refer to *uddīpana-vibhāva* (stimuli)*.* The other two refer to *ālambana-vibhāva* (i.e the *viṣaya* and *āśraya* of a relationship).

Viśvanātha explains that the word *śubhaṁyu* is formed from the *avyaya śubham* which expresses *praśaṁsā* (fame, glory).

*Aruṣā* is made from the word *arus* (“a wound”), which Viśvanātha glosses as *manaḥ-pīḍā,* while the next word *ruṣā* is made from *ruṣ.*

The word *sasa* is made from *ṣas\[ū\] svapne* and *pac-āder a\[t\].*

Viśvanātha says that the name of the place is not mentioned here, because the meeting place should be kept a secret.

Viśvanātha glosses *sadāmodare* as follows: *sadā modaṁ rāti dadātīti tasmin* (“towards He who always gives pleasure”).

*Svara* means the threeVedic accents: *svarita, udātta,* and *anudātta.*

According to Viśvanātha, in that clause the *samāpikā kriyā* (“main verb”) is *syāt: rātrir manas tudantī syād iti.*

Viśvanātha: *vidhattām* means *kurutām.* It is either *vidhātā parapada* 1.2 or *vidhātā ātmapada* 1.1.

This is a *vidhātā* 2.1 of the *dhātu dal\[a\] viśaraṇe,* in *prākṛta.* It is connected with the word *mā.*

*Viśva-prakāśa* says, *citraṁ syād adbhutālekhya-tilakeṣu vihāyasi:* “*Citra* means *adbhūta* (“amazing”), *ālekhya* (“picture”), *tilaka* (“design on the forehead”), and *vihāyas* (“sky”).”

*koṣa, citra* means *adbhuta* (“amazing”), and *ālekhya* (“picture”). Bhārata Muni has stated, *tac citraṁ yatra varṇānāṁ khaḍgādy-ākṛti-hetutā:* “*Citra* is that in which the *varṇas,* situated in a particular arrangement, reveal the forms of a sword and so on.”

Viśvanātha: “*Vedha* is made from the *dhātu vidh\[a\]* in its sense of *vidhāne,* and *\[gh\]a\[ṇ\].*” The *ācārya* glosses *vedha* as *vidhāna,* then as *kriyā.*

Viśvanātha: “*Sva-sada* means *sva-gata. Sada* is made from the *dhātu ṣad\[ḷ\] gatau.*” Then *pac-āder a\[t\]* is added.

When this word is read backwards, the *hariveṇu* in *ayantī* becomes a *viṣṇucakra: sā + ayantī* becomes *sāyaṁtī.* Therefore we didn’t transliterate the word as *sāyantī* in the verse.

In this verse, every line reads the same backwards, as well as upwards and downwards when the syllables are written in a diagram of squares. The next two verses are other examples of *sarvato-bhadram* (“good on every side”). The reason this particular verse is called “*mahā-sarvato-bhadram*” is that there are two extra *viṣṇusargas,* and one extra *t.* If this verse were written in a diagram of squares, these extra *varṇas* wouldn’t be considered while reading an opposite way, or else they would appear outside the diagram. Thus the word *mahā* (“big”) is used.

*Sādhārā* is a *kṛt-samāsa* using the *dhātu ṛ gatau.*

*Viśva-prakāśa, a syād abhāve sv-alpārthe viṣṇāv īśe tv anavyayam:* “The letter *a* is used in the sense of absence, and littleness. But when it refers to Viṣṇu or Śiva it is not an *avyaya.*”

*Viśva-prakāśa, mā niṣedhe ’vyayaṁ matam:* “*Mā* used in the sense of negation is considered an *avyaya.*”

*Viśva-prakāśa, i khede ca ruṣoktau ca kāmadeve tv anavyayam:* “The letter *i* is used to express affliction, anger, and in the sense of calling; but when it refers to Cupid it is not an *avyaya.*”

*Amara-koṣa* (1.3.11): *dhārā-sampāta āsāraḥ.*

*Bṛhad-dhātu-kusumākara* gives the *dhātu las\[a\] krīḍāyām* the additional meaning of *śleṣaṇa.*

Viśvanātha glosses *asya* as *gamya.* Thus *asya* is made from the *dhātu as\[a\] gatau.* The same *dhātu as\[a\]* is used in its sense of *dīpti* in the word *kālāsā* in the third line of this verse. Moreover, the word *rasi* stands for *rasin* (“who has *rasa*”).

*Kāsā* is a *kṛt-samāsa* made from *as\[a\] gatau. Kāsā* stands for *ka-āsā,* meaning “She has gone to Her head” i.e “She is in *māna*”). *Amara-koṣa* (3.3.5) says, *kaṁ śiro ’mbunoḥ:* “In the neuter gender *ka* means head, and water.”

Viśvanātha quotes *Medinī-kośa, raso gandha-rase jale śṛṅgārādau viṣevīrye tiktādau drava-rāgayoḥ:* “*Rasa* means an aromatic juice; water; the five moods beginning with *madhura-rasa;* poison; semen; the six tastes beginning with bitter; liquid; and feeling.”

Viśvanātha writes, *sudhā-sindhu-dhārā nu bhoḥ.* In this connection, *Viśva-prakāśa* says, *nu syāt praśne vikalpārthe ’py atītānunayārthayoḥ:* “*Nu* is used in the sense of asking a question; a possibility; excessive; and for the sake of courtesy or reverence.” Thus *nu* is part of the *sambodhana* (“addressing”).

Viśvanātha glosses *sāra* as *sthira. Sāra* can mean “the fixed part,” according to *Amara-koṣa* (3.3.171), *sāro bale sthirāṁśe ca nyāyye klībaṁ vare triṣu:* “In the masculine gender, *sāra* means stength, and the solid or fixed part; in the neuter gender it means fit, proper; in any gender it can mean best.”

An *akṣara* is a *varṇa* (“phoneme”) (HNV *Vṛtti* 1). In the context of this chapter however, *akṣara* means “consonant.” This is explained by Viśvanātha Kavirāja in *Sāhitya-darpaṇa, anuprāsaḥ śabda-sāmyaṁ vaiṣamye ’pi svarasya yat \[…\] svara-mātra-sadṛśyaṁ tu vaicitryābhāvān na gaṇitam:* “Alliteration is a sameness of sound. Even though there may be a sameness of vowels, it isn’t counted as an alliteration because it doesn’t produce any astonishment.”

*Go-mūtrikā* literally means “similar to the course of cow’s urine.” This signifies zigzagging.

Jīva Gosvāmī explains that *mā* can mean knowledge, *mā-dhavaḥ sarva-vidyā-patir iti:* “The name Mādhava signifies that he is the master of all knowledge” (*Krama-sandarbha* 10.16.16). In regard to the *citra-kāvya* diagram, the third line reads the same as the second line in reverse, except that *va-kāra* in *mādhava* changes to *ba-kāra* (in *mā-badha*). This type of occurence is common. *Sāhitya-darpaṇa* says, *yamakādau bhaved aikyaṁ ḍa-lor ba-vor la-ros tathā:* “In regard to *yamakas* and so on, *ḍ* and *l* are considered the same, as are *b* and *v,* as well as *l* and *r.*” Jīva Gosvāmī echoes this in HNV *Bṛhat vṛtti* 128.

*Badha* is made from the *dhātu badh\[a\],* in its sense of *nindā,* and *pac-āder a\[t\].*

Viśvanātha points out that both words *dheya* in the verse are made from the *dhātu \[ḍu\]dhā\[ñ\];* the first *dheya* is in the sense of *dhāraṇa,* the second one is in the sense of *poṣaṇa.*

Here *gadā* just refers to a fancy stick, since this verse is *pratilomānuloma,* that is to say it can be read backwards. In that event, some rules are bent in *citra-kāvya:* i) the *viṣṇucakra* on *araṁ,* in the fourth *pāda,* becomes *ma-kāra,* ii) *na-kāra* in *bandha* changes to *ma-kāra,* and iii) *ba-kāra* in the word *bandha* changes to *va-kāra.* In regard to the second modification, it is merely a *hariveṇu* that changes *varga.* Examples of the last case are often seen.

There is no diagram for this verse, yet Viśvanātha says *garbhākṣara* is a type of *citra-kāvya.* As is sometimes seen, the name of the meter is hidden in the verse. Here it is just a resemblance (*āraya*). For more information on the *āryā* meter, consult Appendix 1 of the *Nāma-prakaraṇa, Hari-nāmāmṛta-vyākaraṇa.*

*Nā* is the 1.1 masculine form of *nṛ* (“a man”).

The form *arājate* is a *samāsa.* An *ākhyāta* can be compounded with *na\[ñ\]* only when the sense is reproach (HNV 778).

*Medinī-koṣa* says, *rāsaḥ kolāhale dhvāne bhāṣā-śṛṅkhalake ’pi ca, krīḍā-bhede ca gopānāṁ vatsaḥ-putrādi-varṣayoḥ:* “The word *rāsa* means an uproar; sounding; speech; sound of a chain; a particular pastime of the *gopas;* semen of a bull, and; semen of a man.” In this verse *rāsa* means *dhvāna* (“sounds”). The *avyaya ā\[ṅ\]* is used in the sense of *samyak* (“completely, perfectly”). *Ārāsa* is made from *ā\[ṅ\], ras\[a\] śabde,* and *\[gh\]a\[ṇ\].* In the innovative *sa-sārasā* verse ( 7.66), the similar form *ārasa,* which is made with *pac-āder a\[t\]* instead of *\[gh\]a\[ṇ\],* is used; there Viśvanātha glossed *ārasa* as: *ā samyak rasati śabdāyate.*

Each verse from 7.94 to 7.100 corresponds to the bow diagram. This is confirmed by Viśvanātha in his commentary on 7.100.

*Kāraṇāyita* is made from *kāraṇa, \[k\[ya\[ṅ\],* and *\[k\]ta.*

An apostrophe has been inserted at the beginning of the second line to show that the word is *āmoda.* The use of the single and the double *avagraha* (“apostrophe”) between two vowels “*a*” is an optional rule very often seen in Devanāgarī script, but it isn’t a BBT standard. Here the *avagraha* should be double because it stands for *ā,* i.e the compound is *vidyā-āmoda.*

The word *kathana* is *laghu* for metrical reasons. It should be *kathanaṁ.* The meter is *srag-dharā*.

Viśvanātha says, *vidyāyā āmodaḥ saurabhyaṁ jagad-vyāpi yaśa ity arthaḥ:* “The word *vidyāmoda* signifies “fame,” that is to say “the all-pervading fragrance of knowledge.””

The change of *m* to a *viṣṇucakra* that would normally take place by *mo viṣṇucakraṁ viṣṇujane* (HNV 72) doesn’t take place in *samrāj* because of the Pāṇinian *sūtra, mo rāji samaḥ kvau* (*Aṣṭādhyāyī* 8.3.25).

*Amara-koṣa* (1.10.42) says, *mṛṇālaṁ visam:* “*Visa* means *mṛṇāla* (“lotus stem”).” *Visa* is listed as *bisa* in Monier-Williams’ as well as in Apte’s Sanskrit-English Dictionary.

The word *akṣara* means “consonant” in this context. See the footnote to the *vṛtti* of 7.87 for an explanation. Additionally, verses 101 and 102 are considered to be *citra-kāvya* although there is no diagram assigned to them. Here the meaning of *citra-kāvya* is simply “astonishing poetry.” Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī has also composed similar verses, some corresponding to diagrams, in the *citra-kavitvani* section of *Stavamālā.*

Viśvanātha gives two interpretations of *śaṁ-śīḥ.* In both cases it’s in apposition with *śaśī. Śam* is an *avyaya.* First, *śaṁ-śīḥ* is a *yasya saḥ bahuvrīhi,* where *śīḥ* is originally a feminine word meaning *hiṁsā* (“harm”) made from the *dhātu śṝ hiṁsāyām* and *\[k\]vi\[p\]* in *bhāve prayoga* (HNV 902, 439, and 192). Second, *śaṁ-śīḥ* is a *kṛt-samāsa* made from *śī\[ṅ\] svapne* and *\[k\]vi\[p\]* in *kartari prayoga* (HNV 850). In this case the meaning is: “\[The moon\] sleeps happily” i.e “It’s not awake to others’ happiness.”

*Apapāpa-pa* is a *yasmāt-saḥ-bahuvrīhi-garbhita-kṛt-samāsa* (“a *kṛt-samāsa* containing a *bahuvrīhi* called *yasmāt saḥ*”). The *dhātu* is *pai śoṣaṇe.*

*Papiḥ* is a variation of the *uṇādi-sūtra* 3.159, which ordains a reduplication of the *dhātu pā pāne.* The moon “drinks \[all luminosity\].” Viśvanātha glosses *papiḥ* as follows, *papiḥ āśām asman-manorathaṁ pibatīti saḥ:* “The moon drinks our (i.e the *gopīs’*) innermost desire.” Thus *āśām* has a double meaning in the verse. Although the word *papis* is a *kṛdanta,* its object doesn’t take a *ṣaṣṭhī viṣṇubhakti* (*Kāśikā* 2.3.69). *Papis* is declined like *havis.*

*Amara-koṣa* (3.3.205) says, *lolaś cala-satṛṣṇayoḥ:* “*Lola*means “one who goes” or “one who thirsts for.”” Viśvanātha says, *lolo yuvati-satṛṣṇaḥ kṛṣṇaḥ* (“*Lola* signifies Kṛṣṇa who thirsts for young girls”). The word *lola* is formed by applying *pac-āder a\[t\]* after the *dhātu luḍ\[a\] viloḍane,* considering that *l* and *ḍ* are interchangeable (HNV *Bṛhat vṛtti* 128). This *dhātu* isn’t listed in *Bṛhad-dhātu-kusumākara,* but *Mādhavīyā dhātu-vṛtti* and Monier-Williams list *lul\[a\] viloḍane.* Apte’s Sanskrit-English Dictionary lists the same *dhātu* as *luḍ\[a\].*

Here the *dhātu* is *lī\[ṅ\] śleṣaṇe.* By double meaning, *līlālīm* can mean *līlā-ālīm:* “\[Kṛṣṇa desired\] a multitude of pastimes (i.e many pastimes).”

Viśvanātha supplies the object *samprayogam* to the verb *yayau.*

*Amara-koṣa* (3.3.111) says, *inaḥ sūrye prabhau:* “*Ina* means sun, or master.”

The word *nuḥ* is the 6.1 form of *nṛ,* just as *nā* in the first line is the 1.1 form. Viśvanātha writes: *nur jīvasya jaḍasyāpy ananaṁ jīvanam:* “*Ananam* means *jīvanam,* in the sense of “the life of living entitities (*nuḥ*) and \[the existence\] of inert things as well.””

Viśvanātha says the first word *nut* stands for *nuta* (by poetic license), and means *stuta.* The second word *nut* is made from the *dhātu ṇud\[a\] preraṇe.* The *ācārya* also mentions that *nun-nut* can also signify that the Lord “praises the act of praising,” i.e “He encourages devotion.” In this case, the second word *nut* is made from the *dhātu ṇu stutau, \[k\]vi\[p\]* is added, then *t\[uk\]* is applied (HNV 777). The word *anu* in the verse is a *kṛṣṇa-pravacanīya* and means *lakṣī-kṛtya,* “having made the target” (HNV 672).

According to Viśvanātha, *dhāma* refers to *tejaḥ* in the previous verse. *Amara-koṣa* (3.3.124) says, *gṛha-deha-tviṭ-prabhāvā dhāmāni:* “*Dhāma* means house, body, splendor, and power.”

Viśvanātha: The word *sura-cchavi,* which refers to the effulgence of the sun god and so on, is used in reference to these three verses among others: i) *na tad bhāsayate sūryo na śaśāṅko na pāvakaḥ, yad gatvā na nivartante tad dhāma paramaṁ mama* (*Bhagavad-gītā* 15.6), ii) *yad āditya-gataṁ tejo jagad bhāsayate ’khilam, yac candram asi yac cāgnau tat tejo viddhi māmakam* (*Bhagavad-gītā* 15.12), and iii) *na tatra sūryo bhāti na candra-tārakaṁ nemā vidyuto bhānti kuto ’yam agniḥ, tam eva bhāntam anu bhāti sarvaṁ tasya bhāsā sarvam idaṁ vibhāti:* “The sun does not shine there, neither do the moon and the stars. Lightning doesn’t exist there, so how could fire be present? All these shine because it shines. Its effulgence illuminates everything” (*Kaṭha Upaniṣad* 2.2.15). In these verses, the word *dhāma* means either “abode” in reference to Goloka, or “effulgence” in regard to Brahman, which is Kṛṣṇa’s splendor (*Hari-vaṁśa* 2.114.9). In the verse of *Kaṭha Upaniṣad,* the word *anu* is a *kṛṣṇa-pravacanīya* used in the sense of *hetu* (“cause”) (HNV 672). This is in accordance with Viśvanātha, who says that the compound *sura-cchavi* is a *yasmāt saḥ bahuvrīhi.*

Viśvanātha: In this verse, the following words are synonyms, but since they are used in a dissimilar sense, there is *punar-uktavad-ābhāsa* (“a semblance of a repetition”): i) *tanu* and *śarīra,* and ii) *kāñcana* and *kanaka.* The *ācārya* explains that the compound *mukhavad-anaṅka* is a *punar-uktavad-ābhāsa* as well because *mukha* and *vadana* are synonyms, even though the actual word *vadana* isn’t used.

Viśvanātha explains the derivation of *vidhu: sarveṣāṁ saṁsāra-duḥkhaṁ vidhunoti haratīti:* “*Vidhu* is one who takes away everyone’s unhappiness.” Additionally, in this verse the following words are *punar-uktavad-ābhāsa* (“a semblance of a repetition”): *ghana* and *jalada; rucira* and *sundara; dhāma* and *mahas; sañcaya* and *ogha; vidhu, amṛta-kara,* and *kalā-nidhi;* as well as *nabha, puṣkara,* and *ākāśa.*

Here *ākāśa* means *ā + kāśa.* Moreover, Viśvanātha comments as follows: *sarovare yathā śveta-kamalaṁ bhāsate, tathaiva nabho-rūpa-sarovare candro ’pīty arthaḥ:* “As a white lotus on a lake shines, so the moon shines on a calm lake.”

HNV *Bṛhat* 139 says, *sātvata-paratve lopyaś ca:* The *viṣṇusarga* can be deleted when a *śauri* which is followed by a *sātvata* follows. By this rule, since the word *sthāne* in the verse begins with a *śauri* and a *sātvata, kim asthāne* can mean *kima sthāne,* which stands for *kimaḥ sthāne.*

HNV 1017 explains that the word *ku* is replaced by *kā* when the sense is *īṣat.* HNV 931 explains that *ku* means *kutsita.*

In this context, the *dhvani* in *strī tvaṁ bhavati* is, “You need to become a woman in order to understand who she really is!” According to the verse, yet another interpretation is possible; Kṛṣṇa Himself may have said, *iyaṁ kā-strī strī tvaṁ bhavati:* “Who is this little girl \[that sent you\]? *You* are a woman \[fit for enjoying pastimes of love\]!

Śrī Kṛṣṇa may have used *kāku* in two ways. Firstly, *na kākor udbhūtaṁ bhavati* can mean, “Has it not arisen from *kāku*?” Secondly, Kṛṣṇa may have meant, *na kā strī kor udbhūtaṁ bhavati:* “Has a particular lady not manifested from the earth?” The answer is: She is Sītā-devī, who is not different than Śrī Rādhā Ṭhākurāṇī. *Kāku* is highly suggestive (*vyañjanā vṛtti*).
